Measured with $N = 80$ and $m = 4$, on both evaluators: the LC branch passes period 100 after 1606 points, at $\beta_1 = -0.0023714546120214$, which is $3.4 \cdot 10^{-14}$ from the shooting value. The HOM run from that point at $T = 100.006$ covers $\beta_2 \in [-0.1, -0.05]$ in 11 points (step at most 0.01), with $\beta_1$ within $7.4 \cdot 10^{-15}$ of the shooting value at every point and no label. The test bounds that error by $10^{-12}$.
5. **Unit tests.** Every test of the table on hand-made spectra, including the sign changes and the NaN cases; the sign-function bases on matrices with a Jordan block and with a complex pair, checking that $A^\top L_s$ stays in the span of $L_s$ and that $L_s^\top$ is zero on the unstable subspace of $A$; the rotation ($C^\top L = C^\top C$); the non-periodic equidistribution; and a random test of the finite-difference columns of the boundary rows against central differences. pc-engine has no `proptest` dependency, so this test draws 16 cases (parameters of Sandstede's model and points within 0.01 of the start) from a fixed-seed xorshift generator and checks the whole dense Jacobian against central differences. Measured: worst scaled entry error $7.3 \cdot 10^{-8}$, bound $10^{-6}$.
6. **Interface.** The row width, `cycle_stride` and the label list agree in the Rust unit tests, the FFI tests and the CLI tests, and in `Rows2BranchTest.m` and `HomCurveTest.m` on the MATLAB side.
7. **Regression.** The existing `cargo test` suite stays green.

## 8b-2. Adjoint problem and inclination flips (phase E8b, internal slice)

The first slice of E8b adds the adjoint variational problem along a HOM orbit and the two inclination-flip test functions that use it. It adds internal functions and unit tests only. It adds no label, no row field, no stride change, no JSON key, no C function and no MATLAB signature, so a HOM run writes the rows of Section 8b. Section 8b-3 wires the tests into a run.

**Sources.** AUTO-07p `src/homcont.f90` at commit 44cc1c42a888179bdbd608a40613caee9cbc4675 (BSD-3, ported, with an entry in `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md`); the lecture slides cited in the research notes as "Champneys, MRI Master Class, Utrecht" (page numbers are those of the PDF); the CWI report AM-R9516 (HomCont manual), page 8; and AUTO's `auto.tex` for PAR(10) and PAR(11). Champneys, Kuznetsov and Sandstede (1996) has not been read. A formula below is marked confirmed when the code and at least one of the other three sources give it. Everything else is marked single source or deferred, and the code does not use it.

**Time convention (confirmed).** AUTO's PAR(11) is the length of the truncated interval, with $t \in [-\text{PAR}(11)/2, \text{PAR}(11)/2]$ (manual p. 8; slide 4, $2T = \text{par}(11)$). The $T$ of Section 8b is PAR(11), so every flip exponent below has $T/2$.

**Adjoint problem (confirmed).** The unknowns are $\varphi(\tau) \in \mathbb{R}^n$ on the mesh and a scalar $\gamma$ (AUTO's PAR(10), with ITWIST = 1). The equation is
$$\varphi' = T\big(-f_x(u, p)^\top \varphi + \gamma f(u, p)\big)$$
(`homcont.f90` FNHO 175-178, FFHO 252-256, INHO 886-889; slides 33 and 34; `auto.tex` 5879-5887). The boundary rows use bases of $A = f_x(x_0, p)$ itself, where the rows of the orbit problem use bases of $A^\top$: $B_u(A)^\top \varphi(0) = 0$ ($n_u$ rows) and $B_s(A)^\top \varphi(1) = 0$ ($n_s$ rows), with the columns of $B_u(A)$ and $B_s(A)$ spanning the unstable and stable invariant subspaces of $A$ (FBHO 503-523, the two PRJCTN calls with ITRANS = 2; slide 34, $P_u \varphi(-T) = 0$ and $P_s \varphi(T) = 0$). So $\varphi(0)$ lies in the stable subspace of $A^\top$ and $\varphi(1)$ in its unstable subspace. The scale of $\varphi$ is free, so AUTO adds the integral condition $\int \tilde\varphi^\top(\varphi - \tilde\varphi)\, dt = 0$ against the previous point (slide 34; ICHO writes it as a sum over the adjoint entries, and the quadrature weights were not read). In theory $\gamma = 0$, because $\frac{d}{dt}\langle \varphi, f\rangle = \gamma \|f\|^2$ and both ends of the bounded solution are orthogonal to $f$. On the truncated interval $\gamma$ is not zero, but it is of the order of the squared distance of the ends from $x_0$.

**Inclination-flip tests (confirmed).** In the notation of Section 8b,
$$\mathrm{IFS} = \langle v_S, \varphi(0)\rangle\, e^{-T\lambda_S/2},\qquad \mathrm{IFU} = \langle v_{S+1}, \varphi(1)\rangle\, e^{+T\lambda_{S+1}/2},$$
where $v_j$ is the right eigenvector of $A$ for $\lambda_j$ (HomCont tests 13 and 14, PSIHO 1941-1955, with V from EIGHO and ITRANS = 2; slide 35). Each is linear in $\varphi$. IFS is defined when $\lambda_S$ is real and IFU when $\lambda_{S+1}$ is real. AUTO has no guard here: EIGHO 2069-2077 copies the real part into the second column of a complex pair. The guard is the rule of Section 8b for OFS and OFU, and slide 30 assumes real leading eigenvalues.

**First point (design A only).** PREHO starts the adjoint with $\gamma = 0$ and the value 0.1 at every mesh point, and the adjoint phase row of the first Newton step uses that guess as $\tilde\varphi$. The post hoc solve of design B does not need a guess.

**Single source or deferred, not used by the code.**

- The orientation index. AUTO's PSIHO with IS = 0 computes $-S_1 S_2 / \sqrt{\|f(u(0))\|^2 \|f(u(1))\|^2 \|\varphi(0)\|^2 \|\varphi(1)\|^2}$ with $S_1 = \langle f(u(1)), \varphi(0)\rangle$ and $S_2 = \langle f(u(0)), \varphi(1)\rangle$, and 0 when the denominator is 0 (PSIHO 1816-1845, FNCSHO 1757-1771). AUTO only prints it, as "Non-orientable" when it is negative, and only at print level 3 or more. Both research reports read this one piece of code. The definition and sign convention of CKS 1996 are unconfirmed.
- The sign rule for right eigenvectors (EIGHO 2104-2126): the largest component of each column is made positive on the first call, and a later call flips a column whose dot product with the same column of the previous call is negative. The state is kept per ITRANS, not per equilibrium. Section 8b already uses this rule for left eigenvectors. For right eigenvectors it rests on the code alone.
- The ascending eigenvalue order that puts $\lambda_S$ at index $n_s$. The headers of EIGHO ("largest first") and PSIHO ("smallest first") disagree with each other, and the sort at 2082-2102 is ascending. Section 8b uses the ascending order.
- HomCont tests 15 and 16 (non-central saddle-node homoclinic orbits). They belong to the IEQUIB = 2 problem, in which the saddle-node point $x_1$ is an unknown and the eigenvalue at index $n_s + 1$ is held at zero (FBHO 484-501), so they are not tests of the saddle homoclinic problem of Section 8b. PSIHO 1957-1967 gives $\sum_i (x_{1,i} - u_i(1)) v_{S+1,i}$ and the same with $u(0)$. Slide 35 gives $\psi_{15} = \frac{1}{T}\langle w_1, x(T) - x_0\rangle$ for a different formulation, and the two forms have not been reconciled. Deferred to the IEQUIB = 2 phase.
- Heteroclinic orbits, Lin's method and a free $T$. The code paths were read (FBHO 400-422, 525-545, FNHO 98-208) and none was run. Deferred.

**Checks done for this section (ours, not from a source).**

1. *Closed-form adjoint at the parameters of Section 8b, test 1* ($a = \tilde\mu = \alpha = \gamma = \mu = 0$, $b = 1$, $c = -2$). The $(x, y)$ part is divergence free, so $\varphi = (\dot y, -\dot x) = (x - \tfrac32 x^2,\ -y)$ with $\varphi_z = 0$ and $\gamma = 0$ solves the adjoint equation. A numpy check on $T = 40$ with 4000 intervals gives an ODE residual of $2.7 \cdot 10^{-5}$, which is the central-difference error of the sampled functions, a boundary direction $(1, -1)$ at $\tau = 0$ and $(1, 1)$ at $\tau = 1$ (orthogonal to the unstable eigenvector $(1, 1, 0)$ and to the stable space spanned by $(1, -1, 0)$ and $e_z$), and a relative misalignment of $5.8 \cdot 10^{-9}$, which is of the order $e^{-T/2}$. This is the oracle for the residual map. For the flip tests it gives only a limit: the orbit ends are $x(\pm T/2) \approx 4 e^{-T/2}$, so $\varphi(0) \approx x\,(1, -1, 0)$ and $\varphi(1) \approx x\,(1, 1, 0)$, and $|\mathrm{IFS}|$ and $|\mathrm{IFU}|$ tend to $4\sqrt{2}$ as $T$ grows. This is a generic point of the curve, not a flip.
2. *IFS in the models of Section 8b, tests 2 and 3, is at the noise floor.* The $(x, y)$ equations do not depend on $z$, so $f_x$ is block lower triangular and $\varphi_z' = -T C^\top \varphi_z + T\gamma f_z$ with $C$ the $z$ block. The right eigenvectors of $\lambda_S$ lie in the $z$ block, and the stable subspace of $A$ contains the whole $z$ block, so the boundary row at $\tau = 1$ forces $\varphi_z(1) = 0$. With $\gamma = 0$ the equation then gives $\varphi_z \equiv 0$, and IFS is zero for every parameter value on the curve. On the truncated interval $\gamma$ is of the order $e^{-T}$ and so is IFS. A numpy check of test 2 ($(a, b, c) = (-0.5, 2.5, -1)$, trapezoid rule, $\gamma$ free) gives $|\varphi_3| \le 3 \cdot 10^{-15}$ and $|\mathrm{IFS}| \le 3 \cdot 10^{-12}$ at $\mu = 0$ for $T = 10$ and 16, and at $\mu = 0.05$, $T = 10$ an IFS divided by $\max|\varphi|$ of $-1.3 \cdot 10^{-4}$, $3.3 \cdot 10^{-5}$ and $-8.1 \cdot 10^{-6}$ for $h = 1/30$, $1/60$ and $1/120$. That value falls like $h^2$ and changes sign, so it is discretization noise around zero. Test 3 follows from the same argument and was not run. A locator that looks for sign changes of IFS on these curves would report labels that mean nothing. Whether to add a guard, and what it should be, is an owner decision.

**What this slice implements.**

- Bases of $A$ for the adjoint boundary rows, in `pc-linalg`, with a Beyn rotation state for each kind of boundary row: ITRANS = 1 for the orbit rows (bases of $A^\top$) and ITRANS = 2 for the adjoint rows (bases of $A$), each holding one stable and one unstable reference basis. AUTO's PRJCTN keys CPREV by (IS, ITRANS), and a homoclinic orbit has one equilibrium, so only the kind separates the slots (CPREV and IFLAG, 2188 and 2254-2287). The existing bases stay as they are.
- A right-eigenvector helper with the sign rule above, in `hom.rs`.
- IFS and IFU computed from a given $\varphi$.
- A linear map from $(\varphi, \gamma)$ to the adjoint residual and boundary rows, used by tests only.

**Acceptance (this slice).** Unit tests in `pc-linalg` and `pc-engine`, no MATLAB run: (1) the $A$-bases satisfy $A B \subset \operatorname{span} B$ and $B_u^\top$ is zero on the stable subspace of $A$ and the other way round, on matrices with a Jordan block and with a complex pair; (2) the two rotation slots (bases of $A^\top$ and of $A$) keep separate state, each with its own stable and unstable reference; (3) the right-eigenvector helper returns the eigenvector of the leading stable and unstable real eigenvalue with the sign rule, and no value for a complex leading pair; (4) IFS and IFU match hand-computed values on triangular $3 \times 3$ matrices; (5) the residual map applied to $\varphi = (\dot y, -\dot x)$ on the orbit of check 1 falls like the collocation error as $N$ grows, and the boundary rows are below $10^{-6}$ at $T = 20$; (6) the existing `cargo test` suite stays green.

## 8b-3. Inclination flips in a HOM run (phase E8b, wiring)

**Status (2026-11-08).** The solve for the adjoint (Design B, below) is built and tested (commit df47eb1, acceptance 1 and 2). The wiring (phase P32) follows this text: the guard of decision 2 is the bracket floor below, with dropping IFS and IFU as the fallback if the floor fails its validation (acceptance 6). Acceptance 6 ran on 2026-11-08 and the floor passed the hard bounds, so the fallback is not used. One signal bracket misses the 4-orders target by a small margin (acceptance 6, below).

This section wires the internal slice of Section 8b-2 into a HOM run. It adds the labels IFS and IFU to the codim-2 list of Section 8b, an opt-in JSON key and a linear solve for the adjoint at every point the tests are evaluated at. The owner delegated the open decisions of E8b on 2026-11-08 ("decisions make them as u recomended go ahead"), and the list below records each one under the number it has in the E8b list in `docs/STATUS.md`. The owner's answer on the guard was "do what u recomended build a -c" (option (a), the bracket floor, with option (c), dropping IFS and IFU, as the fallback). That wording is short, so the report to the owner says which reading was used.

**Decisions.**

1. The codim-2 list widens from 12 to 14 entries. IFS and IFU follow OFU, so the entries 1 to 12 keep their places. A HOM run has no release behind it (the first HOM commit, e5e9f93, is not an ancestor of `v1.0.0`, the only tag), so no stored session holds a HOM row and the stride change breaks nothing. The stride is the same with the key off: the two columns hold NaN.
2. IFS and IFU need a guard against the noise described in Section 8b-2, check 2. The cosine guard planned first fails its own test (Measurements). The guard is a floor on the larger end value of a bracket (Guard, below), with $\epsilon_b = 10^{-8}$. The value is our own choice. No reference gives one, and the validation of acceptance 6 is what supports it. If that validation fails, IFS and IFU are dropped from the label list instead (option (c)), and the list returns to the 12 entries of Section 8b.
3. Design B: the adjoint is a post hoc linear solve given the orbit and $x_0$ of an accepted point or trial point. Design A (the adjoint inside the Newton system) needs second derivatives of the field and a first-point guess, and is not built.
4. HomCont tests 15 and 16 and the IEQUIB = 2 problem stay deferred.
5. The orientation index is not exposed. Its sign convention is unconfirmed (Section 8b-2).
6. The formulas of Section 8b-2 stay as marked there. CKS 1996 has not been read, so nothing in this section is marked as confirmed against it. The code, the manual and the slides are the sources.
7. No AUTO-07p run. The oracle of the acceptance tests is our own numpy computation (below).
8. Heteroclinic orbits, Lin's method, a free $T$ and the homotopy start stay deferred.

**Solve for the adjoint.** At a point with orbit $u$ on the mesh, $T$, $x_0$ and $p$, the unknowns are $\varphi$ at the $n(Nm+1)$ mesh nodes and $\gamma$. The rows are the collocation rows of Section 8b-2 on the same mesh ($nNm$ rows, with $\gamma$ in a column of its own), the boundary rows of Section 8b-2 ($n_u$ rows $B_u(A)^\top\varphi(0)$ and $n_s$ rows $B_s(A)^\top\varphi(1)$, from the rotating bases of kind ITRANS = 2 of Section 8b-2) and one normalization row $\langle r, \varphi\rangle = 1$ over the stacked node values. The system without the last row has $n(Nm+1)$ rows and $n(Nm+1) + 1$ unknowns, so it has a null space, which is one-dimensional when the bounded adjoint is unique. The engine solves the square system with the condensation of `pc-linalg` (one extra unknown and one border row). The first $r$ is a fixed pseudo-random vector (xorshift, fixed seed), and the solve is repeated once with $r = z / \|z\|$ for the first solution $z$ (one step of inverse iteration), so the row is not nearly orthogonal to the null vector. The result is divided by its 2-norm, so $\|\varphi\| = 1$.

**Sign.** The scale of $\varphi$ is positive but its sign is free, and IFS and IFU change sign with it. The engine fixes it from the end vectors, which do not depend on the mesh. At the first point the component of largest magnitude of $(\varphi(0), \varphi(1))$ is positive. At every later accepted point $\varphi$ is flipped when $\langle\varphi(0), \varphi_{\text{prev}}(0)\rangle + \langle\varphi(1), \varphi_{\text{prev}}(1)\rangle < 0$, with $\varphi_{\text{prev}}$ the vector of the previous accepted point. The locator's trial points use the same reference, which `accept()` updates.

**Tests.** IFS and IFU are computed from $\varphi(0)$ and $\varphi(1)$ with the formulas of Section 8b-2, with $T$ the PAR(11) of Section 8b. IFS is NaN when $\lambda_S$ is complex and IFU is NaN when $\lambda_{S+1}$ is complex, as in Section 8b-2. Both are NaN when the key is off and when the solve fails (a singular system). The row holds the raw values. The guard below acts on the label, so a stored IFS value in the noise models is small, not NaN.

**Key.** The problem JSON of a HOM run has the boolean key `"inclination_flips"`, default `false`. The MATLAB option is `InclinationFlips`, a logical scalar, default `false`. No C function signature changes and `abi_version` stays 1. The row of Section 8b becomes `[a2, u, T, x0, a1, mesh, eig_re, eig_im, codim2(1..14), h, newton_iters]`, with stride $n(Nm+1) + N + 3n + 20$.

**Oracle (ours, numpy, independent of the engine).** Sandstede's model of Section 8b with $(a, b, c, \beta) = (-0.25, 1, -3, 1)$ and $\mu = \tilde\mu = \gamma = 0$. The plane $z = 0$ holds the orbit of Section 8b, test 1, for every $\alpha$, because $x^2(1-x) - y^2 = 0$ on it. $A = f_x(0)$ has the eigenvalues $-1.25$, $0.75$ and $-3$, so $n_s = 2$, $n_u = 1$, $\lambda_S = -1.25$, and NSA $= -0.5$ and ZES $= -1.25$ do not change sign. The numpy solve (trapezoid rule, dense, $\gamma$ free, the boundary rows of Section 8b-2) gives an IFS with a transversal zero at $\alpha^* \approx 1.8961$. IFS is about 21.4 at $\alpha = 0$ and about 8.86 at $\alpha = 1$ in its normalization ($\varphi_x$ at the midpoint equal to $-0.5$). The zero converges like $h^2$: at $T = 20$ the values for $h = 0.04$, $0.02$ and $0.01$ differ by $1.6 \cdot 10^{-3}$ and then $4.1 \cdot 10^{-4}$, and Richardson extrapolation gives 1.89605. It depends on $T$ like $e^{-T}$: at $h = 0.02$ the values are 1.89530, 1.89659, 1.896655 and 1.896673 for $T = 10$, 20, 30 and 40, a correction of about $+10^{-4}$ at $T = 20$. The oracle value is $\alpha^* = 1.8961$ with an error of about $2 \cdot 10^{-4}$.

**Guard.** In the models of Section 8b, tests 2 and 3, IFS is identically zero in the limit of large $T$, and at finite $T$ it is sign-indefinite noise of order $e^{-T}$ plus a discretization error (Section 8b-2, check 2). The first plan used the scale-free cosine $c_S = \langle v_S, \varphi(0)\rangle / (\|v_S\|\,\|\varphi(0)\|)$ (and $c_U$ with $v_{S+1}$ and $\varphi(1)$ for IFU), reporting IFS only where $|c_S|$ is at least a floor $\epsilon_g$. It was to be adopted only if the noise values of $|c_S|$ and the signal values on the oracle curve were two orders of magnitude apart. They are not (Measurements), so the cosine is not used.

The guard that is built acts on the bracket of a sign change. Let $a$ and $b$ be the values of IFS (or IFU) at two consecutive accepted points with $ab < 0$. The locator of Section 8 places a label in that bracket only if $\max(|a|, |b|) \ge \epsilon_b$, with $\epsilon_b = 10^{-8}$. The values are the ones of unit-norm $\varphi$ (Solve for the adjoint), including the factor $e^{-T\lambda_S/2}$ or $e^{+T\lambda_{S+1}/2}$. A bracket below the floor gets no label, and the run goes on. The test value in the row is not changed, and the bisection of `locate_one` still runs on the raw test function, since a floor on the value itself would stop it once the trial values get small. In code the guard is a method of the `Problem` trait of `pc-engine`, `crossing_ok(i, a, b) -> bool`, whose default returns true. The index `i` counts the codim-2 tests, so IFS is 12 and IFU is 13. `detect_inner` calls it for a codim-2 column whose bracket has a sign change or exactly one end at zero, before it calls `locate_one` or places a label at an end, and never for the LP, BP and H slots. A refused bracket gives no label and no warning. Only `HomProblem` overrides it, for the two IF columns. Every other problem type keeps its labels, so no other run changes.

Why $10^{-8}$: the noise values of $|\mathrm{IFS}|$ in Measurements are at most $2.2\cdot10^{-12}$ and the larger end values of the signal brackets are at least $2.4\cdot10^{-4}$ (oracle) and $7.4\cdot10^{-4}$ (second set), so $10^{-8}$ is the geometric middle of a gap of about eight orders of magnitude. That is a property of five runs of two models, not of the method. A genuine zero in a model whose $\varphi$ is small at the ends, for example a long mesh with a large $N$ (the norm of $\varphi$ is spread over $n(Nm+1)$ nodes), could be hidden. Acceptance 6 measures the separation on more models, $\lambda_S$ and $T$, and the floor stays at $10^{-8}$ only if the gap there is still at least four orders of magnitude on each side. The result of acceptance 6 is that the noise side has a gap of 6.9 orders and the signal side one of 3.9 orders, so the target of four orders is missed by 0.06 orders at one bracket, while the hard bounds of the acceptance (signal above $10^{-6}$, noise below $10^{-10}$) hold with room. The floor stays at $10^{-8}$.

**Measurements (2026-11-08, engine, JIT evaluator, `ncol` = 4, `ntst` = 8$T$, $T$ = 20, 30, 40, $\varphi$ of unit stacked 2-norm).** $\varphi$ is solved at every point of a HOM run with the sign rule above. Each cell lists the three periods in order, and a cell with one value holds for all three. The first-point values are at the first point of the run.

| Run | first-point $c_S$ | $\min\lvert c_S\rvert$ over the run | $\lvert c_U\rvert$ | first-point $\lVert\varphi(0)\rVert$ | $\lvert$IFS$\rvert$ over the run | sign changes of IFS |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Oracle, $\alpha$ from 0 to 3, 65 points | $-1.000$, $+1.000$, $+1.000$ | 0.9999, 1.000, 1.000 | 1.000 | $1.4\cdot10^{-5}$, $2.7\cdot10^{-8}$, $5.3\cdot10^{-11}$ | $1.3\cdot10^{-4}$ to 3.8 ($T$ = 20), $1.1\cdot10^{-4}$ to 3.8, $9.6\cdot10^{-5}$ to 3.8 | 3, 3, 3 |
| Test 2, $\mu$ from $-0.1$ to 0.1, 9 points | $-3.5\cdot10^{-10}$, $-7.7\cdot10^{-6}$, $-0.316$ | $3.1\cdot10^{-10}$, $1.7\cdot10^{-17}$, $2.1\cdot10^{-9}$ | 0.9997 | $4.2\cdot10^{-13}$, $1.3\cdot10^{-19}$, $4.2\cdot10^{-26}$ | $2.8\cdot10^{-18}$ to $4.0\cdot10^{-17}$, $7\cdot10^{-30}$ to $2.0\cdot10^{-17}$, $6.5\cdot10^{-18}$ to $5.1\cdot10^{-17}$ | 3, 2, 2 |
| Test 3, $\delta$ from $-0.1$ to 0.1, 14 points | $-1.9\cdot10^{-8}$, $-1.4\cdot10^{-11}$, $-1.2\cdot10^{-9}$ | $4.6\cdot10^{-9}$, $1.6\cdot10^{-12}$, $2.1\cdot10^{-10}$ | 0.966 | $4.8\cdot10^{-5}$, $3.2\cdot10^{-7}$, $2.2\cdot10^{-9}$ | $1.6\cdot10^{-12}$ to $2.2\cdot10^{-12}$, $2.3\cdot10^{-18}$ to $1.1\cdot10^{-16}$, $1.4\cdot10^{-17}$ to $8.0\cdot10^{-17}$ | 0, 4, 4 |

Three results follow.

1. *The cosine does not separate noise from signal.* At the first point of test 2 at $T$ = 40, $|c_S|$ is 0.316 against 1.000 on the oracle curve, which is less than one order of magnitude. $\varphi(0)$ there is $4\cdot10^{-26}$ in a vector of norm 1, and the solve still returns a direction for it, which is arbitrary. The noise values of $|c_S|$ run from $10^{-17}$ to 0.3, so no floor sits below the signal and above all the noise.
2. *The cosine hides real zeros in a generic model.* A genuine zero of IFS is a zero of $\langle v_S, \varphi(0)\rangle$, so $c_S$ is zero there unless the rest of $\varphi(0)$ vanishes too. On the oracle model $\varphi(0)$ stays parallel to $v_S$ (the plane $z = 0$ decouples), so $c_S$ jumps between $+1$ and $-1$ across the zero and never gets small, and a floor on $|c_S|$ does not hide that zero. It would hide the zero of a model where $\varphi(0)$ has other components.
3. *The scale $\lVert\varphi(0)\rVert / \lVert\varphi\rVert$ does not separate either.* Over all points of the runs the noise models span $4\cdot10^{-26}$ to $4.8\cdot10^{-5}$ and the oracle spans $5\cdot10^{-14}$ to $1.4\cdot10^{-5}$, so the ranges overlap.

The sign changes of IFS in the noise models are spurious: IFS there is $10^{-16}$ or smaller at $T \ge 30$ and $2\cdot10^{-12}$ at $T$ = 20 in test 3, and a label there would be wrong.

The value of IFS itself does separate on these three models. The noise is at most $2.2\cdot10^{-12}$ in unit-norm $\varphi$, and the IFS values at the points that bracket the zeros of the oracle have a larger end value of at least $2.4\cdot10^{-4}$ in absolute value for every bracket (about eight orders of magnitude apart). A bracket-level floor on the larger of the two end values (a label only where the larger end value is at least $\epsilon_b$) would use it. It needs a new hook that `detect_inner` in `locate.rs` calls with the two end values, because a floor on the test value itself breaks the location of the zero by bisection, where the trial points have small values. The floor carries the factor $e^{-T\lambda_S/2}$, so its scale depends on $T$, $\lambda_S$ and the normalization of $\varphi$. Three models at $T \le 40$ do not fix $\epsilon_b$ for other models and no reference gives a value, so the floor is adopted as in Guard above, with the validation of acceptance 6 behind it.

*The oracle has more than one real zero.* On the oracle run the engine's IFS changes sign three times at each of $T$ = 20, 30 and 40, at the same three steps: between points 41 and 42 ($\alpha$ = 1.9258 at point 42), between 49 and 50 ($\alpha$ = 2.3258) and between 56 and 57 ($\alpha$ = 2.6758). The first is the transversal zero of the oracle ($\alpha^* \approx 1.8961$). At $T$ = 20 the values of IFS at points 42, 50 and 57 are $2.5\cdot10^{-3}$, $-6.6\cdot10^{-4}$ and $3.3\cdot10^{-4}$, and the sign of the whole curve is fixed by the sign rule, so it differs between periods. The independent numpy solve, with $\varphi$ divided by its stacked norm and the sign fixed by continuity in $\alpha$, has sign changes of IFS at $\alpha$ = 1.8966, 2.2847 and 2.6382 for $T$ = 20 with 1000 intervals, and at 1.8986, 2.2829 and 2.6336 for $T$ = 40. With 400, 800 and 1200 intervals at $T$ = 20 they settle (1.8995, 2.2822, 2.6321; 1.8969, 2.2845, 2.6375; 1.8964, 2.2849, 2.6385). Each lies inside one bracket of the engine run (step 0.05 in $\alpha$), so all three are zeros of IFS for this model and not engine noise. Whether the later two are inclination flips in the limit of large $T$ was not checked. Acceptance 3 uses a range of $\alpha$ that holds only the first one.

*A second parameter set.* The oracle model at $(a, b, c, \beta) = (-0.5, 1.5, -3, 1)$ has $\lambda_S = -2$ instead of $-1.25$. The engine run with free $[\tilde\mu, \alpha]$, $\alpha$ from 0 to 4 and 85 points gives four sign changes of IFS at each of $T$ = 20, 30 and 40, and $|\mathrm{IFS}|$ over the run lies between $7.4\cdot10^{-4}$ and 7.8 at every $T$. The numpy sweep with unit-norm $\varphi$ ($T$ = 20, 600 intervals, step 0.1) has four sign changes too, in $(1.9, 2.0)$, $(2.4, 2.5)$, $(3.1, 3.2)$ and $(3.8, 3.9)$, and the four engine brackets, $(1.976, 2.026)$, $(2.476, 2.526)$, $(3.076, 3.126)$ and $(3.826, 3.876)$ (the same at each $T$), each overlap one of those intervals, in the same order. The numpy intervals are 0.1 wide, so this shows the two sets are consistent, not that each bracket holds the numpy zero. The larger end value of every bracket is at least $7.4\cdot10^{-4}$ here, against a noise of at most $2.2\cdot10^{-12}$ in the other models. This changes $\lambda_S$ but not the model, so it does not show that one $\epsilon_b$ fits other models.

**Out of scope.** Tests 15 and 16, IEQUIB = 2, the orientation index, heteroclinic orbits, Lin's method, a free $T$ and the homotopy start.

**Acceptance.** The tolerances are upper bounds, and the first run records the measured values here, as in Section 8a.

1. *Solve against the closed form.* On the orbit of Section 8b-2, check 1 ($a = \tilde\mu = \alpha = \gamma = \mu = 0$, $b = 1$, $c = -2$) at $T = 20$ with $N_{\text{col}} = 4$, the solved $\varphi$, rescaled to unit norm with the sign of $(\dot y, -\dot x, 0)$, differs from the closed form by an amount that falls with $N$, and $|\gamma|$ falls with $N$ and is below $10^{-6}$ at $N = 40$ and below $10^{-8}$ at $N = 80$. Measured: the largest difference is $8.4\cdot10^{-5}$, $1.5\cdot10^{-6}$ and $4.0\cdot10^{-8}$ and $|\gamma|$ is $3.3\cdot10^{-6}$, $2.4\cdot10^{-8}$ and $2.6\cdot10^{-10}$ at $N = 20$, 40 and 80. Done (`hom_adjoint.rs`, `solved_adjoint_matches_the_closed_form`).
2. *Residual, determinism and sign.* The solved $(\varphi, \gamma)$ satisfies the residual of Section 8b-2 to $10^{-10}$ where $A$ is not symmetric ($\mu = 0.9$), two calls give the same bits, and `orient_adjoint` makes the largest end component positive at the first point and flips $\varphi$ when its end vector has a negative inner product with the previous one. Done (`solved_adjoint_satisfies_the_residual`, `solved_adjoint_is_deterministic` and the unit tests of `hom_adjoint.rs`). The solve gives the same $\varphi$ for different seeds of $r$ up to sign and $10^{-9}$ (`the_solution_does_not_depend_on_the_normalization_seed`).
3. *Oracle zero.* The HOM run on the oracle model continues in $(\alpha, \tilde\mu)$ from $\alpha = 0$ to $\alpha = 2.1$ at $T = 20$ with the key on, and reports one IFS label with $|\alpha - 1.8961| < 5\cdot10^{-3}$ and no other label. The range stops before the two later zeros of Measurements.
4. *Noise floor.* With the key on, the runs of Section 8b, tests 2 and 3, report no IFS and no IFU label, at $T$ = 20, 30 and 40.
5. *Layout and hook.* The row width, `cycle_stride` and the label list agree with the 14 entries in the Rust unit tests, the FFI tests and the CLI tests, and in `Rows2BranchTest.m` and `HomCurveTest.m`. With the key off the two columns are NaN and the labels of Section 8b are unchanged. A unit test of `detect_inner` with a problem whose `crossing_ok` refuses one column shows that the column gets no label, that the other columns keep theirs, and that the default method changes no existing label.
6. *Floor validation.* The larger end value of the brackets of IFS and IFU is measured on runs that differ from Measurements in $\lambda_S$, in $T$ (10, 20, 30, 40) and in the model, including a model other than Sandstede's in which IFS or IFU has a transversal zero. The test asserts that every bracket that holds a numpy zero of the same model has a larger end value above $10^{-4}$ and that no bracket in a noise model has one above $10^{-10}$. If a signal bracket falls below $10^{-6}$ or a noise bracket rises above $10^{-10}$, the floor is not adopted and IFS and IFU are dropped (option (c)). Done (`hom_inclination.rs`, `every_ifs_bracket_of_the_signal_models_clears_the_floor` and `the_noise_models_have_no_bracket_above_1e_10`), with one deviation: the signal test asserts the bound $10^{-6}$ of the fallback rule and not the target $10^{-4}$, because one bracket is below $10^{-4}$ (see Measured).

   *Setup.* `ntst` = 8$T$, `ncol` = 4, `hmax` = 0.05, the key on, release build, $T$ = 10, 20, 30 and 40. The signal families are Sandstede's model at $(a, b, c, \beta) = (-0.25, 1, -3, 1)$ ($\alpha$ from $-0.1$ to 3, 65 points) and at $(-0.5, 1.5, -3, 1)$ ($\alpha$ from $-0.1$ to 4, 85 points), and `sandstede_lin` at $(-0.25, 1, -3, 1)$ ($\alpha$ from $-0.1$ to 4, 85 points). `sandstede_lin` is our own variant (`tests/models/sandstede_lin.ode`): the $z$ coupling is multiplied by $(1 + x)$, so the plane $z = 0$ still holds the orbit for $|a| < b$ and $A$ is the one of Sandstede's model, and the adjoint coupling differs. The count of numpy zeros comes from our own trapezoid computation (T = 20, 600 intervals, step 0.1) and is 3, 4 and 7 for the three families. The noise families are the orbit flip model of Section 8b, test 2, and the Belyakov model of Section 8b, test 3.

   *Measured.* At every $T$ the engine has as many brackets as labels as numpy zeros (3, 4 and 7), and every label is IFS. The smallest larger end value of a bracket, by family and $T$ = 10, 20, 30, 40: oracle $3.54\cdot10^{-4}$, $3.27\cdot10^{-4}$, $2.81\cdot10^{-4}$, $2.48\cdot10^{-4}$; second set $2.76\cdot10^{-2}$ at $T$ = 10 down to $2.78\cdot10^{-3}$ at $T$ = 40; `sandstede_lin` $2.18\cdot10^{-4}$, $1.15\cdot10^{-4}$, $9.85\cdot10^{-5}$, $8.695\cdot10^{-5}$. The smallest over the 12 sets is $8.695\cdot10^{-5}$ (`sandstede_lin`, $T$ = 40, the seventh and last bracket). That is 1.5% under the target of $10^{-4}$ and 87 times above the bound of $10^{-6}$, and it is 3.9 orders above $\epsilon_b$. No IFU bracket appears in any signal family. In the noise models the largest end value of any bracket of IFS or IFU, over $T$ = 10 to 40, is $1.4\cdot10^{-15}$, which is 6.9 orders below $\epsilon_b$, and IFU has no bracket. The raw IFS of the Belyakov model at $T$ = 10 reaches $7.5\cdot10^{-6}$ without a sign change, so it gets no label, and the floor does not touch it.

   *Limits.* (i) The larger end of a bracket scales with the step of the continuation: it is about half a step times the slope of the test function at the zero, so a floor of $10^{-8}$ hides a transversal zero only where that product is below $10^{-8}$, which needs a step below about $10^{-5}$ at the slopes seen here, and a user who runs with a much smaller step or a much flatter test function could lose a label. (ii) No signal zero of IFU exists in any tested model, so the signal side of IFU is untested and only its noise side is measured. (iii) `sandstede_lin` has the same planar orbit and the same $A$ as Sandstede's model, so it varies the adjoint coupling and not the orbit or the spectrum. (iv) Whether the later zeros of the oracle are inclination flips in the limit of large $T$ is not checked (Measurements). (v) The value $\epsilon_b = 10^{-8}$ is our own choice. No reference gives one.

7. *Regression.* The existing `cargo test` suite stays green.

## 8c. Characteristic roots of linear delay equations (phase E9a, step 1)

Step 1 of E9a finds the characteristic roots of
$$\dot x(t) = A_0 x(t) + \sum_{j=1}^m A_j x(t - \tau_j),\qquad x \in \mathbb{R}^d,$$
with real $d \times d$ matrices $A_j$ and delays $\tau_j > 0$. Write $\tau_{\max} = \max_j \tau_j$. This step reads no `.ode` file and changes no parser, IR or JIT code. The `.ode` syntax for delays, equilibria and their stability in a nonlinear delay equation, Hopf detection, continuation of a delay equation and the normal forms of Bosschaert et al. are later steps of E9a. Neutral equations, distributed delays and state-dependent delays are out of scope.

**Characteristic equation.** $\lambda \in \mathbb{C}$ is a root exactly when $\det\Delta(\lambda) = 0$, with
$$\Delta(\lambda) = \lambda I - A_0 - \sum_{j=1}^m A_j e^{-\lambda\tau_j},\qquad \Delta'(\lambda) = I + \sum_{j=1}^m \tau_j A_j e^{-\lambda\tau_j}.$$
The equation is of retarded type, so only finitely many roots have $\operatorname{Re}\lambda \ge r$ for any $r$. Such a root is an eigenvalue of $A_0 + \sum_j A_j e^{-\lambda\tau_j}$, hence $|\lambda| \le \|A_0\| + \sum_j \|A_j\| e^{-r\tau_j}$ in any induced norm. The tests use this bound to choose a contour that holds every root with $\operatorname{Re}\lambda \ge r$.

**Pseudospectral matrix.** The nodes are $\theta_k = \tfrac{\tau_{\max}}{2}(\cos(k\pi/n) - 1)$ for $k = 0, \dots, n$, the Chebyshev extreme points mapped to $[-\tau_{\max}, 0]$, with $\theta_0 = 0$ and $\theta_n = -\tau_{\max}$ set exactly. $\ell_k$ is the Lagrange polynomial of degree $n$ with $\ell_k(\theta_i) = \delta_{ik}$ and $D_{ik} = \ell_k'(\theta_i)$. The vector $u = (u_0, \dots, u_n) \in \mathbb{R}^{d(n+1)}$ stands for the samples $x(t + \theta_k)$ of the history, and $u_k' = \sum_i D_{ki} u_i$ for $k \ge 1$ replaces the shift. The $d(n+1)$-square matrix $A_n$ has the blocks
$$(A_n)_{0k} = \delta_{k0} A_0 + \sum_{j=1}^m \ell_k(-\tau_j) A_j,\qquad (A_n)_{ik} = D_{ik} I_d\quad (i \ge 1).$$
The eigenvalues of $A_n$ approximate the roots with $|\operatorname{Im}\lambda|$ and $|\lambda|$ small against $n/\tau_{\max}$. Breda, Maset and Vermiglio (2005) introduced this construction for several discrete delays; I read only the bibliographic record of that paper. de Wolff, Scarabel, Verduyn Lunel and Diekmann (arXiv:2006.13810, read) write it for the scalar equation with one delay in the form $\Delta_n(\lambda) = \lambda - L(\ell_0 + P(D - \lambda I)^{-1}D\mathbf{1})$ and show that $\Delta_n \to \Delta$ on every compact set with an error of at most $n^{-1/2}(C/n)^n$ (their Lemma 5.1), so by Hurwitz's theorem the roots of $\Delta_n$ converge to those of $\Delta$ there. The block form above for $d > 1$ and $m > 1$ is my own generalization of that scalar form, and the tests below check it with exact residuals and root counts, not with a citation.

The barycentric form gives $D$ and $\ell_k$ without solving for the interpolant. With $w_k = (-1)^k$, halved at $k = 0$ and $k = n$, $D_{ik} = \frac{w_k/w_i}{\theta_i - \theta_k}$ for $i \ne k$ and $D_{ii} = -\sum_{k \ne i} D_{ik}$. For $s$ that is not a node, $\ell_k(s) = \frac{w_k/(s - \theta_k)}{\sum_i w_i/(s - \theta_i)}$, and $\ell_k(s) = \delta_{ki}$ when $s = \theta_i$ (Berrut and Trefethen 2004). A delay $\tau_j = \tau_{\max}$ always lands on the node $\theta_n$, and a delay can also land on an interior node, so the second case is part of the code and of the tests. Tests 1 and 2 check the formulas by polynomial exactness and against the weights of the product formula.

**Refinement.** Every eigenvalue $\lambda_0$ of $A_n$ with $\operatorname{Re}\lambda_0 \ge r_{\min} - 10^{-6}(1 + |r_{\min}|)$ and $\operatorname{Im}\lambda_0 \ge 0$ is the start of a Newton iteration for $(v, \lambda) \in \mathbb{C}^d \times \mathbb{C}$ on
$$F(v, \lambda) = \begin{pmatrix}\Delta(\lambda)v\\ c^{H}v - 1\end{pmatrix} = 0,\qquad J = \begin{pmatrix}\Delta(\lambda) & \Delta'(\lambda)v\\ c^{H} & 0\end{pmatrix}.$$
This is my own derivation. $J$ is nonsingular when $\lambda$ is a simple root, meaning $w^{H}\Delta'(\lambda)v \ne 0$ for a left null vector $w$ of $\Delta(\lambda)$, and $c^{H}v \ne 0$. The step is solved as a real system of order $2(d+1)$ with the LU of `pc-linalg`. The start vector comes from two steps of inverse iteration on $\Delta(\lambda_0 + \delta)$ with $\delta = 10^{-8}(1 + |\lambda_0|)$ and the right-hand side $b_i = 1 + i/(d+1)$, and $c = v_0/(v_0^{H}v_0)$ stays fixed. The scaled residual is
$$\rho = \frac{\|\Delta(\lambda)v\|_2}{\|v\|_2\, s(\lambda)},\qquad s(\lambda) = |\lambda| + \|A_0\|_F + \sum_j \|A_j\|_F\, e^{-\operatorname{Re}\lambda\,\tau_j}.$$
The iteration stops when $|\delta\lambda| \le 10^{-13}(1 + |\lambda|)$ or after 30 steps, and the root is accepted when $\rho \le 10^{-12}$ and $\operatorname{Re}\lambda \ge r_{\min}$. It is not accepted when the LU reports $J$ singular, when an iterate is not finite, or when $\rho$ stays above the bound. An eigenvalue of $A_n$ with $\operatorname{Re}\lambda_0 \ge r_{\min}$ that does not give an accepted root goes into a second list, `unrefined`, with its $A_n$ value, and with its conjugate when $\operatorname{Im}\lambda_0 > 0$. Nothing in that range is dropped without a record.

The problem is real, so an accepted root with $\operatorname{Im}\lambda > 0$ is returned with its exact conjugate. A start with $\operatorname{Im}\lambda_0 = 0$ stays on the real axis, since $\Delta$ is real there and every operation of the iteration then has a zero imaginary part exactly. Accepted roots that agree within $10^{-11}(1 + |\lambda|)$ are one root, because two eigenvalues of $A_n$ can refine to the same simple root. The result is sorted by real part descending, then imaginary part descending.

**Limits stated up front.** The run does not detect multiplicity. A root of multiplicity $k$ shows up as up to $k$ entries that each have an error near $\varepsilon^{1/k}$, or in `unrefined` when $J$ is singular. `n` fixes which roots can be found. A refined root is a root to the residual bound whatever $n$ is, but a root that no eigenvalue of $A_n$ approximates is missing without a message. The strip $\operatorname{Re}\lambda \ge r_{\min}$ holds roots with $|\operatorname{Im}\lambda|$ up to $B_r$ (test 7), and $A_n$ resolves only the modes with $|\operatorname{Im}\lambda|$ below about $n\pi/\tau_{\max}$, so $n$ has to grow with $B_r\tau_{\max}/\pi$ as $r_{\min}$ falls. Test 7b shows a run that returns a subset of the roots, each with a residual below $10^{-12}$ and an empty `unrefined`. Comparing runs at $n$ and $2n$, or the argument-principle count of test 7, is how a user checks completeness. The input has no default for `n` or `r_min`.

**Input rules.** $d \ge 1$, each matrix has $d^2$ finite entries, there is at least one delay, every $\tau_j$ is finite and positive, $n \ge 1$, $d(n+1) \le 4000$, and $r_{\min}$ is finite. Anything else is `BadProblem`.

**Command line.** `pc-cli dde-roots <problem.json>` reads
```json
{"dim": 2, "a0": [..d*d row-major..], "delays": [{"tau": 1.0, "a": [..d*d..]}], "n": 30, "re_min": -2.0}
```
and prints `{"n", "roots": [{"re", "im", "residual"}], "unrefined": [{"re", "im"}]}`. The reader is strict: a key it does not take is an error. Exit codes follow `cont`.

**Acceptance tests.** All are Rust tests with closed forms or an independent count.

1. Nodes and $D$: $\theta_0 = 0$ and $\theta_n = -\tau_{\max}$ exactly; each row of $D$ sums to 0; $D$ is exact on $\theta^p$ for $p \le n$, $n \le 12$ and $\tau_{\max} = 2$, within $10^{-9}\max|p'|$ (Chebyshev differentiation loses a factor of order $n^2$ in rounding); $D$ built from the product-formula weights equals $D$ from the closed-form weights for $n = 5$ to $10^{-12}$ relative.
2. $\ell_k$: $\sum_k \ell_k(s) = 1$ and $\sum_k \ell_k(s)\theta_k^p = s^p$ for $p \le n$ at nodes and off nodes.
3. $\dot x = -\tfrac{\pi}{2}x(t-1)$: $\pm i\pi/2$ are roots within $10^{-12}$, the largest real part over all returned roots is $0$ within $10^{-12}$, and every returned root has $\rho \le 10^{-12}$.
4. $\dot x = a x(t-1)$ with $a = 1$: the real root is $W_0(1) = 0.5671432904097838\ldots$ (the omega constant) to $10^{-14}$, the largest real part of all roots. With $a = -0.1$ the root nearest zero is a real root of $\lambda = a e^{-\lambda}$ checked by the scalar residual.
5. Rotation system, $A_1 = \begin{pmatrix}a & -b\\ b & a\end{pmatrix}$, $A_0 = 0$: $A_1$ acts on $\mathbb{R}^2$ as the complex number $\mu = a + ib$, so the roots are those of $\lambda = \mu e^{-\lambda\tau}$ and of its conjugate. Every returned root satisfies one of them to $10^{-12}$, and the count with $\operatorname{Re}\lambda \ge r$ equals the argument-principle count of test 7. The commuting pair $A_0 = \begin{pmatrix}p & -q\\ q & p\end{pmatrix}$, $A_1$ as above gives $\lambda = (p + iq) + (a + ib)e^{-\lambda\tau}$ and its conjugate.
6. Two delays, scalar: $\dot x = a x(t - \tau_1) + b x(t - \tau_2)$ with $\tau_2 = 2\tau_1$. The larger delay is the node $\theta_n$. The smaller one is the interior node $\theta_{n/2}$ for even $n$ and lies between nodes for odd $n$, and the test runs $n = 20$ and $n = 21$. Every returned root satisfies $\lambda = a e^{-\lambda\tau_1} + b e^{-\lambda\tau_2}$ with $\rho \le 10^{-12}$ measured by the scalar residual, and the two runs return the same roots to $10^{-11}$.
7. Completeness: for $d = 2$ and $d = 3$ systems with non-commuting matrices, the number of returned roots with $\operatorname{Re}\lambda \ge r$ equals the number of zeros of $\det\Delta$ inside the rectangle $[r, B_r] \times [-B_r, B_r]$, counted as the winding number of $\det\Delta$ along the boundary. Here $B_r$ is the Frobenius bound $\|A_0\|_F + \sum_j \|A_j\|_F e^{-r\tau_j}$ plus 1, which bounds the spectral one. The test computes the determinant with its own complex LU. It samples each edge on a uniform grid of spacing at most 0.01 and then bisects until each phase increment is below $\pi/4$. A single segment per edge aliases, because a whole number of turns gives a phase ratio near 1. The test fails when $|\det\Delta| \le 10^{-8}$ on the contour or when the winding is not near an integer. `unrefined` is empty and every residual is at most $10^{-12}$. The $d = 2$ system with delays 1.0 and 1.7 runs at $r = -2$, $n = 80$ and has 18 roots. The $d = 3$ system with one delay of 1.3 runs at $r = -2.5$, $n = 100$ and has 30 roots.
7b. Small $n$: the $d = 2$ system of test 7 at $r = -3$ has 76 roots by the winding number. $n = 40$ returns 50 of them, and $n = 80$ and $n = 120$ return all 76. Every run has residuals below $10^{-12}$ and an empty `unrefined`. At $r = -2$ the count is 18 for every $n$ from 40 to 200.
8. Errors: a matrix of the wrong length, no delay, $\tau \le 0$, NaN in a matrix, $n = 0$, $d(n+1) > 4000$ and a NaN $r_{\min}$ each give `BadProblem`.
9. `pc-cli dde-roots` on the data of test 3 prints roots with the same values; a missing `n`, an unknown key and an unreadable file exit with the `cont` codes.

## 8d. `.ode` delays, equilibria and their stability (phase E9a, step 2)

Step 2 lets a `.ode` file call `delay(x,tau)`, finds the equilibria of the resulting delay equation and reports the characteristic roots at an equilibrium with the code of Section 8c. It does not simulate a delay equation, continue one, detect a Hopf point on a branch, or compute normal forms. Those are later steps. `lower` and `build` keep refusing every model that calls `delay`, with the error they give today (`IrErrorKind::Delay`), so simulation, continuation, ensembles, code generation, the C ABI and the MATLAB API see no change. The only ways in are `lower_dde`, `build_dde` and `pc-cli dde-eq`.

**What is not verified.** XPPAUT also reads `@ delay=<max>` and takes the history before $t = 0$ from the initial values. PhaseCont reads neither: an equilibrium and its roots need no history, and the option is not used. I did not check what XPPAUT does with `delay(x,tau)` when `x` is not a state, or when `tau` is an expression, so those forms are refused and not guessed at.

**Accepted form.** `delay(x,tau)` may stand wherever a state may in the right side of a state equation, an `aux` formula, a fixed quantity or a user function body, with
- `x` a bare state name, not shadowed by a formal argument of the enclosing user function, and
- `tau` a positive numeric literal, a `number` constant, or a bare name declared with `par`.

Anything else is an error at the call (`IrErrorKind::Delay`, message "the first argument of delay must be a state" or "the delay time must be a number or a parameter"): another first argument, any expression for `tau`, a non-positive literal, a derived parameter or a fixed quantity used as `tau`, a wrong argument count, and a delay in a model with difference equations. `pc-ode` does not check the form. It keeps its ODE0054 warning on every call of `delay`, and the text now says that `delay` is read only by `pc-cli dde-eq` and that simulation and continuation do not support it. `lower_dde` makes every check above, and it also refuses a delay in a table, in the formula of a derived parameter and in a model with difference equations. A parameter value that is not finite and positive when the delays are read is `BadProblem`.

**Lowering.** `lower_dde` is `lower` with the delay refusal replaced by the rules below, and it needs at least one delay call. Each distinct pair (state $k$, delay time) found in the model becomes one hidden parameter, appended after the declared parameters, so that `n_p` counts it, `p` has length `n_p + 1` as before and the last slot is still `t`. The pair is keyed by the state and by the literal value or the parameter, so `delay(x,tau)` twice is one slot, `delay(x,1)` and `delay(x,tau)` are two slots even when `tau = 1`, and `delay(x,1)` and `delay(x,1.0)` are one. A `number` constant is keyed and named by its value, so it shares a slot with the literal of that value. Slots are numbered in the order of first occurrence in the statements of the expanded model. The hidden parameter is named by the call as written with the literal printed by Rust's `{}`, for example `delay(y,tau)` or `delay(x,1)`; no declaration can have such a name. Its entry in `par_values` is NaN, so an evaluation that forgets to set it gives NaN and not a plausible number. `IrModel` gets `n_user_params` and a `delays: Vec<DelaySlot>` with the state index and the delay time (a literal or a parameter index) of each slot. The JIT, the AOT C code, the entry kinds, the C ABI and the `Interp` walk see an ordinary parameter vector. `Interp` reads the slot of a call from the same table.

**Linearization.** Let $G(x, p) = f(x, \ell(x), p)$, where $\ell$ puts $x_k$ into the slot of every pair $(k, \cdot)$, and let the distinct delay times be $\tau_1 < \dots < \tau_m$ (slots of equal value are merged, whether they came from a literal or a parameter). With $J_u$ and $J_p$ the `JacU` and `JacP` entries of the lowered model at $(x, \ell(x), p)$,
$$A_0 = J_u,\qquad (A_j)_{\cdot k} = \sum_{s:\ \text{state}(s) = k,\ \tau(s) = \tau_j} (J_p)_{\cdot s},\qquad \frac{\partial G}{\partial x} = A_0 + \sum_j A_j.$$
At an equilibrium $x(t - \tau) = x^\ast$, so the derivative of $f$ with respect to a delay time vanishes there ($f$ depends on $\tau$ only through $x'(t-\tau) = 0$), and $\tau$ enters the characteristic equation only through $e^{-\lambda\tau_j}$ of Section 8c. This is why a delay time held as a plain parameter is enough, and why the same derivation does not carry over to periodic orbits.

**Equilibria.** $G(x^\ast, p) = 0$ by Newton's method with $\partial G/\partial x$ above, one LU per step, the tolerances and iteration cap of the corrector (`Options`), and `NewtonFailed` otherwise. The routine is `dde::equilibrium(model, setup, opts)`. The caller gives $p$ for the declared parameters only; the routine fills the slots.

**Stability.** `dde::residual(model, x, p)` (exported as `dde_residual`) returns the largest entry of $|f(x, x, p)|$ and is NaN when any entry is NaN; `pc-cli dde-eq` prints it so that a caller sees how well $x$ solves the equation. `dde::stability(model, x, p, root_options)` linearizes at $x$ and calls `characteristic_roots` with $A_0$ and the $(\tau_j, A_j)$. It needs at least one delay time: a model with delays always has one. It does not check that $x$ is an equilibrium. The roots with $\operatorname{Re}\lambda \ge r_{\min}$ are returned with the limits of Section 8c, and `n` and `r_min` have no default.

**Command line.** `pc-cli dde-eq <problem.json>` reads
```json
{"model": "path.ode", "params": {"r": 1.5, "tau": 1.0}, "x0": [0.8], "n": 30, "re_min": -2.0}
```
with `params` naming declared parameters (any other name is an error; one that is missing keeps the file's value, as `cont` does), runs Newton from `x0`, then the roots there, and prints `{"x", "residual", "delays": [tau_j], "roots": [{"re", "im", "residual"}], "unrefined": [{"re", "im"}]}`. The reader is strict like that of `dde-roots`. Exit codes follow `cont`.

**Acceptance tests.** Rust tests with closed forms.

1. Delayed logistic `x' = r*x*(1-delay(x,tau))`, $r = 1.5$, $\tau = 1$. The lowered model has one hidden slot and `n_p` is 3. Newton from 0.8 with the iteration cap set to 8 and both tolerances set to $10^{-14}$ returns $x^\ast = 1$ within $10^{-14}$. At $x^\ast = 1$, $A_0 = 0$ and $A_1 = (-r)$ exactly; at $x^\ast = 0$, $A_0 = (r)$ and $A_1 = (0)$ and the root $\lambda = r$ is returned within $10^{-12}$. At $x^\ast = 1$ the largest real part is negative for $r\tau = 1.5$, positive for $r\tau = 1.7$, and $0$ within $10^{-12}$ for $r\tau = \pi/2$ (the characteristic equation is $\lambda = -r e^{-\lambda\tau}$). The test fixes $\tau = 1$ and takes $r \in \{1.5, 1.7, \pi/2\}$, so the roots at $r = \pi/2$ include $\pm i\pi/2$.
2. `x' = a - x - 0.5*delay(x,tau)^2` with $a = 1.5$: $x^\ast = -1 + \sqrt{1 + 2a} = 1$, $A_0 = (-1)$, $A_1 = (-1)$ and $\partial G/\partial x = -2$. Newton from 0.5 with the same cap and tolerances returns $x^\ast$ within $10^{-14}$. The Jacobian $A_0 + A_1$ is the reason: with $A_0$ alone the iteration map would be $x \mapsto a - x^2/2$, whose multiplier at the root is $-1$. The roots satisfy $\lambda = -1 - e^{-\lambda\tau}$ with $\rho \le 10^{-12}$.
3. Two states, two delays, `x' = -delay(y,tau1)`, `y' = delay(x,tau2) - 2*y` with $\tau_1 = 0.7$ and $\tau_2 = 1.3$ at the origin: $A_0 = \operatorname{diag}(0, -2)$, the delay times come out as $[0.7, 1.3]$ with $A$ equal to $\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$ and $\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$. The same model with the two assignments of the parameter names swapped gives the times in the same ascending order.
4. Merging: `x' = -delay(x,tau) - 2*delay(y,tau)`, `y' = x - y` with `tau = 1` has two slots and one delay time with $A_1 = \begin{pmatrix}-1&-2\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$. `x' = -delay(x,1) - 0.5*delay(x,2)` has two slots and two delay times, $A_1 = (-1)$ and $A_2 = (-0.5)$. `delay(x,tau)` used twice in one model has one slot. A literal `1` and a parameter equal to 1 give two slots and one delay time.
5. Slot order: for `x' = delay(y,t2) + delay(x,t1)` the hidden names are `delay(y,t2)` and `delay(x,t1)` in that order, and `n_user_params` is the declared count.
6. Both evaluators (JIT and `Fallback`) give `F`, `JacU` and `JacP` equal within $10^{-14}$ relative on a model with three slots, for several values of the hidden parameters, including every hidden column of `JacP`.
7. Refusals, each with the span of the call: `delay(x+1,tau)`, `delay(x,2*tau)`, `delay(x,y)`, `delay(a,tau)` with `a` a parameter, `delay(w,tau)` with `w` an `aux` and with `w` a fixed quantity, `delay(x,0)`, `delay(x,-1)`, a derived parameter as `tau`, `delay(x)`, `delay(x,tau,1)`, a delay in a user function whose formal argument is named `x`, a delay in a table, a delay in the formula of a derived parameter and a delay in a map. Every one of these is an error of `lower_dde`, and `lower` refuses each of them as it refuses any delay. A parameter `tau` set to 0, to $-1$, to NaN and to infinity gives `BadProblem` in `dde::equilibrium` and `dde::stability`. A delay in a user function body that names a global state as `x` is accepted.
8. `lower` and `build` of a model with a delay, valid or not, give the error of kind `Delay` that they give today; `lower_dde` and `build_dde` of a model without a delay are errors. A model without delays has the same parameter vector, `n_p`, entries and results as before this step (the existing suites, including the `delayed.ode` case of `pc-cli`, pass unchanged).
9. `pc-ode`: every call of `delay` still gives ODE0054 at the call, with the new text, and the checker, corpus, golden and language reference tests say so.
10. `pc-cli dde-eq` on the data of test 1 prints $x = 1$, one delay time and roots with the values of test 1; `residual` is at most $10^{-10}$ there. A model path is read relative to the problem file. An unknown parameter name, an unknown key, a missing `model`, a wrong type, a wrong length of `x0` and an unreadable problem file exit 2. An unreadable model, a model without a delay, a delay of the wrong form, a model that reads `t` and a Newton run that fails exit 1. A parameter the problem does not give keeps the value of the model file.

## 8e. Hopf points along a branch of delay equilibria (phase E9a, step 3)

Step 3 continues an equilibrium of a delay equation in one declared parameter and reports where characteristic roots cross the imaginary axis. A pair that crosses at $\pm i\omega$ is a Hopf point, and one real root that crosses zero comes with a fold or a branch point, which the engine already finds. It does not continue a Hopf point in two parameters, compute a Lyapunov coefficient, follow a periodic orbit, or compute a normal form. It changes no C function and no MATLAB function, so the only ways in are `dde::continue_equilibrium` and `pc-cli dde-branch`.

**What is not verified.** I did not read how DDE-BIFTOOL or Knut locate a Hopf point along a branch. Counting the roots with positive real part at each point and bisecting where the count changes is my own method, built from the definition of a local bifurcation of an equilibrium (a root on the imaginary axis). The tests below check it against closed forms and against an argument-principle count that shares no code with the engine.

**The branch.** The branch is a curve of solutions of the equation of Section 8d, $G(x, p) = f(x, \ell(x), p) = 0$, with the free parameter $\alpha$. The start is the equilibrium that Newton's method finds from `x0` at the starting value of $\alpha$. The continuation is the pseudo-arclength continuation of `continue_ep`, through `EpProblem` on the collapsed model and `palc::continue_branch`. The free parameter is a declared parameter and may be a delay time. $G$ does not depend on a delay time, so a branch in a delay time has constant $x$. A delay time must stay positive, so when the free parameter is a delay time, `Options::bounds` must have a lower bound above 0, and `continue_equilibrium` returns `BadProblem` otherwise. `Options` are those of `continue_ep`, except that
- `detect_h` is off, since the eigenvalues of $\partial G/\partial x$ say nothing about a delay equation, and
- the refinement pass of `continue_ep` is not run. It needs second derivatives (`Dir` entries) that are not defined for a delay equation, and the fold coefficient of the collapsed model is not that of the delay equation. LP and BP points keep the location the step control gives them, which `locate_one` brackets to $10^{-13}$ in arclength, and they carry no coefficient. The `eigenvalues` stored with each point are those of $\partial G/\partial x$ and have no meaning for a delay equation; `dde-branch` does not print them.

**The count.** $N(\alpha)$ is the number of characteristic roots with $\operatorname{Re}\lambda > 0$, from `dde::stability` at $(x, p)$ with `p[free]` set to $\alpha$, with the options `n` and `re_min` of Section 8c. `re_min` must be negative, so that every root with positive real part is searched for, and a `re_min` of 0 or above is `BadProblem`. A root with $\operatorname{Re}\lambda = 0$ exactly does not count. A point is counted when the run ends, once per point. An error in `dde::stability` at any point is returned as the error of the run.

**Crossings.** Let step $k$ run from point $k$ to point $k+1$ with $N_k \ne N_{k+1}$. Write $d = \|x_{k+1} - x_k\|$ and $v = (x_{k+1} - x_k)/d$, where $x$ is the full state $(u, \alpha)$ of the branch, and let $X(h)$ be the solution of $G = 0$ on the hyperplane $v^\top(X - x_k) = h$, found by `palc::correct` from $x_k + hv$ with the corrector tolerances. Then:
1. `lo = 0`, `hi = d`, with the counts $N_k$ and $N_{k+1}$.
2. At $m = (lo + hi)/2$ compute $X(m)$ and $N(m)$. If $N(m) = N(lo)$ then `lo = m`, otherwise `hi = m`. Stop when $hi - lo \le 10^{-12} d$ or after 60 halvings.
3. This gives one crossing, between `lo` and `hi`. If $N(hi) \ne N_{k+1}$, repeat from `hi` to $d$ with the counts $N(hi)$ and $N_{k+1}$. At most 16 crossings are taken from one step, and a warning says so when the cap is hit.

If `correct` fails at a trial, the step gets a warning naming it and no crossing, and the run goes on.

**Classification.** At the end of the bracket, look at the roots at the end with the larger count (`hi` when $N$ grows, `lo` when it falls) and take the one with the smallest positive real part.
- If $|\operatorname{Im}\lambda| > \omega_{\min} = 10^{-8}$, the crossing is a Hopf point, labelled `H`, with $\omega = |\operatorname{Im}\lambda|$ and the point at that end. $\omega_{\min}$ is a fixed constant of the method, not an option. A real root has $\operatorname{Im}\lambda$ at rounding level, far below it, but a pair that crosses with $\omega < 10^{-8}$ (a Bogdanov-Takens point) is reported as a real crossing.
- Otherwise a real root crosses zero. It is explained when the engine reported an LP or a BP at the same step (a `SpecialPoint` with `index = k + 1`), and the crossing gets no label of its own. If there is none, the run gets the warning "a real root crosses zero between points k and k+1 and no LP or BP is reported there".

**Limits.**
- A step on which one pair enters the right half-plane and another leaves it has $N_k = N_{k+1}$ and is not seen. `hmax` bounds this. Two crossings of different kinds in one step are separated by the bisection only when the count differs at the ends of every sub-interval.
- A subset of the roots is returned when `n` is too small (Section 8c), and the count is then too low without any message. A point of the branch or a bisection trial whose root set has candidates that did not converge (`unrefined`) is reported once per run, as one warning with the number of such points and the first one.
- Several roots on the axis at once (a codimension-2 point) are classified by the one with the smallest positive real part and the rest are not looked at.
- The first Lyapunov coefficient is not computed, so a Hopf point is not called sub- or supercritical.

**Engine API.** `dde::continue_equilibrium(model, setup, roots, opts, cancel)` takes the `&mut DdeModel`, an `EpSetup` as in `continue_ep` (`params` holds the user parameters only, and `free` indexes them), the `RootOptions` of Section 8c and the `Options` above. It returns `DdeBranch { points, specials, stop, warnings }`. A point is `{alpha, x, unstable}`. A special is `{label, alpha, x, omega, between, unstable_before, unstable_after}`, with `label` one of `"H"`, `"LP"` and `"BP"`, `omega` an `Option<f64>`, and `between` the indices of the two points that bracket the step. The bisection is the private function `bisect_crossings(trial, d, n_lo, n_hi)`, where `trial(h)` returns the count at $X(h)$ or `None` when `correct` fails. It returns the brackets plus two flags, `capped` and `failed_at`, and the outer function turns the flags into warnings that name the step. A test can drive it with a made-up count.

**Command line.** `pc-cli dde-branch <problem.json>` reads
```json
{"model": "path.ode", "params": {"tau": 1.0}, "free": "r", "x0": [1.0], "n": 30, "re_min": -2.0,
 "options": {"max_steps": 200, "hmax": 0.05, "bounds": [1.2, 2.0]}}
```
with `params` as in `dde-eq`. `free` is the name of a declared parameter, and the run starts at its value in `params` or, when `params` does not give it, in the model file. When `free` is a delay time, `options.bounds` must give a lower bound above 0. `options` takes the keys of the `cont` problem files (`h0`, `hmin`, `hmax`, `max_steps`, `tol_x`, `tol_f`, `max_newton`, `direction`, `bounds`, `detect.lp`, `detect.bp`) through the same reader; `detect.h` and `mesh_adapt` are `BadProblem`, since Hopf points come from the count and there is no mesh. `n`, `re_min` and `options` have no defaults except those of `Options`. The output is
```json
{"stop": "bounds", "points": [{"alpha": 1.2, "x": [1.0], "unstable": 0}],
 "specials": [{"label": "H", "alpha": 1.5707963267948966, "x": [1.0], "omega": 1.5707963267948966,
               "between": [20, 21], "unstable_before": 0, "unstable_after": 2}],
 "warnings": []}
```
Specials are sorted along the branch, by step and then by distance from the start of the step. `omega` is `null` for LP and BP, and for those `unstable_before` and `unstable_after` are the counts at the two ends of their step. Exit codes follow `dde-eq`: 2 for a bad problem file or a `BadProblem` error, 1 for a model error or a failed start, and the stop reasons as in `cont`.

**Acceptance tests.** Rust tests with closed forms. Every run pins `n`, `re_min`, a start value of $\alpha$ strictly inside its bounds on the low side of the crossing, and `direction`, so that the branch covers the values a test names. A claim of the form "$N = k$ below $\alpha^\ast$" is checked on every point of the branch with $\alpha < \alpha^\ast$. The counts in tests 1 to 3, 5 and 8 are those of an argument-principle count of $\Delta(\lambda)$ on the contour $\operatorname{Re}\lambda = 10^{-9}$, $|\operatorname{Im}\lambda| \le R$ with $R \ge 6$ (40 for test 8), written in a script that shares no code with the engine. Tests 4 and 7 use the counts of the same script at $x = -0.2$ and $x = -0.3$. The first run records the numbers in the test comment. Before the tests are written, `dde-eq` at the points of tests 1, 3, 4, 5 and 8 is checked for an empty `unrefined` with the `n` and `re_min` of the test.

1. Hutchinson `x' = r*x*(1-delay(x,tau))`, $\tau = 1$, `n = 30`, `re_min = -0.5`, free $r$ over $[1.2, 2.0]$, start $r = 1.3$ and $x = 1$, `direction = 1`, `hmax = 0.05`. One `H` at $r = \pi/2$ and $\omega = \pi/2$, each within $10^{-9}$, $x = 1$ within $10^{-10}$, with `unstable_before` 0 and `unstable_after` 2. Every point with $r < \pi/2$ has $N = 0$ and every point with $r > \pi/2$ has $N = 2$. The last point is at $r = 2$ and `stop` is `bounds`. No other special and no warning.
2. Hayes `x' = -x - 2*delay(x,tau)`, `n = 30`, `re_min = -0.5`, free $\tau$ over $[0.9, 1.6]$, start $\tau = 1$ and $x = 0$, `direction = 1`. One `H` at $\tau^\ast = 2\pi/(3\sqrt 3) = 1.209199576156145$ and $\omega = \sqrt 3$, within $10^{-9}$. $N = 0$ below $\tau^\ast$ and $N = 2$ above it. $x$ stays 0 on every point. No warning.
3. Sigmoid `x' = -x + a/(1+delay(x,tau)^2)`, $\tau = 2$, `n = 30`, `re_min = -0.5`, free $a$ over $[6.9, 7.9]$, start $a = 7$ and $x = 1.7$, `direction = 1`. One `H` at $a^\ast = 7.409583945668119$ with $x^\ast = 1.779031616618799$ and $\omega = 1.144464864051702$, each within $10^{-9}$. $N = 0$ below $a^\ast$ and $N = 2$ above it. The three constants come from solving the Hopf conditions with a bisection in a script of its own.
4. Fold `x' = mu - x*x - 0.5*delay(x,1)`, where $\partial G/\partial x = -2x - 0.5$ and the delayed coupling of $-0.5$ stays fixed. `n = 30`, `re_min = -0.5`, free $\mu$ over $[-0.07, -0.03]$, start $\mu = -0.04$ and $x = -0.1$, `direction = -1`. One `LP` at $\mu = -0.0625$ and $x = -0.25$ within $10^{-8}$, with `unstable_before` 0 and `unstable_after` 1. Every point with $x > -0.25$ has $N = 0$ and every point with $x < -0.25$ has $N = 1$. No `H` and no warning.
5. Delayed pitchfork `x' = mu*x - x*x*x - 0.5*delay(x,1)` on $x = 0$, `n = 30`, `re_min = -0.5`, free $\mu$ over $[0.3, 0.8]$, start $\mu = 0.35$, `direction = 1`. One `BP` at $\mu = 0.5$ within $10^{-8}$, with `unstable_before` 0 and `unstable_after` 1. Every point with $\mu < 0.5$ has $N = 0$ and every point with $\mu > 0.5$ has $N = 1$. No `H` and no warning.
6. Hutchinson as in test 1 with $r$ over $[0.5, 1.4]$ and start $r = 1$: no special, $N = 0$ at every point, `stop` is `bounds`, no warning.
7. The fold of test 4 with `detect.lp = false`: no special, $N$ goes from 0 to 1 along the branch, and the one warning that a real root crosses zero with no LP or BP reported.
8. Hutchinson as in test 1 with $r$ over $[1.2, 8.5]$, start $r = 1.3$, `n = 40`, `re_min = -0.5` and `h0 = hmax = 8`, so that a single step spans both crossings. The branch has exactly 2 points, the start and the landing on $r = 8.5$. Two `H` at $r = \pi/2$ and $r = 5\pi/2$ with $\omega = \pi/2$ and $5\pi/2$, each within $10^{-8}$, the first from 0 to 2 and the second from 2 to 4, and $N = 4$ at the last point.
9. `bisect_crossings` on made-up counts: a count that jumps at known values of $h$ gives brackets that contain them and are at most $10^{-12} d$ wide. A count with 20 jumps in one step gives 16 crossings and `capped` set. A `trial` that returns `None` ends the search for that crossing, sets `failed_at`, and gives no crossing for it; crossings already found in the step are kept.
10. `pc-cli dde-branch` on the data of test 1 prints one `H` with the values of test 1, `stop` is `bounds`, and every point has an `unstable` of 0 or 2. A model path is read relative to the problem file. A missing `free`, an unknown `free` name, `options.detect.h`, `options.mesh_adapt`, an unknown key, a `re_min` of 0 or above, a delay time as `free` without a lower bound above 0, a missing `model`, a wrong type and an unreadable problem file exit 2. An unreadable model, a model without a delay and a failed Newton start exit 1. `--help` and a missing argument behave as for `dde-eq`.

## 8f. The Hopf curve of delay equilibria in two parameters (phase E9a, step 4)

Step 4 takes a Hopf point of a delay equation, for example one that `dde-branch` reports, and continues it in two declared parameters. It reports the points of the curve where the Hopf frequency reaches zero (a Bogdanov-Takens point, `BT`) and where a real root crosses zero while the pair is on the axis (a zero-Hopf point, `ZH`). It does not compute a normal form or a root count along the curve, and it does not continue a fold or a periodic orbit. Without the option of Section 8g-2 it computes no Lyapunov coefficient either. It changes no C function and no MATLAB function, so the only ways in are `dde::continue_hopf` and `pc-cli dde-hopf`.

**What is not verified.** The base system, $\Delta(i\omega)v = 0$ with a linear normalization on $v$ and $f(x,\dots,x,\eta) = 0$, is the Hopf determining system of DDE-BIFTOOL, Sieber et al., arXiv 1406.7144 (BSD-2-Clause), Section 10.1, equation (14). I did not read how DDE-BIFTOOL or Knut treat the sign of $\omega^2$ or the normalization beyond that equation. The rewrite in $\kappa = \omega^2$, the real pair of rows $c^\top a = 1$, $c^\top b = 0$ and the labels are my own, derived below and checked against closed forms only (tests 2 to 5). No root count is made along the curve, so the engine does not check that the pair on the axis is the rightmost crossing, or that no other root has crossed the axis since the start. A Hopf curve of a delay equation can pass through points where a different pair is the one that matters, and the output does not say so. No oracle from DDE-BIFTOOL or Knut is used.

**The equation.** For a point $(x, p)$ of Section 8d let $A_0$ and $A_j$ be the matrices of `dde::linearize` and $\tau_j$ the delay times, so that $\Delta(\lambda) = \lambda I - A_0 - \sum_j A_j e^{-\lambda\tau_j}$. The delay times and the $A_j$ are read from the hidden-parameter columns of `JacP` at every call, as in Section 8d, so a delay time may be one of the two free parameters. For $\kappa = \omega^2$ define
$$c_\kappa(\tau) = \cos(\sqrt\kappa\,\tau),\quad s_\kappa(\tau) = \frac{\sin(\sqrt\kappa\,\tau)}{\sqrt\kappa} \quad (\kappa > 0),$$
$$c_\kappa(\tau) = \cosh(\sqrt{-\kappa}\,\tau),\quad s_\kappa(\tau) = \frac{\sinh(\sqrt{-\kappa}\,\tau)}{\sqrt{-\kappa}} \quad (\kappa < 0),$$
and $c_0(\tau) = 1$, $s_0(\tau) = \tau$. For $|\kappa\tau^2| < 10^{-8}$ the series $c_\kappa = 1 - \kappa\tau^2/2 + \kappa^2\tau^4/24$ and $s_\kappa = \tau(1 - \kappa\tau^2/6 + \kappa^2\tau^4/120)$ are used, so that both functions are analytic in $\kappa$ on the computer as well. Let
$$R(\kappa) = -A_0 - \sum_j A_j c_\kappa(\tau_j), \qquad \tilde S(\kappa) = I + \sum_j A_j s_\kappa(\tau_j).$$
For $\kappa > 0$ and $\omega = \sqrt\kappa$ this gives $\Delta(i\omega) = R + i\omega\tilde S$. With $v = a + i\omega b$, $a, b$ real, the condition $\Delta(i\omega)v = 0$ is, after taking real and imaginary parts and dividing the second by $\omega$,
$$R a - \kappa\tilde S b = 0, \qquad \tilde S a + R b = 0.$$
These two equations contain only $\kappa$, so they are the equations of the curve for every sign of $\kappa$. For $\kappa = -\mu^2 < 0$ one has $R \pm \mu\tilde S = \Delta(\pm\mu)$ and the equations say $\Delta(\mu)(a + \mu b) = 0$ and $\Delta(-\mu)(a - \mu b) = 0$. With the normalization below, a solution with $\kappa < 0$ has both $a + \mu b \ne 0$ and $a - \mu b \ne 0$, so both $\mu$ and $-\mu$ are characteristic roots (a neutral saddle). That part of the curve is not a bifurcation of the equilibrium. It is the continuation of the Hopf curve through $\kappa = 0$.

**The unknowns and the residual.** $x_{\rm ext} = (u, a, b, \alpha_1, \kappa, \alpha_2)$ with $u \in \mathbb R^n$, $a, b \in \mathbb R^n$ and two free parameters, so $3n + 3$ entries. The residual has $3n + 2$ rows:
- $G(u, p) = f(u, \ell(u), p)$, with $p$ holding $\alpha_1$ and $\alpha_2$ in their slots ($n$ rows),
- $R a - \kappa\tilde S b$ ($n$ rows) and $\tilde S a + R b$ ($n$ rows),
- $c^\top a - 1$ and $c^\top b$ (2 rows), with a fixed real vector $c$.

The last entry $\alpha_2$ is the entry that `Options::bounds` bounds. $\alpha_1$ and $\alpha_2$ are both free in the continuation. In the `Point` of the branch, `u` holds all entries but the last, and `alpha` is $\alpha_2$.

**The normalization.** Multiplying $v$ by $s_r + i\omega\sigma$ maps $(a, b)$ to $(s_r a - \kappa\sigma b,\ \sigma a + s_r b)$. The equations are unchanged by this map, and it is the whole freedom in $(a, b)$ when the kernel of $\Delta(i\omega)$ is simple. Its derivative at the identity has the determinant $(c^\top a)^2 + \kappa(c^\top b)^2 = |c^\top v|^2$ for $\kappa > 0$ and $(c^\top w_+)(c^\top w_-)$ for $\kappa < 0$ with $w_\pm = a \pm \mu b$. The rows $c^\top a = 1$, $c^\top b = 0$ fix this freedom, and they are regular as long as that determinant is not zero. $c$ is chosen once, at the start, as whichever of $\operatorname{Re} v_0$ and $\operatorname{Im} v_0$ of the start's null vector has the larger norm, and it is not changed along the curve. **Limit:** on a long curve the null vector can turn until $c^\top v$ reaches zero, or, for $\kappa < 0$, until one of $c^\top w_\pm$ does. The run then ends with `StepTooSmall` or `Singular` and the result up to that point stays valid. A rotating $c$ is not implemented.

**The Jacobian.** The residual is exact. Its Jacobian is computed by central differences in all $3n + 3$ columns, with the step $10^{-6}(1 + |x_j|)$, so that each step of the corrector costs $2(3n + 3)$ linearizations. The model has no second derivatives for a delay equation (`Dir` entries are not defined), so no analytic Jacobian exists. A linearization that fails (a value that is not finite) is `EngineError::NotFinite`, which the corrector treats as a failed step, so the step is halved. A delay time that becomes zero or negative is `BadProblem` from the parameter check of Section 8d, and the run stops with `StopReason::Error`.

**The start.** `HopfSetup { free: [usize; 2], params, x0, omega }`. `params` holds the user parameters, `free` the indices of the two free parameters, and the start value of $\alpha_1$ is `params[free[0]]` and that of $\alpha_2$ is `params[free[1]]`. `x0` is a guess of the equilibrium and `omega` of the Hopf frequency. The start does not have to be exact: it is polished.
1. The null vector $v$ is taken from the real $2n \times 2n$ embedding of $\Delta(i\omega)$ at $(x_0, p)$, with $a = \operatorname{Re} v$ and $b = \operatorname{Im} v/\omega$. $\kappa = \omega^2$. $c$ is chosen and $(a, b)$ is rescaled to $c^\top a = 1$, $c^\top b = 0$ by solving the $2 \times 2$ system for $(s_r, \sigma)$ above.
2. A Newton iteration with $\alpha_2$ held at `params[free[1]]` solves the $3n + 2$ equations in $(u, a, b, \alpha_1, \kappa)$ with a dense LU solve, the Jacobian above without its last column, and the `tol_x`, `tol_f` and `max_newton` of `Options`. A failure is `NewtonFailed` and a singular Jacobian is `Singular`.
3. The first tangent is the null vector of the full $(3n + 2) \times (3n + 3)$ Jacobian, as `start_tangent` of `curve2.rs` computes it, with its sign chosen so that its $\alpha_2$ component is positive when that component is larger than $10^{-8}$ in absolute value. Otherwise the sign rule of `start_tangent` applies. `Options::direction` $= -1$ reverses the tangent. A rank-deficient Jacobian is `BadProblem`.

The following are `BadProblem`, each with its own message: the two entries of `free` are equal or are not indices of user parameters, `x0` or `params` has the wrong length, $\omega$ is not finite or is at most $10^{-8}$, and $\alpha_2$ is a delay time while `Options::bounds` has no lower bound above 0. A start whose $\alpha_2$ lies outside the bounds returns a branch of the one start point with `stop = bounds`, as every continuation does.

**The labels.** Two codimension-2 tests are evaluated at every point, through the `codim2_tests` of the problem, and their sign changes are located and labelled by the code of `palc` and `locate` that the other curves use:
- `BT`: the test is $\kappa$. It changes sign where the frequency reaches zero.
- `ZH`: the test is $\det\Delta(0) = \det(-(A_0 + \sum_j A_j))$ by an LU factorization, and it is defined only for $\kappa > 0$ and is NaN otherwise, so that the zero at the BT point does not give a label.

`state_jacobian` returns `None`, `ep_tests` is false and no eigenvalues are stored. With `ep_tests` false the three standard tests are NaN whatever the `detect_*` options say, and a NaN on either side of a step skips that test, so only the two tests above are looked at. Generalized Hopf points are looked for only with the option of Section 8g-2, and double Hopf points are not looked for.

**Engine API.** `dde::continue_hopf(model, setup, opts, cancel)` takes the `&mut DdeModel`, the `HopfSetup`, the `Options` of Section 8 and the cancel flag. `Options::detect_lp`, `detect_bp`, `detect_h` and `mesh_adapt` have no meaning for this curve and are ignored. It returns `DdeHopfBranch { points, specials, stop, warnings }`. A point is `{alpha: [alpha1, alpha2], x, kappa, omega}` with `omega` the square root of $\kappa$ when $\kappa > 0$ and `None` otherwise, and `x` the equilibrium. A special is `{label, alpha, x, kappa, index}` with `label` `"BT"` or `"ZH"` and `index` the index of the point at or after the special. Specials are sorted along the branch.

**Command line.** `pc-cli dde-hopf <problem.json>` reads
```json
{"model": "path.ode", "params": {"tau": 1.0}, "free": ["r", "tau"], "x0": [1.0], "omega": 1.57,
 "options": {"max_steps": 400, "hmax": 0.05, "bounds": [0.5, 3.0]}}
```
`model`, `params`, `free`, `x0`, `omega` are required and an unknown key is an error, as in `dde-eq`. `free` is an array of two distinct names of declared parameters. `params` is read as in `dde-eq`, and a free parameter that `params` does not give takes the value of the model file. `options` is optional and takes the keys `h0`, `hmin`, `hmax`, `max_steps`, `tol_x`, `tol_f`, `max_newton`, `direction` and `bounds` through the same reader as `dde-branch`. `bounds` applies to the second name in `free`. `detect` and `mesh_adapt` are `BadProblem`, and there is no `n` or `re_min`. The output is
```json
{"stop": "bounds",
 "points": [{"alpha": [1.5707963267948966, 1.0], "x": [1.0], "kappa": 2.4674011002723395,
             "omega": 1.5707963267948966}],
 "specials": [],
 "warnings": []}
```
A special prints as `{"label": "BT", "alpha": [a1, a2], "x": [...], "kappa": 0.0, "index": 12}`. A point has `omega` `null` when $\kappa \le 0$. The start comes from the `H` special of `dde-branch`: its `alpha` is the value to give the branch parameter in `params`, and its `x` and `omega` are the `x0` and `omega` of the problem. Exit codes follow `dde-branch`: 2 for a bad problem file or a `BadProblem` error, 1 for a model error or a failed start, and the stop reasons as in `cont`. An `Error` stop still prints what was found and the message goes to stderr.

**Acceptance tests.** Rust tests with closed forms. The closed forms of the four models are the following; the numbers were computed by hand and recomputed in a numpy script.
- Hutchinson `x' = r*x*(1-delay(x,tau))` has the Hopf curve $r\tau = \pi/2$ with $x = 1$, $\omega = r$ and $\kappa = r^2$.
- Hayes `x' = -a*x - 2*delay(x,tau)` has, for $|a| < 2$, $\kappa = 4 - a^2$, $\cos(\sqrt\kappa\,\tau) = -a/2$ with $0 < \sqrt\kappa\,\tau < \pi$, and $x = 0$. At $a = 1$ it gives $\tau = 2\pi/(3\sqrt3) = 1.209199576156145$ and $\omega = \sqrt3$.
- The BT model `x' = c - a*x - b*delay(x,tau) - x*x` with $c = -0.25$ and $b = 1$ has, along its equilibria, $a + 2x = c/x + x - b =: a_{\rm eff}$. The Hopf curve is Hayes with $a_{\rm eff}$ and $b$: for $|a_{\rm eff}| < b$, $\kappa = b^2 - a_{\rm eff}^2$ and $\tau = \arccos(-a_{\rm eff}/b)/\sqrt\kappa$. For $a_{\rm eff} < -b$ the curve continues with $\kappa < 0$: with $s = \operatorname{arcosh}(-a_{\rm eff}/b)$, $\tau = s/(b\sinh s)$, $\mu = s/\tau = b\sinh s$ and $\kappa = -\mu^2$. The BT point is at $x = 1/2$, $(a, \tau) = (-2, 1)$, where $a_{\rm eff} = -b$ and $b\tau = 1$. Points $(x: a, \tau, \kappa)$: $0.8$: $-2.1125$, $1.20265$, $0.73734$. $0.6$: $-2.01667$, $1.06598$, $0.33306$. $0.4$: $-2.025$, $0.93116$, $-0.50063$. $0.3$: $-2.13333$, $0.85313$, $-1.35111$. $0.25$: $-2.25$, $0.80689$, $-2.0625$.
