At the start $x = 1$: $a = -2.25$, $\tau = 1.3613444250345883$, $\omega = \sqrt{0.9375} = 0.9682458365518543$.
- The ZH model `x' = -(a+y)*x - b*delay(x,tau)`, `y' = a - y*y` with $b = 1$ has $x = 0$ and $a = y^2$ on the equilibria that matter, the Hopf pair of the $x$ block with $a_{\rm eff} = a + y = y^2 + y$, $\kappa = 1 - a_{\rm eff}^2$, $\tau = \arccos(-a_{\rm eff})/\sqrt\kappa$, and the real root $-2y$ of the $y$ block. So $\det\Delta(0) = (a + y + b)\,2y$. The ZH point is at $y = 0$, $(a, \tau) = (0, \pi/2)$. At $y = -0.25$: $a = 0.0625$ and $\tau = 1.4071360574511174$. At $y = 0.25$: $\tau = 1.98819$.

Every test sets `tol_x = tol_f = 10^{-12}` and, where the start is polished, `max_newton = 20`.

1. Unit tests of the equation. $c_\kappa$ and $s_\kappa$ agree with $\cos, \sin$ and $\cosh, \sinh$ at $\kappa = \pm 0.7$ for $\tau = 1$ and $\tau = 2.5$ within $10^{-15}$. They are continuous at $\kappa = 0$, and the series and the closed forms agree within $10^{-15}$ across $|\kappa\tau^2| = 10^{-8}$ on both sides, for $\tau \in \{0.5, 1, 3\}$. For Hutchinson at the exact Hopf point $r = \pi/2$, $\tau = 1$, $x = 1$, $a = 1$, $b = 0$, $\kappa = r^2$ every row of the residual is below $10^{-13}$ in absolute value.
2. Hutchinson with free `["r", "tau"]`, start $r = 1.57$, $\tau = 1$, `x0 = [1.001]`, $\omega = 1.57$, `bounds = [0.5, 3]`, `hmax = 0.05`, for `direction` $= 1$ and $-1$. The polished start has $r = \pi/2$ within $10^{-10}$. On every point $r\tau = \pi/2$ within $10^{-8}$, $\kappa = r^2$ within $10^{-8}$, $x = 1$ within $10^{-8}$, and $\omega = r$ within $10^{-8}$. No special. With `direction` $= 1$ $\tau$ grows along the branch and `stop` is `bounds` with the last $\tau = 3$ within $10^{-8}$. With `direction` $= -1$ $\tau$ falls to $0.5$.
3. Hayes with free `["tau", "a"]`, $b = 2$ in the model, start $a = 1$, $\tau = 1.15$, `x0 = [0.0001]`, $\omega = 1.7$, `bounds = [-1.5, 1.5]`, for both directions. The polished start has $\tau = 1.209199576156145$ within $10^{-9}$. On every point $\kappa = 4 - a^2$ and $\cos(\sqrt\kappa\,\tau) = -a/2$ within $10^{-8}$, and $x = 0$ within $10^{-10}$. Both runs end at a bound of $a$, `stop` is `bounds`, and there is no special.
4. The BT model with free `["a", "tau"]`, start $a = -2.24$, $\tau = 1.3613444250345883$, `x0 = [1.001]`, $\omega = 0.97$, `bounds = [0.8, 1.5]`, `direction` $= -1$, `hmax = 0.05`. Exactly one special, `BT`, at $(a, \tau) = (-2, 1)$ and $x = 0.5$ within $10^{-8}$, with $|\kappa| < 10^{-8}$. No `ZH`. $\kappa$ changes sign exactly once along the points, at that special. Each point is checked in its own $x = u_0$, with $b = 1$ and $c = -0.25$: $a = c/x - b - x$ and $a_{\rm eff} = c/x + x - b$ within $10^{-8}$. For $\kappa > 0$, $\cos(\sqrt\kappa\,\tau) = -a_{\rm eff}/b$ and $\sqrt\kappa = b\sin(\sqrt\kappa\,\tau)$ within $10^{-7}$. For $\kappa < 0$, with $\mu = \sqrt{-\kappa}$, $\cosh(\mu\tau) = -a_{\rm eff}/b$ and $\mu = b\sinh(\mu\tau)$ within $10^{-7}$. The run ends with `stop = bounds` at $\tau = 0.8$ within $10^{-8}$, with $x$ between $0.2$ and $0.25$.
5. The ZH model with free `["a", "tau"]`, start $y = -0.25$ as `x0 = [0.001, -0.249]`, $a = 0.07$, $\tau = 1.4071360574511174$, $\omega = 0.98$, `bounds = [1.3, 2.0]`, `direction` $= 1$. The polished start has $a = 0.0625$ within $10^{-9}$. Exactly one special, `ZH`, at $(a, \tau) = (0, \pi/2)$ and $u = (0, 0)$ within $10^{-8}$. No `BT`. On every point $a = y^2$, $x = 0$, $\kappa = 1 - (a + y)^2$ and $\cos(\sqrt\kappa\,\tau) = -(a + y)$, each within $10^{-8}$. The run ends with `stop = bounds` at $\tau = 2$.
6. Start errors. Each of these gives `BadProblem`: `free = [0, 0]`, an index of `free` beyond the user parameters, a wrong length of `x0`, a wrong length of `params`, $\omega = 0$, $\omega = $ NaN, $\omega = 10^{-9}$, and $\tau$ as $\alpha_2$ with the default bounds or with `bounds = [0, 3]`. Hutchinson with the start of test 2 and `max_newton = 1` gives `NewtonFailed`, never a branch. A start at `x0 = [0]` on Hutchinson, where the linearization has no delay term, gives `NewtonFailed`, `Singular` or `NotFinite`, never a branch, and the test accepts any of the three. The polish passes a `NotFinite` from a linearization on unchanged. A start outside the bounds gives one point and `stop = bounds`.
7. `pc-cli dde-hopf` on the data of test 2 with `direction` 1 prints the points of test 2 and `stop` is `bounds`. A model path is read relative to the problem file. An unknown key, `options.detect`, `options.mesh_adapt`, a missing key, a name twice in `free`, a `free` that is not an array of two names, an unknown parameter name and an unreadable problem file exit 2. An unreadable model, a model without a delay (stderr has `error[ir:delay]`) and a start that fails in Newton's method exit 1. `--help` and a missing argument behave as for `dde-branch`.

## 8g. The first Lyapunov coefficient at a Hopf point of a delay equilibrium (phase E9a, step 5)

Step 5 takes a Hopf point of a delay equation (an equilibrium, its parameters and the frequency, for example an `H` special of `dde-branch` or a point of `dde-hopf`) and computes the first Lyapunov coefficient $\ell_1$. Its sign says whether the Hopf bifurcation is supercritical ($\ell_1 < 0$) or subcritical ($\ell_1 > 0$). It computes the number at one point. It does not look for a generalized Hopf point along a curve, does not compute the second coefficient (Section 8h adds it as an option), and does not continue a cycle. It changes no C function, no MATLAB function and no IR entry, so the ways in are `dde::lyapunov` and `pc-cli dde-lyap`.

**What is not verified.**
- The formulas are those of Bosschaert, Janssens and Kuznetsov, arXiv 1903.08276 v1, Sections 2.5, 6.1, 6.1.1 and 7. I read v1 only. The paper states the coefficient $c_1$ and prints $\ell_2 = \operatorname{Re} c_2/\omega_0$ for the generalized Hopf point. It does not print a formula for $\ell_1$ in terms of $c_1$. The step $\ell_1 = \operatorname{Re} c_1/\omega$ is my own, by analogy with $\ell_2$ and with the reduction to an ODE, and tests 1, 2 and 3 check it against three independent closed forms and a published value.
- The paper does not fix the scale of $q$. $c_1$ depends on it (see "The normalization"), so the value of $\ell_1$ depends on the scale and only its sign and the equation $\ell_1 = 0$ do not. I use $\|q\|_2 = 1$ because that is the default of DDE-BIFTOOL (`nmfm_nullvector.m`, `q_scale = false`), which I read for this one convention and for the formulas. I copied no code from it. I could not retrieve its LICENSE file, so there is no entry in `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md`, and the section does not rely on one.
- $\operatorname{Im} c_1$ has no oracle, and only $\operatorname{Re} c_1$ is tested.

**Inputs.** `dde::lyapunov(model, x, p, omega)`, with the `&mut DdeModel`, the equilibrium `x`, the declared parameters `p` and the Hopf frequency `omega`. The point is not polished: `x` and `omega` are taken as given, and the result reports how well they satisfy the equations. These are `BadProblem`: a wrong length of `x` or `p`, a delay time that is not finite and positive (the check of Section 8d), and $\omega$ not finite or at most $10^{-8}$ (the constant of Section 8e).

**The linear part.** With $A_0$, $A_j$ and $\tau_j$ from `dde::linearize` at $(x, p)$, put $\tau_0 = 0$, $M_0 = A_0$, $M_j = A_j$ and
$$\Delta(z) = zI - \sum_{j=0}^{m} M_j e^{-z\tau_j},\qquad \Delta'(z) = I + \sum_{j=0}^{m}\tau_j M_j e^{-z\tau_j}.$$
For $z = i\sigma$ the exponentials are $\cos\sigma\tau - i\sin\sigma\tau$. Four matrices are built: $\Delta(i\omega)$, $\Delta'(i\omega)$, $\Delta(2i\omega)$ and $\Delta(0)$. A complex matrix $C = C_r + iC_i$ is handled through the real embedding $\begin{pmatrix} C_r & -C_i \\ C_i & C_r\end{pmatrix}$ of size $2n$, and a complex solve is a real solve with it.

**The normalization.** $q \in \mathbb C^n$ and the row vector $p \in \mathbb C^{1\times n}$ satisfy
$$\Delta(i\omega)q = 0,\qquad p\,\Delta(i\omega) = 0,\qquad p\,\Delta'(i\omega)\,q = 1,\qquad \|q\|_2 = 1.$$
The product $p\,\Delta'q$ is bilinear: $p$ is not conjugated. $q$ is the right singular vector of the smallest singular value of the embedding of $\Delta(i\omega)$, read as a complex vector and scaled to unit norm. The vector $p^\top$ is the same for the embedding of $\Delta(i\omega)^\top$ (the transpose, not the conjugate transpose), scaled so that $p\,\Delta'q = 1$. The norm is the named constant `Q_NORM` $= 1$. A change $q \to \kappa q$ with a real $\kappa > 0$ maps $p \to p/\kappa$ and $c_1 \to \kappa^2 c_1$, and a phase $q \to e^{i\theta}q$, $p \to e^{-i\theta}p$ leaves $c_1$ unchanged, so the phase of the singular vector does not matter.

**The coefficient.** Let $\varphi(\theta) = e^{i\omega\theta}q$ for $\theta \in [-\tau_m, 0]$. The multilinear forms $B$ and $C$ are the second and third derivatives of $f$ with respect to the state and the lagged states, taken at the equilibrium, applied to the vectors $\Xi\psi = (\psi(0), \psi(-\tau_1), \dots, \psi(-\tau_m))$. For $\psi(\theta) = e^{s\theta}h$ the column of $\tau_j$ is $e^{-s\tau_j}h$. Then
$$H_{2000} = \Delta(2i\omega)^{-1}B(\varphi,\varphi),\qquad H_{1100} = \Delta(0)^{-1}B(\varphi,\bar\varphi),$$
where $H_{2000}(\theta) = e^{2i\omega\theta}H_{2000}$ and $H_{1100}$ is constant, and
$$c_1 = \tfrac12\,p\,\bigl(B(\bar\varphi, H_{2000}) + 2B(\varphi, H_{1100}) + C(\varphi,\varphi,\bar\varphi)\bigr),\qquad \ell_1 = \frac{\operatorname{Re} c_1}{\omega}.$$
For an ODE ($m = 0$) these are the formulas of Kuznetsov, *Elements of Applied Bifurcation Theory*, Section 3.5, with the different scale of $q$.

**The forms.** The model evaluates $D^r f[v_1, \dots, v_r]$ through `EntryKind::Dir(vec![Slot::Full; r])` on `model.eval`, for $r = 2$ and $3$. `Collapsed` of Section 8d refuses `Dir`, so the code calls `model.eval` directly. A direction of `Slot::Full` is $(dx, dp)$ with $dx \in \mathbb R^n$ and $dp \in \mathbb R^{n_p}$, and the parameters of the lowered model are the declared ones followed by one hidden slot per (state, delay time) pair. The direction of $\psi = e^{s\theta}h$ has $dx = h$, the entry of hidden slot $k$ equal to $h[\text{state}_k]\,e^{-s\tau_k}$, and zero for the declared parameters. The parameters passed are the full vector of `full_params` (declared values, then $x[\text{state}_k]$ per slot, then $t = 0$). A complex argument $a = a_r + ia_i$ is split, so that a form of $r$ complex arguments is the sum over the $2^r$ choices of real or imaginary parts, each multiplied by $i^k$ with $k$ the number of imaginary parts. A `Dir` that returns a value that is not finite is `NotFinite`.

**The checks and the errors.** No new `EngineError` variant.
- The relative null residual $\|\Delta(i\omega)q\|_2 / s$, with $s = \omega + \|A_0\|_F + \sum_j \|A_j\|_F$ (the scale $s(\lambda)$ of section 8c at $\lambda = i\omega$), must be at most `NULL_RESIDUAL_MAX` $= 10^{-4}$. The scale is not $\|\Delta(i\omega)\|_F$, because $\Delta(i\omega)$ is a zero matrix up to rounding for a scalar equation at a Hopf point, and a ratio against it is about 1 there. Otherwise the result is `BadProblem("omega is not a Hopf frequency ...")`, since the coefficient is meaningless there. The constant is my choice and an owner item. At the published FHN point of test 3 the ratio is $4 \cdot 10^{-7}$, because the published parameters have four digits.
- The null space of $\Delta(i\omega)$ must have dimension 1. With $\sigma_1 \ge \dots \ge \sigma_n$ the singular values of $\Delta(i\omega)$, the ratio $\sigma_{n-1}/\sigma_1$ must be at least `RESONANCE_MIN` $= 10^{-8}$ (the test is skipped for $n = 1$), else `Singular` with the text "null space of dimension 2 or more". The real embedding repeats each singular value, so the code reads the ratio from the embedding. This test comes before the pairing test, because a semisimple double root passes the pairing test: for two identical oscillators at $\omega$, $\Delta' = I$ and the pairing of any null vector with its left partner is not 0, while $c_1$ depends on which null vector is taken. The threshold has no source.
- $|p\,\Delta'q|$ before the scaling must be at least `PAIRING_MIN` $= 10^{-8}$ (the pair is not a simple root), else `Singular`.
- The smallest singular value over the largest one of the embedding of $\Delta(0)$ and of $\Delta(2i\omega)$ must be at least `RESONANCE_MIN` $= 10^{-8}$, else `Singular` naming which of the two matrices it is. A zero root is a zero-Hopf point and $2i\omega$ a root is a 1:2 resonance. $\ell_1$ is not defined at either.
- The residual $\|f(x,\dots,x,p)\|_\infty$ is returned and not checked.

**Result.** `Lyapunov { omega, c1: [re, im], l1, null_residual, equilibrium_residual }`, with `l1` $= $ `c1[0]` $/$ `omega`.

**Command line.** `pc-cli dde-lyap <problem.json>` reads
```json
{"model": "path.ode", "params": {"a": 1.5707963267948966}, "x0": [0.0], "omega": 1.5707963267948966}
```
All four of these keys are required (Section 8h adds an optional fifth key, `l2`) and an unknown key is an error. `params` is read as in `dde-hopf`: a declared parameter that it leaves out keeps the value of the model file. `x0` and `omega` are used as given and are not polished. The output is
```json
{"c1": [-0.3363560664247413, -0.41413092244177396], "equilibrium_residual": 0.0,
 "l1": -0.2141309224417739, "null_residual": 3.1e-17, "omega": 1.5707963267948966}
```
with `null_residual` a few $10^{-17}$ for this model (the digits depend on rounding).
Exit codes follow `dde-hopf`: 2 for a bad problem file or a `BadProblem` error, 1 for a model error or any other engine error. The model is built with the JIT, so the CLI test also runs `Dir` through compiled code.

**Acceptance tests.** Rust tests. The closed forms were recomputed in a numpy script written for this section from the formulas above, without the Rust code.
- Wright's equation `u' = -a*delay(u,1)*(1+u)`: $f = -a\,u(t-1)(1 + u)$, so $n = 1$, $D_1 = (0, -a)$, $D_2[0,1] = D_2[1,0] = -a$ and $D_3 = 0$ in the flat arguments $(u, u(t-1))$. At $a = \omega = \pi/2$, $x = 0$: $\ell_1 = 2(2 - 3\pi)/(5(4 + \pi^2)) = -0.21413092244177392$ and $c_1 = -0.33635606642474136 - 0.41413092244177396\,i$.
- Polar `x' = -w*y + a*x*(x^2+y^2)`, `y' = w*x + a*y*(x^2+y^2)`, with a decoupled third state `z' = -2*z - delay(z,1)` so that the model has a delay and $\Delta(0)$, $\Delta(2i\omega)$ are regular. With $\omega = 1.3$ and $a = 0.37$: $c_1 = 2a = 0.74$, $\ell_1 = 2a/\omega = 0.5692307692307692$ at $\|q\|_2 = 1$. (The textbook $\ell_1 = a$ is for $p\cdot q = 1$ with the other scale of $q$, and a test must say which scale it means.)
- FitzHugh-Nagumo with delayed feedback, from Example 8.1 and equation (100) of arXiv 1903.08276: `u1' = -u1^3/3 + (c+alpha)*u1^2 + d*u1 - u2 + 2*beta*tanh(delay(u1,tau))`, `u2' = eps*(u1 - b*u2)`, with $b = 0.9$, $\varepsilon = 0.08$, $c = 2.0528$, $d = -3.2135$, $\tau = 1.7722$, $\beta = 1.9$. The equilibrium is $0$ and $\alpha$ enters only $D_2$, so the Hopf point has the same $\omega$ for every $\alpha$. In the flat arguments $(u_1, u_2, u_1(t-\tau), u_2(t-\tau))$: $D_1[0,\cdot] = (d, -1, 2\beta, 0)$, $D_1[1,\cdot] = (\varepsilon, -\varepsilon b, 0, 0)$, $D_2[0,0,0] = 2(c+\alpha)$, $D_3[0,0,0,0] = -2$, $D_3[0,2,2,2] = -4\beta$. The Hopf frequency, from a minimum of the smallest singular value of $\Delta(i\omega)$, is $\omega = 0.07199637889093541$, where that value is $2.9\cdot 10^{-6}$ because the published parameters are rounded to four or five digits.

Every test uses `Evaluator::Fallback` unless it says otherwise, and the CLI test uses the JIT.

1. Wright at $a = \pi/2$: $\ell_1$ within $10^{-9}$ of the closed form, $\operatorname{Re} c_1$ within $10^{-9}$ of $\omega\ell_1$, the normalization residuals ($\|p\,\Delta(i\omega)\|$, $|p\,\Delta'q - 1|$, $\|\Delta(i\omega)q\|$) below $10^{-12}$ in a test that rebuilds them from the returned values, and $\operatorname{Im} c_1$ within $10^{-9}$ of the numpy value. The test also runs the same model with the JIT.
2. Polar with the decoupled state: $c_1 = 0.74$ within $10^{-12}$ in the real part and $0$ within $10^{-12}$ in the imaginary part, $\ell_1 = 0.5692307692307692$ within $10^{-12}$.
3. FHN at $\alpha = -0.9710$ and $\omega = 0.07199637889093541$: $\ell_1 = 0.398075831491134$ within $10^{-8}$ (the numpy value), and within a relative $10^{-3}$ of the published $0.3980$. The derivative of $\ell_1$ in $\omega$ is about $86$, so the test gives $\omega$ to all its digits. At $\omega = 0.07199638$ the value is $0.3980759264$. The sign of $\ell_1$ in $\alpha$: negative at $\alpha = -1.0430$ ($-3.12\cdot 10^{-4}$) and positive at $-1.0428$ ($7.56\cdot 10^{-4}$), with a root at $\alpha = -1.042941555598$ at $\omega = 0.07199638$, found by bisection in the test within $10^{-6}$.
4. The scale. An internal function takes the scale of $q$ as an argument and the public function passes $Q_{\rm NORM}$. For Wright and FHN, $c_1$ at scale $s \in \{0.5, 2\}$ is $s^2 c_1$ within $10^{-12}$ relative, and rotating the phase of $q$ and $p$ (the test builds both from the vector it rotates) leaves $c_1$ unchanged within $10^{-12}$ relative.
5. The forms. On the FHN model, $B(h, h)$ and $C(h, h, h)$ for a real $h$ equal the central second and third differences of `F` along the direction $(h, h_{\rm slots})$ within a relative $10^{-5}$ (step $10^{-3}$), $B(a,b) = B(b,a)$ for complex $a$, $b$, $B(\varphi,\bar\varphi)$ has an imaginary part below $10^{-14}$, and a model whose $D_3$ is zero (Wright) gives $C = 0$.
6. Errors. Each of these gives the named error: $\omega = 1.2$ on Wright at $a = \pi/2$ (`BadProblem`, null residual); a wrong length of `x` or `p`; $\omega = 0$, NaN and $10^{-9}$ (`BadProblem`); $\tau$ not positive (`BadProblem`); the zero-Hopf model of Section 8f test 5 at $(a, \tau) = (0, \pi/2)$, $x = 0$, $y = 0$, $\omega = 1$ (`Singular` from $\Delta(0)$); a 1:2 resonance, the linear system $x_1' = -y_1$, $y_1' = x_1$, $x_2' = -2y_2$, $y_2' = 2x_2$, $z' = -2z - z(t-1)$ with a cubic term $x_1^3$ added to the first row so that the forms are not zero, at $\omega = 1$ (`Singular` from $\Delta(2i\omega)$); and a semisimple double root, the polar model of test 2 with a second oscillator $e_0' = -\omega e_1$, $e_1' = \omega e_0$ at the same $\omega = 1.3$ (`Singular` with "null space of dimension 2 or more"). A Jordan block at $i\omega$ (four states, `x' = -w*y + u`, `y' = w*x + v`, `u' = -w*v`, `v' = w*u` with $w = 1.3$, and the decoupled `z`) is `Singular` from the pairing, with "not simple" in the message and not the dimension text, so each of the two tests is seen to fire alone.
7. `pc-cli dde-lyap` on the data of test 1 prints $\ell_1$ within $10^{-9}$ of the closed form. A model path is read relative to the problem file. An unknown key, a missing key, a wrong type, `omega` as a string and an unreadable problem file exit 2. An `omega` that is not a Hopf frequency exits 2. An unreadable model and a model without a delay (stderr has `error[ir:delay]`) exit 1. `--help` and a missing argument behave as for `dde-hopf`.

## 8g-2. The first Lyapunov coefficient along a Hopf curve (phase E9a, step 5, part 2)

Part 2 adds an option to the Hopf curve of Section 8f. With it, the run computes $\ell_1$ of Section 8g at every point with $\kappa > 0$ and labels a sign change of $\ell_1$ as `GH`, a generalized Hopf point. It changes no C function and no MATLAB function. The ways in are `dde::continue_hopf_with_lyapunov` and the key `lyapunov` of `pc-cli dde-hopf`. Without the option nothing changes: `dde::continue_hopf` is the function of Section 8f, and a problem file without the key, or with `"lyapunov": false`, prints the output of Section 8f byte for byte.

**What is not verified.**
- $\ell_1$ has the status of Section 8g: the step from $c_1$ to $\ell_1$ and the scale $\|q\|_2 = 1$ are conventions that Section 8g explains. A zero of $\ell_1$ does not depend on the scale, so the position of `GH` does not, and the sign of $\ell_1$ at a point does not either. The value of $\ell_1$ at a point does.
- A `GH` label is a sign change of $\ell_1$ between two points of the curve. Part 2 does not compute the second coefficient $\ell_2$ (Section 8h adds it at a `GH` special), so by itself it does not say whether the point is a generalized Hopf point in the sense of Bosschaert, Janssens and Kuznetsov (that needs $\ell_2 \ne 0$). A zero of $\ell_1$ at which $\ell_2 = 0$ is not told apart from one at which it is not.
- $\ell_1$ is not defined at $\kappa \le 0$, where the curve has no pair on the axis, and `GH` is not looked for there.
- The function of Section 8g refuses a null space of dimension 2 or more at $i\omega$. The curve test takes every refusal of `lyapunov` as NaN, whatever the error, so at such a point the test is NaN and not an arbitrary number. A run does not stop there, because the curve code drops the error (`l1_at` in `hopf.rs`). No test runs a curve through a double root. Whether a refusal inside a run should abort it is a design choice and an owner item.
- Poles. $\ell_1$ has a pole where $\Delta(0)$ is singular (a `ZH` point), where $\kappa \to 0$ (a `BT` point), and where $\Delta(2i\omega)$ is singular (a 1:2 resonance, a root at $2i\omega$). A sign change through a pole is not a zero. The locator already drops it: a codimension-2 zero counts only if its value is at most `POLE_TOL` $= 10^{-2}$ times the smaller end value of the step (`locate.rs`, written for the first Lyapunov coefficient at a zero-Hopf point). The constant has no source and is an owner item, as in Section 8e. Tests 4 and 5 check the pole at `BT` and at `ZH` on the models of Section 8f. A 1:2 resonance on a curve has no test, so the filter is trusted there on the same argument and no more.
- The cost. Each evaluation of $\ell_1$ builds four complex matrices and factors several real matrices of size $2n$ (Section 8g). It is computed at every point of the curve and again at every trial of the locator, so a run with the option takes several times as long as one without. The step of the curve is not changed to follow $\ell_1$.

**The test.** `continue_hopf_with_lyapunov` has the signature of `continue_hopf`. The problem of Section 8f gets a third codimension-2 test and a third label, so that `codim2_labels` is `["BT", "ZH", "GH"]` and `codim2_tests` returns $(\kappa, \det\Delta(0), \ell_1)$. The third entry is `dde::lyapunov` at $(u, p, \sqrt\kappa)$ with $p$ the declared parameters of the point, taken from the first $n$ entries of the state of the curve and from $\alpha_1$ and $\alpha_2$. It is NaN where $\kappa$ is NaN or at most 0, and also where `lyapunov` returns an error, whatever the error is (a null residual above `NULL_RESIDUAL_MAX`, a singular $\Delta(0)$ or $\Delta(2i\omega)$, a pairing below `PAIRING_MIN`, or a failed linearization). A NaN on either side of a step skips the test, as in Section 8f, so the label is only given where $\ell_1$ is defined at both ends of a step. A run in which `lyapunov` fails at every point has no `GH` and no warning. The point of the branch gets `l1: Option<f64>`, `Some` where the value is finite and `None` otherwise, and it is `None` at every point of a run without the option. A special carries the label `GH`, the point of the curve at the zero, and `index` as in Section 8f. The other two labels, the curve, the first tangent and the stop reason are those of Section 8f, because the extra test enters neither the residual nor the step.

**Command line.** `pc-cli dde-hopf` takes one more optional key, `lyapunov`, a boolean that is false when the key is left out. Any other type is exit 2 with `"lyapunov"` in the message. With `true` every point of the output has `"l1"`, a number, or `null` where $\ell_1$ is not defined, and a special may have the label `"GH"`. With `false` or without the key the output has no `"l1"` and is the output of Section 8f.

**Acceptance tests.** The model is Wright's equation with a cubic term, `u' = -a*delay(u,1)*(1+u) + b*u^3`, $n = 1$, with free `["a", "b"]`. The origin is an equilibrium for every $(a, b)$ and the cubic term does not touch the linearization, so the Hopf curve is the line $a = \pi/2$, $\omega = \pi/2$, $\kappa = \pi^2/4$ for every $b$. In the notation of Section 8g the cubic term adds $3b\,p\,q^2\bar q = 3b/\Delta'(i\omega)$ to $c_1$, with $\Delta'(i\omega) = 1 + i\pi/2$ at $\|q\|_2 = 1$ (here $p\,\Delta' q = 1$ gives $p\,q = 1/\Delta'$, and $|q| = 1$ makes $q^2\bar q = q$). So on the curve
$$\ell_1(b) = \ell_1(0) + s\,b,\qquad s = \frac{6}{\pi(1 + \pi^2/4)},\qquad \ell_1(0) = -0.21413092244177392,$$
and the generalized Hopf point is at $b^* = -\ell_1(0)/s = 0.38875\ldots$. $\ell_1(0)$ is the value of test 1 of Section 8g. The slope was derived by hand and not by the code. Every engine test runs with the JIT and with `Evaluator::Fallback`. The start is $a = 1.57$, $b = 0$, `x0 = [0.001]`, $\omega = 1.57$, with `hmax = 0.05`, `bounds = [-1, 1]` (on $b$), `tol_x = tol_f = 10^{-12}` and `max_newton = 20`.

1. $\ell_1$ along the curve. For `direction` $= 1$, `stop` is `bounds` and there are more than 20 points. On every point $a = \pi/2$ and $\kappa = \pi^2/4$ within $10^{-8}$, and `l1` is `Some` and agrees with $\ell_1(0) + s\,b$ within $10^{-7}$.
2. The `GH` point. The run of test 1 has exactly one special, `GH`, with $b$ within $10^{-6}$ of $b^*$, $a$ within $10^{-8}$ of $\pi/2$, $\kappa$ within $10^{-8}$ of $\pi^2/4$ and $|x| \le 10^{-8}$. The `l1` of the point before the special is negative and that of the point at its `index` is positive.
3. The other direction. Start at $b = 0.9$ with `direction` $= -1$ (JIT only). Exactly one special, `GH`, with $b$ within $10^{-6}$ of $b^*$.
4. Off equals absent. The run of test 1 with `continue_hopf` has no special and `l1` is `None` at every point. `continue_hopf_with_lyapunov` gives the same number of points, and the same `alpha`, `x` and `kappa` bit for bit, and the same warnings.
5. No spurious `GH` at the poles. The `BT` run of test 4 of Section 8f and the `ZH` run of its test 5, each with the option and without, each with both evaluators. The label list is `["BT"]` and `["ZH"]` in both cases, the number of points and the `index` of the special are equal, `alpha[0]` of the special differs by at most $10^{-10}$, and `l1` is `None` wherever $\kappa \le 0$.
6. `pc-cli dde-hopf` on the Wright cubic model with `"lyapunov": true` (JIT). Every point has a numeric `"l1"` within $10^{-7}$ of $\ell_1(0) + s\,b$, and exactly one special, `GH`, at $b^*$ within $10^{-6}$ and $a = \pi/2$ within $10^{-8}$.
7. `pc-cli dde-hopf` on the same file with `"lyapunov": false` and without the key prints the same text, with no `"l1"` on any point and no special. `"lyapunov": 1` exits 2 and the message names `"lyapunov"`. A unit test of the problem reader refuses `1`, `"yes"`, `null` and `[true]`.

## 8h. The second Lyapunov coefficient at a generalized Hopf point (phase E9a, step 6)

Step 6 computes the second Lyapunov coefficient $\ell_2$ at a point where the first one vanishes. Its sign says whether the generalized Hopf point is of the kind where the Hopf curve turns the fold of cycles one way or the other. It computes the number at one point and adds it to the `GH` special of Section 8g-2. It does not continue a fold of cycles, does not compute the other normal-form coefficients of the generalized Hopf point, and does not test for the nondegeneracy $\ell_2 \ne 0$ beyond returning the value. It changes no C function, no MATLAB function and no IR entry, so the ways in are `dde::lyapunov2`, the key `l2` of `pc-cli dde-lyap` and the key `l2` of the `GH` specials of `pc-cli dde-hopf`.

**What is not verified.**
- The formulas are those of Bosschaert, Janssens and Kuznetsov, arXiv 1903.08276 v1, Sections 2.5, 6.1.1 and 7, with Lemma 4 (equation 27) for $H_{2100}$. I read v1 only. I checked the list of terms of $c_2$ against my own enumeration of the set partitions of the five arguments, and the value of $\ell_2$ for FHN against the published $-15.6733$ (test 2). The printed list lost some overbars in the text I read, and the enumeration decides them.
- $c_2$, and so $\ell_2 = \operatorname{Re} c_2/\omega$, is a normal-form coefficient only where $\operatorname{Re} c_1 = 0$. The terms $H_{2200}$ and $H_{3100}$ assume it. The function does not refuse a point with $\operatorname{Re} c_1 \ne 0$. It returns the numbers that the formulas give, together with `c1` and `l1`, and the caller reads `l1` first. At such a point $\ell_2$ has no meaning.
- The scale. $c_2$ scales with $|q|^4$ and $\ell_2$ is therefore tied to $\|q\|_2 = 1$ as $\ell_1$ is (Section 8g). Its sign does not depend on the scale. The value of the published FHN number, taken from the paper, is for the scale of DDE-BIFTOOL, and the agreement in test 2 is also a check of that convention.
- $\operatorname{Im} c_2$ has no invariant meaning (test 4 shows it changes under a change of coordinates) and is returned only as a diagnostic.
- Constants. `NULL_RESIDUAL_MAX`, `PAIRING_MIN` and `RESONANCE_MIN` are those of Section 8g and have no source. The check of $\Delta(3i\omega)$ uses `RESONANCE_MIN` too.

**Inputs.** `dde::lyapunov2(model, x, p, omega)` has the arguments and the `BadProblem` cases of `dde::lyapunov` (Section 8g).

**The linear part.** As in Section 8g, with one more matrix, $\Delta''(z) = -\sum_{j}\tau_j^2 M_j e^{-z\tau_j}$, and with $\Delta(3i\omega)$. The pair $q$, $p$ and the scale are those of Section 8g.

**The vectors.** Write $B$, $C$, $D$ and $E$ for the multilinear forms of orders 2, 3, 4 and 5 (the derivatives of $f$ with respect to the state and the lagged states), applied to the vectors $\Xi\psi$ of Section 8g, and $\varphi(\theta) = e^{i\omega\theta}q$. $H_{2000}$, $H_{1100}$ and $c_1$ are those of Section 8g, and
$$H_{3000} = \Delta(3i\omega)^{-1}\bigl(3B(\varphi, H_{2000}) + C(\varphi,\varphi,\varphi)\bigr),\qquad F_{21} = B(\bar\varphi, H_{2000}) + 2B(\varphi, H_{1100}) + C(\varphi,\varphi,\bar\varphi).$$
$H_{2100}$ is not a pure exponential. With $\kappa = -2c_1$, let $\xi$ solve $\Delta(i\omega)\xi = F_{21} + \kappa\Delta'(i\omega)q$ (the right side is in the range of $\Delta(i\omega)$ because $pF_{21} = 2c_1$ and $p\Delta'q = 1$), $\gamma = -p\,\Delta'(i\omega)\xi + \tfrac12\kappa\,p\,\Delta''(i\omega)q$ and $v_0 = \xi + \gamma q$. Then
$$H_{2100}(\theta) = e^{i\omega\theta}\bigl(v_0 + 2c_1\theta\,q\bigr).$$
The solve for $\xi$ uses the border column $p^H$ and the border row $q^H$, that is the system $\begin{pmatrix}\Delta(i\omega) & p^H\\ q^H & 0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\xi\\ s\end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix}F_{21} + \kappa\Delta'q\\ 0\end{pmatrix}$. It is nonsingular for a simple root because $q^Hq \ne 0$ and $p\,p^H \ne 0$, which is not true of the border $(q, p)$ when $p\,q = 0$. $v_0$ does not depend on the border, because $\gamma$ is defined by the pairing condition: with $\psi(s) = p\,e^{-i\omega s}$ and the Hale pairing $\langle\psi,\phi\rangle = \psi(0)\phi(0) + \sum_j\int_{-\tau_j}^{0}\psi(\xi+\tau_j)M_j\phi(\xi)\,d\xi$, one finds $\langle\psi, H_{2100}\rangle = p\,\Delta'v_0 + c_1\,p\,\Delta''q$, and the choice of $\gamma$ makes it zero. The delayed value of $H_{2100}$ in the column of $\tau_j$ is $e^{-i\omega\tau_j}(v_0 - 2c_1\tau_j\,q)$. Then, with $\Delta(0)$ and $\Delta(2i\omega)$ checked as in Section 8g and $\Delta(3i\omega)$ checked in the same way,
$$H_{2200} = \Delta(0)^{-1}\Bigl[2B(\bar\varphi, H_{2100}) + 2B(\varphi, \bar H_{2100}) + B(\bar H_{2000}, H_{2000}) + 2B(H_{1100}, H_{1100}) + C(\varphi,\varphi,\bar H_{2000}) + 4C(\varphi,\bar\varphi,H_{1100}) + C(\bar\varphi,\bar\varphi,H_{2000}) + D(\varphi,\varphi,\bar\varphi,\bar\varphi)\Bigr],$$
a constant. With
$$F_{31} = B(\bar\varphi, H_{3000}) + 3B(\varphi, H_{2100}) + 3B(H_{1100}, H_{2000}) + 3C(\varphi,\bar\varphi,H_{2000}) + 3C(\varphi,\varphi,H_{1100}) + D(\varphi,\varphi,\varphi,\bar\varphi),$$
let $v_{31} = \Delta(2i\omega)^{-1}\bigl[F_{31} - 6c_1\Delta'(2i\omega)h_{2000}\bigr]$, where $h_{2000} = H_{2000}(0)$. Then $H_{3100}(\theta) = e^{2i\omega\theta}(v_{31} + 6c_1\theta\,h_{2000})$, with the delayed value $e^{-2i\omega\tau_j}(v_{31} - 6c_1\tau_j h_{2000})$. Finally
$$c_2 = \tfrac1{12}\,p\Bigl[\begin{aligned}[t] &2B(\bar\varphi,H_{3100}) + B(\bar H_{2000},H_{3000}) + 3B(\varphi,H_{2200}) + 6B(H_{1100},H_{2100}) + 3B(\bar H_{2100},H_{2000})\\ &+ C(\bar\varphi,\bar\varphi,H_{3000}) + 6C(\varphi,\bar\varphi,H_{2100}) + 6C(\bar\varphi,H_{1100},H_{2000}) + 3C(\varphi,\bar H_{2000},H_{2000})\\ &+ 3C(\varphi,\varphi,\bar H_{2100}) + 6C(\varphi,H_{1100},H_{1100})\\ &+ 3D(\varphi,\bar\varphi,\bar\varphi,H_{2000}) + 6D(\varphi,\varphi,\bar\varphi,H_{1100}) + D(\varphi,\varphi,\varphi,\bar H_{2000})\\ &+ E(\varphi,\varphi,\varphi,\bar\varphi,\bar\varphi)\end{aligned}\Bigr],\qquad \ell_2 = \frac{\operatorname{Re} c_2}{\omega}.$$
For $m = 0$ the delays drop out and these are the formulas of Kuznetsov, *Elements of Applied Bifurcation Theory*, Section 8.3, with the scale of $q$ of Section 8g. $\ell_2 = \operatorname{Re} c_2/\omega$ is the paper's expression.

**The forms.** As in Section 8g, with $r = 4$ and $5$ added: `EntryKind::Dir(vec![Slot::Full; r])` for $r$ up to 5 (the check of `pc-ir` allows order 5). A direction with a delayed value that is not a pure exponential carries the explicit delayed values above in the hidden slots.

**The checks and the errors.** Those of Section 8g, which include the refusal of a null space of dimension 2 or more at $i\omega$, and $\Delta(3i\omega)$ with the same ratio: `Singular` naming `Delta(3 i omega)` (a 1:3 resonance, where $c_2$ is not defined). The order of the checks is $\Delta(0)$, $\Delta(2i\omega)$, $\Delta(3i\omega)$.

**Result.** `Lyapunov2 { omega, c1: [re, im], c2: [re, im], l1, l2, null_residual, equilibrium_residual }` with `l1` $= \operatorname{Re} c_1/\omega$ and `l2` $= \operatorname{Re} c_2/\omega$.

**Command line.**
- `pc-cli dde-lyap` takes one more optional key, `l2`, a boolean that is false when the key is left out. Any other type is exit 2 with `"l2"` in the message. With `false` the output is the same as without the key, and it has the five keys of Section 8g. With `true` the keys, in alphabetical order, are `c1`, `c2`, `equilibrium_residual`, `l1`, `l2`, `null_residual` and `omega`.
- `pc-cli dde-hopf` with `"lyapunov": true` gives a `GH` special one more key, `"l2"`: the value of $\ell_2$ at the point of the special (the located zero of $\ell_1$, with the parameters and the state that the special carries), or `null` where `lyapunov2` returns an error. The other specials have no `"l2"`. Without `lyapunov` the output is that of Section 8f. `dde::continue_hopf_with_lyapunov` gives `DdeHopfSpecial` a field `l2: Option<f64>`, `Some` only for `GH`. The located zero is where the locator stopped, so $\operatorname{Re} c_1$ there is small and not zero (test 12 sets the size), and the value of $\ell_2$ differs from that at the exact zero by the same order. The code does not polish the point further.

**Acceptance tests.** The closed forms below were recomputed in a numpy script written for this section from the formulas above, with its own set-partition check of the term list, without the Rust code. The engine tests run the JIT and `Evaluator::Fallback` unless a test says otherwise.

1. Wright with a cubic and a quintic term, `u' = -a*delay(u,1)*(1+u) + b*u^3 + c*u^5` at $a = \omega = \pi/2$, $x = 0$. The origin is an equilibrium and the added terms do not touch the linearization, so $\omega = \pi/2$ for every $b$ and $c$, and $c_1(b)$ is linear in $b$. The root of $\operatorname{Re} c_1$ is $b^* = 0.3887604649348081$, where $c_1 = -3\pi i/10 = -0.942477796076938\,i$ and $|\operatorname{Re} c_1| < 10^{-15}$. At $b^*$: for $c = 0$, $c_2 = -0.15676488850186443 - 1.9754621004742934\,i$ and $\ell_2 = -0.0997996276332862$; for $c = 1$, $c_2 = 2.7272395029181444 - 6.505645604977195\,i$ and $\ell_2 = 1.7362145915396248$. Each within $10^{-9}$ relative, with both evaluators. The test also checks `l1` within $10^{-12}$ of $0$.
2. FitzHugh-Nagumo of Section 8g, $\beta = 1.9$, with the derivatives $D_4 = 0$ and $E[0,2,2,2,2,2] = 32\beta$ (since $\tanh''' (0) = -2$ and $\tanh^{(5)}(0) = 16$). The published parameters are rounded, and at $\beta = 1.9$ the smallest singular value of $\Delta(i\omega)$ is $2.9\cdot10^{-6}$ at best (Section 8g). Two correct implementations that pick the null vector differently then differ by a relative $7\cdot10^{-7}$ in $c_2$, so the sharp test point is the generalized Hopf point on the Hopf curve, found by Newton on $\det\Delta(i\omega) = 0$ in $(\omega, \beta)$ and a secant step on $\alpha$ for $\ell_1 = 0$: $\beta^* = 1.8999723763147356$, $\omega^* = 0.0720004334947197$ and $\alpha^* = -1.0429409014746944$. There $\sigma_{\min}(\Delta(i\omega^*)) = 9\cdot10^{-18}$ and $\ell_1 = 1.2\cdot10^{-15}$, and $c_1 = -0.18088368829030754\,i$, $c_2 = -1.1284870441889718 + 3.624629668654813\,i$ and $\ell_2 = -15.673336803892045$, each within $10^{-9}$ relative. The published value $-15.6733$ agrees within a relative $10^{-5}$ (the difference is $2\cdot10^{-6}$). At the rounded point $\alpha = -1.0429$, $\beta = 1.9$, $\omega = 0.07199637889093541$: $\ell_1 = 2.2194490639971935\cdot10^{-4}$ and $\ell_2 = -15.674792445917703$, within $10^{-6}$ relative.
3. Polar with a third equation, `x' = -w*y + a*x*r + b*x*r^2 - e*y*r - g*y*r^2`, `y' = w*x + a*y*r + b*y*r^2 + e*x*r + g*x*r^2` with `r = x^2 + y^2` written out, and a decoupled `z' = -2*z - delay(z,1)`, at $w = \omega = 0.7$, $a = 0$, $b = 0.37$. For $e = g = 0$: $c_1 = 0$, $c_2 = 4b = 1.48$ and $\ell_2 = 4b/\omega = 2.1142857142857143$, within $10^{-12}$. For $e = 0.3$, $g = 0.2$: $c_1 = 0.6\,i$ and $c_2 = 4(b + ig) = 1.48 + 0.8\,i$, within $10^{-12}$. The numbers are those of the polynomial normal form with the $z$ block decoupled, at $\|q\|_2 = 1$, computed in numpy from the formulas above.
4. Invariance under a change of coordinates. The model of test 1 in the variable $x$ with $u = x + \alpha x^2$, that is `x' = (-a*(delay(x,1)+al*delay(x,1)^2)*(1+x+al*x^2) + b*(x+al*x^2)^3 + c*(x+al*x^2)^5)/(1+2*al*x)`, at $b = b^*$, $\alpha \in \{0.3, -0.7\}$, $c \in \{0, 1\}$. $\ell_1$ is $0$ within $10^{-12}$, and $\operatorname{Re} c_2$ and $\ell_2$ equal those of test 1 within $10^{-9}$ relative. $\operatorname{Im} c_2$ is not asserted equal; the numpy values for $c = 0$ are $-1.4099754228281303$ at $\alpha = 0.3$ and $4.621882472064265$ at $\alpha = -0.7$, and the test asserts that they differ from each other and from $-1.9754621004742934$.
5. The scale and the phase. $c_2$ at scale $s \in \{0.5, 2\}$ is $s^4 c_2$ within $10^{-11}$ relative, and a phase of $q$ and $p$ (rotated together) leaves $c_2$ unchanged within $10^{-11}$ relative. For Wright with the quintic term at $b^*$ and for FHN at $\alpha^*$.
6. The pairing of $H_{2100}$. At the Wright point of test 1 with $c = 1$, the FHN point of test 2 and the two-delay point of test 8, the Hale pairing $\langle\psi, H_{2100}\rangle$ computed by Gauss-Legendre quadrature (30 nodes on each interval $[-\tau_j, 0]$) is below $10^{-10}$ in absolute value, equals $p\,\Delta'v_0 + c_1\,p\,\Delta''q$ within $10^{-10}$, and the same pairing with $v_0 = \xi$ (that is $\gamma = 0$) has modulus $|\gamma|$ within $10^{-10}$, with $|\gamma| > 10^{-3}$ at two of the three points, so that the test is not vacuous.
7. $\Delta''$. For a linear model with two states and two delays, `x' = -x + 0.3*y + 0.5*delay(x,1) - 0.4*delay(y,2)`, `y' = 0.2*x - 0.7*y + 0.6*delay(y,1) + 0.1*delay(x,2)`, $\Delta''(i\sigma)$ equals the central difference $(\Delta'(i(\sigma+h)) - \Delta'(i(\sigma-h)))/(2ih)$ within a relative $10^{-8}$ at $\sigma = 0.9$ and $h = 10^{-5}$, entry by entry.
8. Two delays. `u' = -a*delay(u,1)*(1+u) - d*delay(u,2)*(1+u) + b*u^3 + c*u^5` with $d = 0.3$. The Hopf point of the linear part is $a = 1.2554671180351438$, $\omega = 1.3525223386535317$ (a root of $a\cos\omega + d\cos 2\omega = 0$, $\omega = a\sin\omega + d\sin 2\omega$ found by Newton's method in the numpy script). $\operatorname{Re} c_1 = 0$ at $b^* = 0.1825032938841381$, with $c_1 = -0.5452168294726812\,i$. For $c = 0$: $c_2 = -0.04453646625048986 - 0.6668427841336013\,i$ and $\ell_2 = -0.03292845151439567$. For $c = 1$: $c_2 = 3.297054800005045 - 4.553605974334239\,i$ and $\ell_2 = 2.437708203242944$. Each within $10^{-9}$ relative. The two delays exercise the sum over the delay columns of $\Delta''$ and of the $\theta$ terms.
9. The forms of order 4 and 5. The model `x' = exp(a*x + b*delay(x,1)) - 1` has $D^rf[h_1,\dots,h_r] = e^{(a+b)x}\prod_i (a\,h_i^{(0)} + b\,h_i^{(1)})$ at the point $x$ for $r = 2,\dots,5$, with $h^{(0)}$ the entry in the state and $h^{(1)}$ that in the delay slot. At $x = 0.3$, for four argument sets (the last one complex) and $r = 2,\dots,5$ the form of `lyap.rs` agrees with the product within $10^{-12}$ relative, with the JIT and with `Evaluator::Fallback`. The FHN model of test 2 gives, for five equal real arguments with a 1 in the slot of $u_1(t-\tau)$ and 0 elsewhere, the first-row values $0$ for $r = 4$ and $32\beta$ for $r = 5$ ($2\beta\tanh^{(r)}(0)$ with $\tanh^{(5)}(0) = 16$), and $-4\beta$ for $r = 3$.
10. Errors. $\omega = 1.2$ on Wright gives `BadProblem`; a wrong length of `x` or `p`, $\omega = 0$, NaN, $10^{-9}$ give `BadProblem`. The zero-Hopf model of Section 8g test 6 gives `Singular` naming `Delta(0)`; the 1:2 resonance model of that test gives `Singular` naming `Delta(2 i omega)`. The model of test 1 with a decoupled `z' = 4.71238898038469*delay(z,1)` at $a = \omega = \pi/2$ gives `Singular` naming `Delta(3 i omega)` (for $\omega = \pi/2$, $\Delta_z(3i\omega) = i(3\pi/2 - 4.71238898038469)$, a zero up to rounding, while $\Delta_z(0)$, $\Delta_z(i\omega)$ and $\Delta_z(2i\omega)$ are regular). Wright at $b = 0$, where $\operatorname{Re} c_1 = -0.3364 \ne 0$, returns a result with `l1` $= -0.21413092244177392$ within $10^{-9}$ and no error.
11. `pc-cli dde-lyap` on the data of test 1 ($b = b^*$, $c = 1$, JIT) with `"l2": true` prints `l2` within $10^{-9}$ relative of $1.7362145915396248$ and the seven keys in alphabetical order. Without the key, and with `"l2": false`, the two outputs are equal and have the five keys of Section 8g. `"l2": 1` exits 2 with `"l2"` in the message, and a unit test of the problem reader refuses `1`, `"yes"`, `null` and `[true]`.
12. `pc-cli dde-hopf` with `"lyapunov": true` on the Wright model with the quintic term (free `["a", "b"]`, JIT, $c = 1$ in the model, as in Section 8g-2 test 6): the one `GH` special has `"l2"` within $10^{-4}$ of $1.7362145915396248$ (the point of the special is the located zero, so the value differs from that at $b^*$ by the size of the locator tolerance) and no other special has the key. On the model with the decoupled `z' = 4.71238898038469*delay(z,1)` block, whose $\Delta(3i\omega)$ is singular at the curve, the `GH` special has `"l2": null`. With `"lyapunov": false` or without the key the output has no `"l2"` and is the output of Section 8f.

## 8i. The critical normal-form coefficients at a zero-Hopf point (phase E9a, step 7)

Step 7 takes a zero-Hopf point of a delay equation (an equilibrium whose characteristic roots include a simple zero and a simple pair $\pm i\omega$, for example the intersection of a fold curve and a Hopf curve) and computes the coefficients of the critical normal form up to order three, together with the three numbers $s$, $\theta$ and $e$ that Section 6.2.1 of the paper builds from them. It computes the numbers at one point. It does not locate zero-Hopf points, does not compute the parameter-dependent normal form (the predictors of the fold, Hopf and torus curves), does not say which unfolding scenario the point has, and does not treat the transcritical-Hopf case. It changes no C function, no MATLAB function and no IR entry, so the ways in are `dde::zero_hopf` and `pc-cli dde-zh`.

**What is not verified.**
- The formulas are those of Bosschaert, Janssens and Kuznetsov, arXiv 1903.08276 v1, Section 6.2.1, with Lemma 4 (equation 27) for the vectors that are not pure exponentials. I read v1 only. The paper prints the coefficients for two zero-Hopf points of a Hindmarsh–Rose neuron with a delay in the quadratic term. Tests 1 and 2 reproduce them, and test 3 checks the same formulas against an independent route in the ODE limit (the homological equation solved in the eigenbasis, with no bordered system).
- The paper does not state the scale of $q_0$ and $q_1$ or the sign of $q_0$. I use $\|q_0\|_2 = \|q_1\|_2 = 1$, the convention of Sections 8g and 8h. At the two published points it reproduces the printed $s$ and $e$, which fixes the scale of $q_1$ in $s$ and of $q_0$ in $e$ (see "The scale and the sign"). The sign of $q_0$ changes $g_{200}$, $g_{110}$ and $g_{011}$ and none of $s_{\rm product}$, $\theta$ and $e$. The rule below (the first component that is not small is positive) gives the printed signs at both points, but it is my rule, so $g_{200}$, $g_{110}$ and $g_{011}$ match the paper only up to that sign. A component of $q_0$ that sits near the threshold of the rule could change sign under a small perturbation of the point.
- $g_{210}$, $g_{111}$ and $g_{021}$ are not invariants. They depend on the resonant part of the quadratic transformation, which Lemma 4 fixes through the pairing condition, and they change under a change of coordinates. $g_{200}$, $g_{110}$, $g_{011}$ and $g_{300}$, and the numbers $s_{\rm product}$, $\theta$ and $e$, do not (test 4).
- The values of the unfolding parameters (the predictors) and the classification of the scenario are not computed.
- A semisimple multiple root, at zero or at $i\omega$ (a null space of dimension 2 or more), is refused by check 4, for the reason given in Section 8g: the pairing test does not see it and the coefficients depend on the null vector taken. The threshold is `RESONANCE_MIN` on the ratio of the second smallest to the largest singular value, and it has no source.
- The constants `NULL_RESIDUAL_MAX`, `PAIRING_MIN`, `RESONANCE_MIN` and `OMEGA_MIN` are those of Sections 8e and 8g and have no source.

**Inputs.** `dde::zero_hopf(model, x, p, omega)` has the arguments and the `BadProblem` cases of `dde::lyapunov` (Section 8g). The point is not polished. `x` and `omega` are taken as given, and the result reports how well they satisfy the equations.

**The linear part.** As in Section 8h: $\tau_0 = 0$, $M_0 = A_0$, $M_j = A_j$, $\Delta(z) = zI - \sum_{j=0}^{m} M_j e^{-z\tau_j}$, $\Delta'(z) = I + \sum_j \tau_j M_j e^{-z\tau_j}$ and $\Delta''(z) = -\sum_j \tau_j^2 M_j e^{-z\tau_j}$. $\Delta(0) = -\sum_j M_j$ is real and does not depend on the delay times.

**The vectors.** There are two pairs.
- $q_0 \in \mathbb R^n$ and $p_0 \in \mathbb R^{1\times n}$ with $\Delta(0)q_0 = 0$, $p_0\Delta(0) = 0$, $p_0\Delta'(0)q_0 = 1$ and $\|q_0\|_2 = 1$. They come from the smallest singular vectors of the real matrix $\Delta(0)$ (right for $q_0$, left for $p_0$), and $p_0$ is scaled by the pairing. The sign of $q_0$ is chosen so that its first component with $|q_{0,i}| \ge 1/(2\sqrt n)$ is positive, and $p_0$ changes sign with it. Such a component exists because $\max_i |q_{0,i}| \ge 1/\sqrt n$ for a unit vector.
- $q_1 \in \mathbb C^n$ and $p_1 \in \mathbb C^{1\times n}$ with $\Delta(i\omega)q_1 = 0$, $p_1\Delta(i\omega) = 0$, $p_1\Delta'(i\omega)q_1 = 1$ and $\|q_1\|_2 = 1$, exactly as $q$ and $p$ of Section 8g (the pairing is bilinear, with no conjugate, and the phase of $q_1$ does not matter).

Let $\varphi_0$ be the constant function $q_0$ and $\varphi_1(\theta) = e^{i\omega\theta}q_1$, so that the column of $\tau_j$ of $\varphi_0$ is $q_0$ and that of $\varphi_1$ is $e^{-i\omega\tau_j}q_1$. $\bar\varphi_1$ is its conjugate. $B$ and $C$ are the multilinear forms of orders 2 and 3 of Section 8g, applied to the vectors $\Xi\psi$.

**The quadratic coefficients.**
$$g_{200} = \tfrac12\,p_0B(\varphi_0,\varphi_0),\qquad g_{110} = p_1B(\varphi_0,\varphi_1),\qquad g_{011} = p_0B(\varphi_1,\bar\varphi_1).$$

**The bordered solve (Lemma 4).** For a root $\lambda$ of $\det\Delta$, a pair $(q, p)$ with $\Delta(\lambda)q = 0$, $p\Delta(\lambda) = 0$, $p\Delta'(\lambda)q = 1$, a vector $\eta$ and a number $\kappa$, let $\xi$ solve
$$\begin{pmatrix}\Delta(\lambda) & p^H\\ q^H & 0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\xi\\ s\end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix}\eta + \kappa\,\Delta'(\lambda)q\\ 0\end{pmatrix},\qquad \gamma = -p\,\Delta'(\lambda)\xi + \tfrac12\kappa\,p\,\Delta''(\lambda)q,\qquad v_0 = \xi + \gamma q.$$
The right side is in the range of $\Delta(\lambda)$ exactly when $\kappa = -p\eta$, and every use below takes that $\kappa$. The function is $H(\theta) = e^{\lambda\theta}(v_0 - \kappa\theta\,q)$ and its column of $\tau_j$ is $e^{-\lambda\tau_j}(v_0 + \kappa\tau_j\,q)$. $v_0$ does not depend on the border, because $\gamma$ is defined by the pairing condition: with $\psi(s) = p\,e^{-\lambda s}$ and the Hale pairing of Section 8h, $\langle\psi, H\rangle = p\,\Delta'v_0 - \tfrac12\kappa\,p\,\Delta''q$, and this choice of $\gamma$ makes it zero. For $m = 0$ one has $\Delta' = I$ and $\Delta'' = 0$, so $\gamma = -p\xi$. The border $(p^H, q^H)$ makes the matrix nonsingular for a simple root, as in Section 8h.

**The quadratic vectors.**
- $H_{20000}$: $\lambda = 0$, $(q, p) = (q_0, p_0)$, $\eta = B(\varphi_0,\varphi_0)$, $\kappa = -2g_{200}$.
- $H_{11000}$: $\lambda = i\omega$, $(q, p) = (q_1, p_1)$, $\eta = B(\varphi_0,\varphi_1)$, $\kappa = -g_{110}$.
- $H_{01100}$: $\lambda = 0$, $(q, p) = (q_0, p_0)$, $\eta = B(\varphi_1,\bar\varphi_1)$, $\kappa = -g_{011}$.
- $H_{02000}(\theta) = e^{2i\omega\theta}\,\Delta(2i\omega)^{-1}B(\varphi_1,\varphi_1)$, a pure exponential, with the column $e^{-2i\omega\tau_j}$ times its value.
- $H_{00200} = \bar H_{02000}$ and $H_{10100} = \bar H_{11000}$.

**The cubic coefficients.**
$$\begin{aligned}
g_{300} &= \tfrac16\,p_0\bigl(3B(\varphi_0, H_{20000}) + C(\varphi_0,\varphi_0,\varphi_0)\bigr),\\
g_{111} &= p_0\bigl(B(\varphi_0, H_{01100}) + B(\varphi_1, H_{10100}) + B(\bar\varphi_1, H_{11000}) + C(\varphi_0,\varphi_1,\bar\varphi_1)\bigr),\\
g_{210} &= \tfrac12\,p_1\bigl(2B(\varphi_0, H_{11000}) + B(\varphi_1, H_{20000}) + C(\varphi_0,\varphi_0,\varphi_1)\bigr),\\
g_{021} &= \tfrac12\,p_1\bigl(2B(\varphi_1, H_{01100}) + B(\bar\varphi_1, H_{02000}) + C(\varphi_1,\varphi_1,\bar\varphi_1)\bigr).
\end{aligned}$$
The critical normal form is $\dot z_0 = g_{200}z_0^2 + g_{011}z_1\bar z_1 + g_{300}z_0^3 + g_{111}z_0z_1\bar z_1 + \dots$ and $\dot z_1 = i\omega z_1 + g_{110}z_0z_1 + g_{210}z_0^2z_1 + g_{021}z_1^2\bar z_1 + \dots$ with $z_0$ real. $g_{200}$, $g_{011}$, $g_{300}$ and $g_{111}$ are real by theory. The function returns every coefficient as `[re, im]` and drops nothing, and tests 1 to 3 check that the imaginary parts of those four are below $10^{-12}$ of the real parts. For $m = 0$ the delays drop out and these are the classical formulas of the homological equation, which test 3 uses as its oracle.

**The classification numbers.**
$$s_{\rm product} = g_{200}\,g_{011},\qquad \theta = \frac{\operatorname{Re} g_{110}}{\operatorname{Re} g_{200}},\qquad e = \operatorname{Re}\Bigl[g_{210} + g_{110}\Bigl(\frac{\operatorname{Re} g_{021}}{g_{011}} - \frac32\,\frac{g_{300}}{g_{200}} + \frac{g_{111}}{2g_{011}}\Bigr) - \frac{g_{021}\,g_{200}}{g_{011}}\Bigr].$$
The paper's $s$ is the sign of $s_{\rm product}$. DDE-BIFTOOL stores the product, and so does the result, with the sign `s` ($-1$, $0$ or $1$; $0$ when the product is exactly $0$) next to it. $\theta$ is `None` when $\operatorname{Re} g_{200} = 0$ and $e$ is `None` when it is not a finite number (for example $g_{011} = 0$).

**The scale and the sign.** Replace $q_0 \to c\,q_0$ with $c \ne 0$ real and $p_0 \to p_0/c$, and $q_1 \to d\,q_1$ with $d \in \mathbb C\setminus\{0\}$ and $p_1 \to p_1/d$. The coefficients scale as $g_{200} \sim c$, $g_{110} \sim c$, $g_{011} \sim |d|^2/c$, $g_{300} \sim c^2$, $g_{111} \sim |d|^2$, $g_{210} \sim c^2$ and $g_{021} \sim |d|^2$, hence $s_{\rm product} \sim |d|^2$, $\theta$ is invariant and $e \sim c^2$ and does not depend on $d$. A phase of $q_1$ changes nothing, and $c = -1$ changes the sign of $g_{200}$, $g_{110}$ and $g_{011}$ only. So the magnitude of $s_{\rm product}$ is tied to $\|q_1\|_2 = 1$ and the magnitude of $e$ to $\|q_0\|_2 = 1$. The signs of $s_{\rm product}$ and $e$ and the value of $\theta$ are not.

**The forms.** As in Sections 8g and 8h, through `model.eval` with `EntryKind::Dir`, orders 2 and 3. The vectors with a $\theta$ term carry their explicit delayed values (the columns above) in the hidden slots.

**The checks and the errors.** No new `EngineError` variant. In this order:
1. A wrong length of `x` or `p`, a delay time that is not finite and positive (Section 8d), and $\omega$ not finite or at most `OMEGA_MIN` ($10^{-8}$): `BadProblem`.
2. The relative residual $\|\Delta(0)q_0\|_2/s(0)$, with $s(0) = \sum_j\|M_j\|_F$, must be at most `NULL_RESIDUAL_MAX` ($10^{-4}$), else `BadProblem` naming $\Delta(0)$: there is no zero root.
3. The relative residual $\|\Delta(i\omega)q_1\|_2/s(i\omega)$, with $s(i\omega) = \omega + \sum_j\|M_j\|_F$, at most `NULL_RESIDUAL_MAX`, else `BadProblem` naming $\Delta(i\omega)$.
4. For the zero root and then the Hopf root, two tests in this order. First the null space has dimension 1: the ratio $\sigma_{n-1}/\sigma_1$ of the singular values of $\Delta(0)$ (the real $n \times n$ matrix) and of $\Delta(i\omega)$ (through its real embedding, as in Section 8g) is at least `RESONANCE_MIN` (the test is skipped for $n = 1$), else `Singular` with the text "null space of dimension 2 or more" naming `Delta(0)` or `Delta(i omega)`. Then $|p_0\Delta'(0)q_0|$ and $|p_1\Delta'(i\omega)q_1|$, before the scaling, at least `PAIRING_MIN` ($10^{-8}$), else `Singular` naming which of the two roots is not simple. So the order is the multiplicity of $\Delta(0)$, the pairing at 0, the multiplicity of $\Delta(i\omega)$ and the pairing at $i\omega$.
5. $\Delta(2i\omega)$ regular, by the singular-value ratio of Section 8g with `RESONANCE_MIN`, else `Singular` naming `Delta(2 i omega)` (a 1:2 resonance). Only $H_{02000}$ needs it.

The equilibrium residual $\|f(x,\dots,x,p)\|_\infty$ is returned and not checked.

**Result.** `ZeroHopf { omega, g200, g110, g011, g300, g111, g210, g021 (each [re, im]), s_product, s, theta: Option<f64>, e: Option<f64>, null_residual_zero, null_residual_hopf, equilibrium_residual }`.

**Command line.** `pc-cli dde-zh <problem.json>` reads
```json
{"model": "path.ode", "params": {"a": 0.0}, "x0": [0.0, 0.0], "omega": 1.0}
```
All four keys are required and an unknown key is an error. `params` is read as in `dde-hopf`: a declared parameter that it leaves out keeps the value of the model file (the same precedent as `dde-lyap`, and an owner item). `x0` and `omega` are used as given. The output has these keys, in alphabetical order: `e`, `equilibrium_residual`, `g011`, `g021`, `g110`, `g111`, `g200`, `g210`, `g300`, `null_residual_hopf`, `null_residual_zero`, `omega`, `s`, `s_product` and `theta`. Each `g` is `[re, im]` and a `None` prints as `null`. Exit codes follow `dde-lyap`: 2 for a bad problem file or a `BadProblem` error, 1 for a model error or any other engine error. The model is built with the JIT.

**Acceptance tests.** Rust tests. The oracle values were computed in numpy scripts written for this section, one with the bordered formulas above and one that solves the homological equation in the eigenbasis (ODE limit) without them. The two agree to $10^{-15}$ in the ODE limit. The engine tests run the JIT and `Evaluator::Fallback` unless a test says otherwise.

The Hindmarsh–Rose model of tests 1, 2 and 4, with $a = 1$, $b = 3$, $c = 1$, $d = 5$, $\chi = -1.6$ (the paper's names, with $S$ written `sl` because XPP ignores case):
`x' = y - a*x^3 + b*delay(x,tau)^2 - c*z + iapp`, `y' = c - d*x^2 - y`, `z' = r*(sl*(x-chi) - z)`.
It has $n = 3$, $M_0 = \begin{pmatrix}-3ax^2 & 1 & -c\\ -2dx & -1 & 0\\ rS & 0 & -r\end{pmatrix}$ at the equilibrium, $M_1$ with the single entry $2bx$ at $(0,0)$, $B(u,v) = (-6ax\,u_0v_0 + 2b\,u_0^{\rm d}v_0^{\rm d},\ -2d\,u_0v_0,\ 0)$ and $C(u,v,w) = (-6a\,u_0v_0w_0, 0, 0)$, where the superscript d is the delayed entry.

1. Point 1: $r = 0.001$, $S = -0.57452592$, $I_{\rm app} = -1.9579343722240001$, $\tau = 5.768830919756731$, $x = (0.13079999999999994,\ 0.9144568000000001,\ -0.9943894623360001)$, $\omega = 1.0079161435515798$. $q_0 = (0.5734503027938144, -0.7500729960543089, -0.3294620627868945)$ and the values are $g_{200} = -0.0023705117051419354$, $g_{110} = 0.3295757773146957 + 0.7005829592112022\,i$, $g_{011} = -0.00779860717029379$, $g_{300} = 0.01064655140341144$, $g_{111} = -0.01462224981115301$, $g_{210} = -2.7764058216894725 - 3.1806449184838144\,i$, $g_{021} = -0.37451557400768787 - 2.475382718221687\,i$, $s_{\rm product} = 1.8486689580985255\cdot10^{-5}$, $\theta = -139.03149121761547$ and $e = 15.69406438255759$. Each within $10^{-8}$ relative of the numpy value (the real or imaginary part separately, with the imaginary parts of the four real coefficients below $10^{-12}$ of their real parts), and within half a unit of the last printed digit of the paper ($g_{200} = -0.0024$, $g_{110} = 0.3296 + 0.7006i$, $g_{011} = -0.0078$, $g_{300} = 0.0106$, $g_{111} = -0.0146$, $g_{210} = -2.7764 - 3.1806i$, $g_{021} = -0.3745 - 2.4754i$, $s = 1.8487\cdot10^{-5}$, $\theta = -139.0315$, $e = 15.6941$). $\det\Delta(0)$ is $5\cdot10^{-19}$ here, so the test also checks that `null_residual_zero` and `null_residual_hopf` are below $10^{-10}$.
2. Point 2: $r = 1.4$, $S = -8$, $I_{\rm app} = -18.84904247552719$, $\tau = 0.9402469410500846$, $x = (1.097167540709727,\ -5.018883061935152,\ -21.577340325677817)$, $\omega = 5.604243934764924$. $q_0 = (-0.07344653310944081, 0.8058315210534073, 0.5875722648755265)$ (the second component is the first that passes the threshold $1/(2\sqrt3) = 0.2887$) and $g_{200} = 0.20113356968855572$, $g_{110} = -0.0315551147150465 - 0.07839252020170437\,i$, $g_{011} = 8.800187670315616$, $g_{300} = -0.20277383695308454$, $g_{111} = -9.518981756536286$, $g_{210} = 0.025797202214236054 + 0.047465229337271556\,i$, $g_{021} = 1.2457186350987874 + 2.1926924610613003\,i$, $s_{\rm product} = 1.7700131600597948$, $\theta = -0.15688636543321866$ and $e = -0.0377936744566541$. The same tolerances, and the paper's $s = 1.770013$, $\theta = -0.156886$ and $e = -0.037794$ within half a unit of the last digit.
3. The ODE limit. Three states with $\dot x = Ax + Q(x) + K(x)$, $A = V\Lambda V^{-1}$, $\omega = 1.3$, $V = \begin{pmatrix}0.6 & 0.2 & -0.4\\ -0.3 & 0.9 & 0.5\\ 0.1 & -0.7 & 0.8\end{pmatrix}$, $\Lambda = \begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\0&0&-\omega\\0&\omega&0\end{pmatrix}$, so that $A$ has the eigenvalues $0$ and $\pm i\omega$ and
$A = \begin{pmatrix}-0.2791411042944785 & -0.5901840490797546 & -0.09570552147239264\\ 0.07377300613496933 & -0.27116564417177924 & -1.2561349693251536\\ 0.6300613496932514 & 1.443558282208589 & 0.5503067484662576\end{pmatrix}$,
$Q = (0.5x_0^2 - 0.8x_0x_1 + 0.3x_1x_2,\ 0.7x_1^2 + 0.4x_0x_2 - 0.6x_2^2,\ -0.5x_0x_1 + 0.9x_1x_2 + 0.2x_0^2)$ and $K = (0.3x_0^3 - 0.4x_0x_1x_2,\ 0.5x_1^3 + 0.2x_0^2x_1,\ -0.6x_2^3 + 0.35x_0x_2^2)$, and a fourth, decoupled state `w' = -2*w - delay(w,1)` so that the model has a delay. At $x = 0$, $\omega = 1.3$: $q_0 = (0.8846517369293829, -0.4423258684646913, 0.14744195615489722, 0)$ and $g_{200} = 0.9246103161511168$, $g_{110} = -0.8140288490349439 - 0.009644784401850182\,i$, $g_{011} = -0.03162487464216188$, $g_{300} = -0.02226087606372551$, $g_{111} = -0.18696383032729416$, $g_{210} = 0.3917134051147323 + 1.3234256618089357\,i$, $g_{021} = 0.004070414517577045 + 0.20894905038180536\,i$, $s_{\rm product} = -0.029240685341128735$, $\theta = -0.8804020837919143$ and $e = -1.8201434223218098$, each within $10^{-9}$ relative.
4. Invariance. The Hindmarsh–Rose model of tests 1 and 2 in the variable $s$ with $x = x^* + s + \alpha s^2$: `s' = (y - a*X^3 + b*Xd^2 - c*z + iapp)/(1 + 2*al*s)`, `y' = c - d*X^2 - y`, `z' = r*(sl*(X-chi) - z)`, with $X = x^* + s + \alpha s^2$ written out ($x^*$ as a 17-digit literal) and $X_d$ the same expression in `delay(s,tau)`. The equilibrium is $(0, y^*, z^*)$ and the linearization is that of the original, so $q_0$ and its sign do not change. For both points and $\alpha \in \{0.3, -0.7\}$: $g_{200}$, $g_{110}$, $g_{011}$ and $g_{300}$ equal the values of tests 1 and 2 within $10^{-9}$ relative, and $s_{\rm product}$, $\theta$ and $e$ within $10^{-8}$ relative. For the test not to be vacuous, at least one of $g_{210}$, $g_{111}$ and $g_{021}$ differs from the unshifted value by a relative $10^{-3}$ or more at each point and $\alpha$.
5. The pairing of the bordered vectors, as unit tests inside the module. At both points of tests 1 and 2, for $H_{20000}$, $H_{11000}$ and $H_{01100}$, the Hale pairing $\langle\psi, H\rangle$ by Gauss–Legendre quadrature (30 nodes on each interval $[-\tau_j, 0]$) is below $10^{-10}$ in absolute value and equals $p\,\Delta'v_0 - \frac12\kappa\,p\,\Delta''q$ within $10^{-10}$. With $\gamma = 0$ (that is $v_0 = \xi$) its modulus is $|\gamma|$ within $10^{-10}$. The numpy values of $|\gamma|$ for $(H_{20000}, H_{11000}, H_{01100})$ are $(1.7128, 2.5922, 2.8174)$ at point 1 and $(1.6126, 0.0451, 35.278)$ at point 2, all above $10^{-3}$, so that the test is not vacuous.
6. The scale and the sign. An internal function `zero_hopf_scaled(model, x, p, omega, s0, s1)` takes $c = $ `s0` (real) and $d = $ `s1` (complex) and the public function passes $1$ and $1$. For the two points, with $(c, d) \in \{(0.5, 2), (2, 0.5i), (-1, 1), (1, e^{0.7i})\}$, the coefficients scale with the weights above within $10^{-11}$ relative, $c = -1$ flips only $g_{200}$, $g_{110}$ and $g_{011}$, and a phase of $q_1$ changes nothing. $\theta$ is invariant, $s_{\rm product}$ scales with $|d|^2$ and $e$ with $c^2$.
7. A zero-Hopf point with a closed form, the model of Section 8f test 5, `par a=0, tau=1, b=1`, `x'=-(a+y)*x-b*delay(x,tau)`, `y'=a-y*y`, at $(a, \tau, b) = (0, \pi/2, 1)$, $x = y = 0$, $\omega = 1$. $q_0 = (0, 1)$, $g_{200} = -1$, $g_{110} = -1/(1 + i\pi/2) = -0.288400439142001 + 0.4530183504502902\,i$, $g_{011} = 0$ exactly (the second component of $q_1$ is zero), so $|s_{\rm product}| < 10^{-12}$, $s = 0$, $\theta = 0.288400439142001$ and $e$ is `None`. Each within $10^{-12}$.
8. Errors. Each of these gives the named error.
   - A wrong length of `x` or `p`, $\omega = 0$, NaN and $10^{-9}$, and a delay time that is not positive: `BadProblem`.
   - Point 1 of test 1 with $s_l = 0.5$ and $r = 0.5$: `BadProblem` naming $\Delta(0)$ (the point is not a zero root). Changing $r$ alone keeps $\Delta(0)$ singular, because $r$ multiplies the whole third row of $\Delta(0)$ and so factors out of its determinant, which is why $s_l$ has to change.
   - Point 1 of test 1 with $\omega = 0.9$, and with $r = 0.01$ (the relative residual of $\Delta(i\omega)q_1$ is about $6.0\cdot10^{-4}$, above $10^{-4}$): `BadProblem` naming $\Delta(i\omega)$.
   - Wright's equation at $a = \pi/2$, $x = 0$, where $\Delta(0) = a \ne 0$: `BadProblem` naming $\Delta(0)$.
   - A Jordan block at zero: `x' = y`, `y' = 0`, an oscillator `u' = -v`, `v' = u` at $\omega = 1$, and the decoupled `w' = -2*w - delay(w,1)`: `Singular`, from $p_0\Delta'(0)q_0 = 0$.
   - A semisimple double root at zero: `a' = 0`, `b' = 0` in place of the Jordan block (a null space of dimension 2 at $\Delta(0)$, $\Delta' = I$ so a pairing that is not 0): `Singular` with "null space of dimension 2 or more" naming `Delta(0)`. A semisimple double root at $i\omega$: one zero root `a' = 0`, and two oscillators at $\omega = 1$: the same text, naming `Delta(i omega)`.
   - The order of the tests of check 4: a Jordan block at zero together with two oscillators at $\omega = 1$ is refused for the pairing at 0 ("the root 0 is not simple") and not for the multiplicity at $i\omega$, and a double root at zero together with a Jordan block at $i\omega$ (the four states of the Hopf-Hopf test) is refused for the multiplicity of $\Delta(0)$.
   - A 1:2 resonance: the model of test 7 with an oscillator `u' = -2*v`, `v' = 2*u` added, at $\omega = 1$: `Singular` naming `Delta(2 i omega)`.
9. `pc-cli dde-zh` on the data of test 1 (JIT) prints the values of test 1 within $10^{-8}$ relative and the fifteen keys in alphabetical order. A model path is read relative to the problem file. An unknown key, a missing key, a wrong type and an unreadable problem file exit 2, and so does an `omega` that is not a zero-Hopf frequency. An unreadable model and a model without a delay (stderr has `error[ir:delay]`) exit 1. `--help` and a missing argument behave as for `dde-lyap`. A unit test of the problem reader names the key in each message.

## 8j. The critical normal-form coefficients at a Hopf-Hopf point (phase E9a, step 8)

Step 8 takes a Hopf-Hopf point of a delay equation (an equilibrium with two simple pairs of characteristic roots $\pm i\omega_1$ and $\pm i\omega_2$ on the imaginary axis, $\omega_1 > \omega_2 > 0$, for example the intersection of two Hopf curves) and computes the four cubic coefficients of the critical normal form, together with the two ratios $\theta$ and $\delta$ that Section 8.3 of the paper builds from them to sort its example point into a case. It computes the numbers at one point. It does not locate Hopf-Hopf points, does not compute the parameter-dependent normal form (the predictors of the Neimark-Sacker curves), does not treat the Bogdanov-Takens point, and leaves the sort into one of the twelve unfolding cases to Section 8j-2. It changes no C function, no MATLAB function and no IR entry, so the ways in are `dde::hopf_hopf` and `pc-cli dde-hh`.

**What is not verified.**
- The formulas are those of Bosschaert, Janssens and Kuznetsov, arXiv 1903.08276 v1, Section 6.3.1. I read v1 only. The paper prints the four coefficients, $\theta$ and $\delta$ for a Hopf-Hopf point of an active control system with a delay in the feedback (Section 8.3 and supplement S3.2). Those two examples are the only places where the paper defines $\theta$ and $\delta$; Section 5.3.2 uses the same real parts in its Neimark-Sacker formulas. Test 1 reproduces them. Tests 2 and 3 check the same formulas against two other routes: a change of coordinates that moves terms between $B$ and $C$, and the ODE limit solved through the homological equation in the eigenbasis, with none of the formulas above.
- The paper is not consistent with itself in three places. Its formula for $\delta$ in Sections 8.3 and S3.2 writes $g_{1101}$, which is not a coefficient of the normal form (72); I read it as $g_{1110}$, and the printed $\delta = -2.3517$ agrees with that reading. The supplement lists the second Hopf-Hopf point of the branch with $\omega_1$ and $\omega_2$ exchanged (its $g_{2100}$ is the $g_{0021}$ of the first point), so that its $\theta$ and $\delta$ are swapped as well. Section 8.3 calls the case "$\theta\delta > 1$" and S3.2 calls it "$\theta\delta > 0$". None of this changes a number here, because the section stops before the case.
- The normal form (72) is written with the coefficients of the monomials, $\dot z_1 = i\omega_1 z_1 + g_{2100}z_1|z_1|^2 + g_{1011}z_1|z_2|^2$ and $\dot z_2 = i\omega_2 z_2 + g_{1110}z_2|z_1|^2 + g_{0021}z_2|z_2|^2$, with no factorial in front. The formulas for $g_{2100}$ and $g_{0021}$ carry the factor $\tfrac12$ that this convention needs. Test 3 settles it in the ODE limit, where the homological equation gives the monomial coefficients directly, and test 4 settles it with a closed form.
- The paper assumes the nonresonance conditions $k\omega_1 \ne l\omega_2$ for $0 < k + l \le 5$. The function checks only the two cases that make one of its own linear systems singular, 1:1 (excluded by $\omega_1 > \omega_2$) and 1:2 (check 5 below). At 1:3, 1:4, 2:3 and the other resonances nothing is singular, the function returns the four numbers, and the normal form that they belong to has further resonant terms. That is an owner item.
- A semisimple multiple root ($\Delta(i\omega_k)$ with a null space of dimension 2 or more) is refused by check 4, because the four coefficients then depend on which null vector $q_k$ is taken. The pairing test alone does not see it: for two identical oscillators at $\omega_k$, $\Delta' = I$ and the pairing of any null vector with its left partner is not 0. The threshold is `RESONANCE_MIN` on the ratio of the second smallest to the largest singular value of $\Delta(i\omega_k)$, and it has no source. The first-Lyapunov-coefficient function of Section 8g and the zero-Hopf function of Section 8i refuse such a root in the same way.
- The paper does not state the scale of $q_1$ and $q_2$. I use $\|q_1\|_2 = \|q_2\|_2 = 1$, the convention of Sections 8g to 8i. At the published point it reproduces all printed digits, which fixes the scale (see "The scale"). The phases do not matter.
- The predictors are not computed, and this section does not name the unfolding case. Section 8j-2 does, with the Guckenheimer and Holmes lettering and its own list of what is not verified. Kuznetsov's book has 5 simple and 6 difficult cases, and its 3rd edition (the paper's reference [43], p. 360) is unread. STATUS lists what a source search confirmed and what it did not. $\theta$ and $\delta$ are returned, and so is the sign of $(\operatorname{Re} g_{2100})(\operatorname{Re} g_{0021})$ that separates the simple from the difficult cases.
- The constants `NULL_RESIDUAL_MAX`, `PAIRING_MIN`, `RESONANCE_MIN` and `OMEGA_MIN` are those of Sections 8e and 8g and have no source.

**Inputs.** `dde::hopf_hopf(model, x, p, omega1, omega2)` has the arguments of `dde::zero_hopf` (Section 8i) with two frequencies. The point is not polished. `x`, `omega1` and `omega2` are taken as given, and the result reports how well they satisfy the equations. The function does not sort the frequencies: $\omega_1 \le \omega_2$ is a `BadProblem`, so that a caller that swaps them gets an error and not silently permuted coefficients.

**The linear part.** As in Section 8i: $\tau_0 = 0$, $M_0 = A_0$, $M_j = A_j$, $\Delta(z) = zI - \sum_{j=0}^{m} M_j e^{-z\tau_j}$ and $\Delta'(z) = I + \sum_j \tau_j M_j e^{-z\tau_j}$.

**The vectors.** For $k = 1, 2$: $q_k \in \mathbb C^n$ and $p_k \in \mathbb C^{1\times n}$ with $\Delta(i\omega_k)q_k = 0$, $p_k\Delta(i\omega_k) = 0$, $p_k\Delta'(i\omega_k)q_k = 1$ and $\|q_k\|_2 = 1$, from the smallest singular vectors of $\Delta(i\omega_k)$, exactly as $q$ and $p$ of Section 8g. The pairing is bilinear, with no conjugate. Let $\varphi_k(\theta) = e^{i\omega_k\theta}q_k$, so that the column of $\tau_j$ of $\varphi_k$ is $e^{-i\omega_k\tau_j}q_k$, and $\bar\varphi_k$ its conjugate. $B$ and $C$ are the multilinear forms of orders 2 and 3 of Section 8g, applied to the vectors $\Xi\psi$ (the columns).

**The quadratic vectors.** All six are pure exponentials. For $\eta \in \mathbb C^n$ and $\lambda$ with $\Delta(\lambda)$ regular, the function $\theta \mapsto e^{\lambda\theta}\Delta(\lambda)^{-1}\eta$ has the column $e^{-\lambda\tau_j}\Delta(\lambda)^{-1}\eta$ for $\tau_j$. So, with no bordered system,
$$\begin{aligned}
H_{110000} &= \Delta(0)^{-1}B(\varphi_1,\bar\varphi_1), & H_{200000}(\theta) &= e^{2i\omega_1\theta}\Delta(2i\omega_1)^{-1}B(\varphi_1,\varphi_1),\\
H_{001100} &= \Delta(0)^{-1}B(\varphi_2,\bar\varphi_2), & H_{002000}(\theta) &= e^{2i\omega_2\theta}\Delta(2i\omega_2)^{-1}B(\varphi_2,\varphi_2),\\
H_{101000}(\theta) &= e^{i(\omega_1+\omega_2)\theta}\Delta(i(\omega_1+\omega_2))^{-1}B(\varphi_1,\varphi_2), & H_{100100}(\theta) &= e^{i(\omega_1-\omega_2)\theta}\Delta(i(\omega_1-\omega_2))^{-1}B(\varphi_1,\bar\varphi_2),
\end{aligned}$$
and $\bar H_{100100}$ is the conjugate of $H_{100100}$, with the conjugated column.

**The cubic coefficients.**
$$\begin{aligned}
g_{2100} &= \tfrac12\,p_1\bigl(2B(\varphi_1, H_{110000}) + B(\bar\varphi_1, H_{200000}) + C(\varphi_1,\varphi_1,\bar\varphi_1)\bigr),\\
g_{1011} &= p_1\bigl(B(\bar\varphi_2, H_{101000}) + B(\varphi_1, H_{001100}) + B(\varphi_2, H_{100100}) + C(\varphi_1,\varphi_2,\bar\varphi_2)\bigr),\\
g_{1110} &= p_2\bigl(B(\bar\varphi_1, H_{101000}) + B(\varphi_1, \bar H_{100100}) + B(\varphi_2, H_{110000}) + C(\varphi_1,\bar\varphi_1,\varphi_2)\bigr),\\
g_{0021} &= \tfrac12\,p_2\bigl(2B(\varphi_2, H_{001100}) + B(\bar\varphi_2, H_{002000}) + C(\varphi_2,\varphi_2,\bar\varphi_2)\bigr).
\end{aligned}$$
Unlike the zero-Hopf case, no quadratic vector needs a pairing condition, because no quadratic monomial is resonant (this is where the nonresonance assumption is used). The four cubic coefficients are therefore invariants of the system and not of a choice of transformation, and test 2 checks that. The function returns each as `[re, im]` and drops nothing.

**The classification numbers.**
$$\theta = \frac{\operatorname{Re} g_{1011}}{\operatorname{Re} g_{0021}},\qquad \delta = \frac{\operatorname{Re} g_{1110}}{\operatorname{Re} g_{2100}},\qquad \text{product} = (\operatorname{Re} g_{2100})(\operatorname{Re} g_{0021}).$$
$\theta$ is `None` when $\operatorname{Re} g_{0021} = 0$ and $\delta$ is `None` when $\operatorname{Re} g_{2100} = 0$. The point is of the difficult type when the product is negative. The result carries the sign `sign` of the product ($-1$, $0$ or $1$; $0$ only when the product is exactly $0$).

**The scale.** Replace $q_1 \to d_1q_1$ and $q_2 \to d_2q_2$ with $d_1, d_2 \in \mathbb C\setminus\{0\}$, and $p_k \to p_k/d_k$. Then $g_{2100}$ and $g_{1110}$ scale with $|d_1|^2$, $g_{1011}$ and $g_{0021}$ with $|d_2|^2$, $\theta$ and $\delta$ are invariant (each is a ratio of two coefficients with the same weight), and the product scales with $|d_1|^2|d_2|^2$. A phase of either vector changes nothing. So the magnitudes of the four coefficients and of the product are tied to $\|q_k\|_2 = 1$, and the signs of the product, $\theta$ and $\delta$ are not.

**The forms.** As in Sections 8g to 8i, through `model.eval` with `EntryKind::Dir`, orders 2 and 3.

**The checks and the errors.** No new `EngineError` variant. In this order:
1. `BadProblem`, in this order: $\omega_1$ or $\omega_2$ not finite or at most `OMEGA_MIN` ($10^{-8}$), with $\omega_1$ tested first and the message naming `omega1` or `omega2`; then $\omega_1 \le \omega_2$, the message naming the order; then a wrong length of `p` and a delay time that is not finite and positive (Section 8d); then a wrong length of `x`.
2. $\Delta(0)$ regular, by the singular-value ratio of Section 8g with `RESONANCE_MIN`, else `Singular` naming `Delta(0)`. $H_{110000}$ and $H_{001100}$ need it.
3. The relative residuals $\|\Delta(i\omega_k)q_k\|_2/s(i\omega_k)$, with $s(i\omega_k) = \omega_k + \sum_j\|M_j\|_F$, at most `NULL_RESIDUAL_MAX` ($10^{-4}$), first for $k = 1$, else `BadProblem` naming `Delta(i omega1)` or `Delta(i omega2)`: the frequency is not a root.
4. For $k = 1$ and then $k = 2$, two tests in this order. First the null space of $\Delta(i\omega_k)$ has dimension 1: with $\sigma_1 \ge \dots \ge \sigma_n$ the singular values of $\Delta(i\omega_k)$, the ratio $\sigma_{n-1}/\sigma_1$ is at least `RESONANCE_MIN` (the test is skipped for $n = 1$), else `Singular` with the text "null space of dimension 2 or more" naming `omega1` or `omega2`. Then $|p_k\Delta'(i\omega_k)q_k|$, before the scaling, at least `PAIRING_MIN` ($10^{-8}$), else `Singular` naming which of the two roots is not simple.
5. $\Delta(2i\omega_1)$, $\Delta(2i\omega_2)$, $\Delta(i(\omega_1+\omega_2))$ and $\Delta(i(\omega_1-\omega_2))$ regular, in that order, else `Singular` naming the first singular one: `Delta(2 i omega1)`, `Delta(2 i omega2)`, `Delta(i (omega1 + omega2))` or `Delta(i (omega1 - omega2))`. At an exact 1:2 resonance ($\omega_1 = 2\omega_2$) two of them are singular, and the first named is `Delta(2 i omega2)`.

The equilibrium residual $\|f(x,\dots,x,p)\|_\infty$ is returned and not checked.

**Result.** `HopfHopf { omega1, omega2, g2100, g1011, g1110, g0021 (each [re, im]), theta: Option<f64>, delta: Option<f64>, product, sign, case: Option<HhCase> (Section 8j-2), null_residual_1, null_residual_2, equilibrium_residual }`.

**Command line.** `pc-cli dde-hh <problem.json>` reads
```json
{"model": "path.ode", "params": {"zeta": -0.01622437043055295}, "x0": [0.0, 0.0], "omega1": 7.6449266134492575, "omega2": 4.52748691549689}
```
All five keys are required and an unknown key is an error. `params` is read as in `dde-zh`: a declared parameter that it leaves out keeps the value of the model file (the same precedent, and an owner item). `x0`, `omega1` and `omega2` are used as given. The output has these keys, in alphabetical order: `case` (Section 8j-2), `delta`, `equilibrium_residual`, `g0021`, `g1011`, `g1110`, `g2100`, `null_residual_1`, `null_residual_2`, `omega1`, `omega2`, `product`, `sign` and `theta`. Each `g` is `[re, im]` and a `None` prints as `null`. Exit codes follow `dde-zh`: 2 for a bad problem file or a `BadProblem` error, 1 for a model error or any other engine error. The model is built with the JIT.

**Acceptance tests.** Rust tests. The oracle values were computed in numpy scripts written for this section, one with the formulas above and one that solves the homological equation in the eigenbasis (ODE limit) without them. The two agree to $10^{-15}$ in the ODE limit after the scale factors of the $q$'s. The engine tests run the JIT and `Evaluator::Fallback` unless a test says otherwise.

The active control system of tests 1 and 2, with $g_u = 0.1$, $g_v = 0.52$ and $\beta = 0.1$ as in the paper, and with $\zeta$ and $\tau$ as parameters:
`s' = tau*y`, `y' = tau*(-s - 2*zeta*y) + tau*(-0.1*delay(s,1) - 0.52*delay(y,1) + 0.1*delay(s,1)^3)`.
It has $n = 2$, the delay time $1$, $M_0 = \tau\begin{pmatrix}0 & 1\\ -1 & -2\zeta\end{pmatrix}$, $M_1 = \tau\begin{pmatrix}0 & 0\\ -0.1 & -0.52\end{pmatrix}$, $B = 0$ and $C(u,v,w) = (0,\ 6\tau\beta\,u_0^{\rm d}v_0^{\rm d}w_0^{\rm d})$, where the superscript d is the delayed entry. The paper's name for the state $s$ is $x$.

1. The published point. $\zeta = -0.01622437043055295$, $\tau = 5.898023025699427$, $x = (0, 0)$, $\omega_1 = 7.6449266134492575$, $\omega_2 = 4.52748691549689$ (the Newton polish of the paper's $(-0.016225, 5.89802, 4.5275, 7.6449)$ on the two determinants; the residual is $7\cdot10^{-15}$). The values are $g_{2100} = -0.09145251411388353 + 0.12143997439638596\,i$, $g_{1011} = -0.3084486219795639 + 0.40958953527678066\,i$, $g_{1110} = 0.21507073151322198 + 0.3876043302325652\,i$, $g_{0021} = 0.18134621941825654 + 0.32682540959089007\,i$, $\theta = -1.7008825602708522$, $\delta = -2.351720273599037$, product $= -0.016584567690847526$ and `sign` $-1$. Each within $10^{-8}$ relative of the numpy value (the real or imaginary part separately), and within half a unit of the last printed digit of the paper ($g_{2100} = -0.0915 + 0.1214i$, $g_{1011} = -0.3084 + 0.4096i$, $g_{1110} = 0.2151 + 0.3876i$, $g_{0021} = 0.1813 + 0.3268i$, $\theta = -1.7009$, $\delta = -2.3517$, product $-0.0166$). `null_residual_1` and `null_residual_2` are below $10^{-10}$. The case is the difficult type (negative product).
2. Invariance. The same system in the variable $\sigma$ with $s = \sigma + \alpha\sigma^2$, written to third order: `par al=0.3` and `s' = tau*y - 2*al*tau*s*y + 4*al^2*tau*s^2*y` for the first equation (the expansion of $\tau y/(1 + 2\alpha s)$ to second order in $s$) and `y' = tau*(-s - 2*zeta*y) + tau*(-0.1*delay(s,1) - 0.52*delay(y,1)) - tau*al*s^2 - tau*al*0.1*delay(s,1)^2 + tau*0.1*delay(s,1)^3`. The linear part is that of the original, so the vectors and their phases do not change.
