For $\alpha \in \{0.3, -0.7\}$ all four coefficients equal the values of test 1 within $10^{-9}$ relative (real and imaginary parts together, as complex numbers), and $\theta$ and $\delta$ within $10^{-8}$. The test is not vacuous: the part of each coefficient that comes from $B$ is large. In numpy the $B$ part of $g_{2100}$ is $1.2305$ times the total at $\alpha = 0.3$ and $6.70$ times at $\alpha = -0.7$, and $1.1838$ and $6.45$ times for $g_{0021}$. The test asserts that the engine's value with $B$ switched off (a third model, the same with the quadratic terms removed) differs from the total by a relative $0.1$ or more at both values of $\alpha$.
3. The ODE limit. Four states with $\dot x = Ax + Q(x) + K(x)$, $\omega_1 = 2.3$, $\omega_2 = 1$, $A = V\Lambda V^{-1}$ with $V = \begin{pmatrix}0.7 & 0.1 & -0.3 & 0.2\\ -0.2 & 0.8 & 0.4 & -0.5\\ 0.1 & -0.6 & 0.9 & 0.3\\ 0.5 & 0.2 & -0.1 & 0.8\end{pmatrix}$ and $\Lambda = \begin{pmatrix}0 & -\omega_1 & 0 & 0\\ \omega_1 & 0 & 0 & 0\\ 0 & 0 & 0 & -\omega_2\\ 0 & 0 & \omega_2 & 0\end{pmatrix}$, so that $A$ has the eigenvalues $\pm i\omega_1$ and $\pm i\omega_2$. To 16 digits
$A = \begin{pmatrix}0.4427996781979083 & -1.2401246983105387 & 0.829666130329847 & -0.8219026548672567\\ 3.556234915526951 & 0.5910297666934833 & 0.24372485921158504 & -1.111061946902655\\ -1.437731295253419 & -0.07087691069991953 & -0.19621882542236524 & -0.7362831858407081\\ 0.9621078037007241 & -0.3832260659694287 & 1.2868463395012069 & -0.8376106194690265\end{pmatrix}$,
$Q = (0.5x_0^2 - 0.8x_0x_1 + 0.3x_1x_2 + 0.4x_2x_3,\ 0.7x_1^2 + 0.4x_0x_2 - 0.6x_2^2 + 0.25x_0x_3,\ -0.5x_0x_1 + 0.9x_1x_3 + 0.2x_0^2 - 0.3x_3^2,\ 0.6x_0x_3 - 0.35x_1x_2 + 0.45x_2x_3)$ and $K = (0.3x_0^3 - 0.4x_0x_1x_2 + 0.2x_2^2x_3,\ 0.5x_1^3 + 0.2x_0^2x_1 - 0.3x_1x_3^2,\ -0.6x_2^3 + 0.35x_0x_2^2 + 0.25x_0x_1x_3,\ 0.4x_3^3 - 0.2x_1^2x_2 + 0.3x_0x_2x_3)$, and a fifth, decoupled state `w' = -2*w - delay(w,1)` so that the model has a delay. At $x = 0$: $g_{2100} = 0.24979441917106765 - 0.08924857884489773\,i$, $g_{1011} = 0.4529990710364762 - 0.09827404319144084\,i$, $g_{1110} = 0.14407605462369236 + 1.0374030071397333\,i$, $g_{0021} = -0.3578412055690872 - 0.4393191015668133\,i$, $\theta = -1.2659220458305134$, $\delta = 0.5767785169172422$ and product $= -0.08938673610060476$, each within $10^{-9}$ relative. The ODE route gives the same coefficients multiplied by $|T_{0}|^2 = 0.92$ for $g_{2100}$ and $g_{1110}$ and $|T_{2}|^2 = 1.045$ for $g_{1011}$ and $g_{0021}$, where $T_0$ and $T_2$ are the scales of its eigenvectors, which are not unit vectors, and the same $\theta$ and $\delta$. A unit test in `hopfhopf.rs` also builds the four-state part of this model as an ODE, without `w`, and compares its coefficients with those of `normal::double_hopf_at`, the separate routine for ODEs. That routine returns Kuznetsov's $G$, so its $G_{2100}$ and $G_{0021}$ are halved to the monomial coefficients used here.
4. A closed form. Four oscillators coupled through the radii, $\dot x_0 = -\omega_1x_1 + x_0(a r_1 + b r_2)$, $\dot x_1 = \omega_1x_0 + x_1(a r_1 + b r_2)$, $\dot x_2 = -\omega_2x_3 + x_2(c r_1 + d r_2)$, $\dot x_3 = \omega_2x_2 + x_3(c r_1 + d r_2)$ with $r_1 = x_0^2 + x_1^2$ and $r_2 = x_2^2 + x_3^2$, the decoupled `w` of test 3, $\omega_1 = 2.3$, $\omega_2 = 1$, $a = 0.7$, $b = -0.4$, $c = 0.55$ and $d = -0.9$. With $\|q_k\|_2 = 1$ one has $x_0 + ix_1 = \sqrt2\,z_1$, so $g_{2100} = 2a = 1.4$, $g_{1011} = 2b = -0.8$, $g_{1110} = 2c = 1.1$ and $g_{0021} = 2d = -1.8$, with imaginary parts below $10^{-12}$, $\theta = 4/9$, $\delta = 11/14$ and product $-2.52$. Each within $10^{-12}$ relative.
5. The scale. An internal function `hopf_hopf_scaled(model, x, p, omega1, omega2, d1, d2)` takes the two complex scales and the public function passes $1$ and $1$. For the points of tests 1 and 4, with $(d_1, d_2) \in \{(0.5, 2), (2i, 0.5), (1, e^{0.7i}), (e^{1.1i}, 3i), (-1, 1)\}$, the four coefficients scale with the weights above within $10^{-11}$ relative, $\theta$ and $\delta$ are invariant within $10^{-11}$, and the product scales with $|d_1|^2|d_2|^2$. The numpy ratios at the point of test 1 match.
6. Errors. Each of these gives the named error, for both evaluators. The models are built from oscillators `u' = -w*v`, `v' = w*u` at the frequencies named, plus the decoupled `w` of test 3, so that every $\Delta$ is known: the regular model has the oscillators at $2.3$ and $1$, where every ratio of check 5 is between $0.07$ and $0.33$ and the pairings are $1$.
   - A wrong length of `x` or `p`, $\omega_2 = 0$, NaN, $10^{-9}$, a negative or an infinite frequency, and a delay time that is not positive: `BadProblem`.
   - $\omega_1 \le \omega_2$ (the frequencies swapped, and equal): `BadProblem`, naming the order.
   - The oscillators at $2.3$ and $1$ plus `u'=0*u`: `Singular` naming `Delta(0)`.
   - The regular model with $\omega_1 = 2.31$, and with $\omega_2 = 1.01$ (the relative residuals are about $2\cdot10^{-3}$ and $3\cdot10^{-3}$): `BadProblem` naming `Delta(i omega1)` and `Delta(i omega2)`.
   - A Jordan block at $i\omega_1$ (`x1' = -w1*x2 + x3`, `x2' = w1*x1 + x4` over a second oscillator at $\omega_1$, plus the oscillator at $\omega_2$): `Singular` naming `omega1`; the same at $i\omega_2$ names `omega2`.
   - A semisimple double root: the regular model plus a second oscillator at $2.3$ (a null space of dimension 2 at $i\omega_1$, with a pairing that is not 0), and plus one at $1$ (the same at $i\omega_2$): `Singular` with the text "null space of dimension 2 or more", naming `omega1` and `omega2`.
   - The regular model plus an extra oscillator at $4.6$, at $3.3$ and at $1.3$: `Singular` naming `Delta(2 i omega1)`, `Delta(i (omega1 + omega2))` and `Delta(i (omega1 - omega2))`. For `Delta(2 i omega2)` the extra oscillator is at $2$.
   - A 1:2 resonance ($\omega_1 = 2$, $\omega_2 = 1$): `Singular` naming `Delta(2 i omega2)`, the first of the two singular ones.
7. `pc-cli dde-hh` on the data of test 1 (JIT) prints the values of test 1 within $10^{-8}$ relative and the fourteen keys in alphabetical order (`case` first, Section 8j-2). A model path is read relative to the problem file. An unknown key, a missing key, a wrong type and an unreadable problem file exit 2, and so does a pair of frequencies that is not a Hopf-Hopf point. An unreadable model and a model without a delay (stderr has `error[ir:delay]`) exit 1. `--help` and a missing argument behave as for `dde-zh`. A unit test of the problem reader names the key in each message.

## 8j-2. The unfolding case at a Hopf-Hopf point (phase E9a, step 8, part 2)

Part 2 sorts the point of Section 8j into one of the twelve cases of the Guckenheimer and Holmes lettering: Ia, Ib, II, III, IVa, IVb, V, VIa, VIb, VIIa, VIIb and VIII. The case is a function of the four real parts $\operatorname{Re} g_{2100}$, $\operatorname{Re} g_{1011}$, $\operatorname{Re} g_{1110}$ and $\operatorname{Re} g_{0021}$, which Section 8j already returns. Part 2 adds one result field and one output key. It does not compute the predictors, the bifurcation curves of the unfolding or any fifth-order term, and it says nothing about the dynamics near a difficult case. It changes no C function, no MATLAB function and no IR entry. The ways in are `dde::hopf_hopf`, the pure function `dde::unfolding_case` and `pc-cli dde-hh`.

**What is not verified.**
- The lettering and the table are Guckenheimer and Holmes, Section 7.5 (Table 7.5.2). I have not read the book. I have its table from two printed copies: the case table of Du, Niu, Guo and Wei, arXiv 1804.03775, and Table 1 of Buono and Bélair, J. Differential Equations 189 (2003) 234–266, which reproduces it. The two copies agree, but one may derive from the other, so I count them as one source. The volume and pages of the second come from another paper's reference list, and I did not open the article.
- What the sources do not carry alone, I derived (next paragraph): the sign pattern of each column follows from the rescaling of the amplitude system, so only the Roman numerals and the letters a and b rest on the printed tables. The letter a is $e_0 > 0$ and the letter b is $e_0 < 0$ in all four pairs.
- The order of the two oscillators. The table is for a normal form in which oscillator 1 is the one with $A = \operatorname{Re} g_{2100}$. Section 8j puts the larger frequency first ($\omega_1 > \omega_2$), and I did not find which order Guckenheimer and Holmes or Du et al. assume. Listing the two oscillators the other way round maps $(A, B, C, D)$ to $(D, C, B, A)$ and exchanges II with III and V with VIII. The other eight cases keep their letter (test 4). The label of a point is therefore conditional on this order, and that is an owner item.
- The split into simple and difficult cases is the sign of the product of Section 8j. Two sources agree on that (STATUS, P22). For a difficult case (V to VIII) the third-order terms may not decide the unfolding. STATUS (P22) lists what a source search found about the fifth-order terms, and none of it is computed. The label names the case of the cubic amplitude system and no more.
- Kuznetsov's book counts 5 simple and 6 difficult cases and uses another numbering. I did not map the two numberings case by case.
- The decision at a boundary is an exact comparison of computed numbers, as for `sign` in Section 8j. Near a boundary (a coefficient near 0 or $\theta\delta$ near 1) the label is not reliable, and the function reports no margin.

**The amplitude system.** Truncated at third order and written in the moduli $r_k = |z_k|$, the normal form of Section 8j gives
$$\dot r_1 = r_1(\nu_1 + A r_1^2 + B r_2^2),\qquad \dot r_2 = r_2(\nu_2 + C r_1^2 + D r_2^2),$$
with $A = \operatorname{Re} g_{2100}$, $B = \operatorname{Re} g_{1011}$, $C = \operatorname{Re} g_{1110}$, $D = \operatorname{Re} g_{0021}$, and $\nu_1$ and $\nu_2$ the linear coefficients that the unfolding parameters move. For $A \ne 0$ and $D \ne 0$ let $\varepsilon_1 = \operatorname{sgn} A$ and $\varepsilon_2 = \operatorname{sgn} D$, and put $\hat r_1 = \sqrt{|A|}\,r_1$, $\hat r_2 = \sqrt{|D|}\,r_2$ and $\hat t = \varepsilon_1 t$. Then
$$\frac{d\hat r_1}{d\hat t} = \hat r_1(\hat\nu_1 + \hat r_1^2 + b_0\hat r_2^2),\qquad \frac{d\hat r_2}{d\hat t} = \hat r_2(\hat\nu_2 + c_0\hat r_1^2 + d_0\hat r_2^2),$$
with
$$d_0 = \varepsilon_1\varepsilon_2,\qquad b_0 = \varepsilon_1\frac{B}{|D|} = d_0\theta,\qquad c_0 = \frac{C}{A} = \delta,\qquad e_0 = d_0 - b_0c_0 = d_0(1 - \theta\delta).$$
I derived this and did not copy it. The four numbers have the signs
$$\operatorname{sgn} d_0 = \operatorname{sgn}(AD),\quad \operatorname{sgn} b_0 = \operatorname{sgn}(AB),\quad \operatorname{sgn} c_0 = \operatorname{sgn}(AC),\quad \operatorname{sgn} e_0 = \operatorname{sgn}(AD - BC).$$
Two of the four are $d_0$ and $e_0$ from the printed tables, and the last identity follows from $e_0 = d_0(AD - BC)/(AD)$.

**The table.** The case is read from the signs of $d_0$, $b_0$, $c_0$ and $e_0$.

| | Ia | Ib | II | III | IVa | IVb | V | VIa | VIb | VIIa | VIIb | VIII |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| $d_0$ | + | + | + | + | + | + | − | − | − | − | − | − |
| $b_0$ | + | + | + | − | − | − | + | + | + | − | − | − |
| $c_0$ | + | + | − | + | − | − | + | − | − | + | + | − |
| $e_0$ | + | − | + | + | + | − | − | + | − | + | − | − |

The columns are the twelve sign combinations that can occur. Of the eight choices of $(d_0, b_0, c_0)$, four have $\operatorname{sgn}(b_0c_0) = -d_0$, which forces $e_0$ to have the sign of $d_0$ and gives one case each (II, III, V, VIII). The other four leave $e_0$ free and give two cases each. That is 4 + 8 = 12. The cases Ia to IVb have $d_0 = +1$ (simple) and V to VIII have $d_0 = -1$ (difficult).

**The function.** `dde::unfolding_case(re_g2100, re_g1011, re_g1110, re_g0021) -> Option<HhCase>` returns `Some` when none of the four values is 0 (a negative zero is a zero) and none is NaN, and $AD - BC \ne 0$. Mathematically that is: `sign` is not 0, $B$ and $C$ are not 0, and $\theta\delta \ne 1$. The function tests the four values and the two products, not $\theta\delta$. Otherwise it returns `None`: the point lies on a boundary of the twelve regions or is not classified by this table. `HhCase` has the twelve variants named as above, `name()` returns the label as a string (`"Ia"`, `"VIIb"`, and so on) and `simple()` is true for Ia to IVb. The comparison $AD - BC \gtrless 0$ is made on the products as computed in `f64`.

**Result.** `HopfHopf` gains the field `case: Option<HhCase>`, the value of `unfolding_case` on the four real parts. Every other field is unchanged.

**Command line.** `pc-cli dde-hh` prints fourteen keys, in alphabetical order, so `case` comes first. Its value is the label as a string, or `null` when `case` is `None`. The input keys and the exit codes do not change.

**Acceptance tests.** Rust tests, and one CLI test.
1. The table. For each case, a quadruple $(A, B, C, D)$ gives that case through `unfolding_case`: Ia $(1, \tfrac12, \tfrac12, 1)$, Ib $(1, 2, 2, 1)$, II $(1, 1, -1, 1)$, III $(1, -1, 1, 1)$, IVa $(1, -\tfrac12, -\tfrac12, 1)$, IVb $(1, -2, -2, 1)$, V $(1, 1, 1, -1)$, VIa $(1, 2, -2, -1)$, VIb $(1, \tfrac12, -\tfrac12, -1)$, VIIa $(1, -2, 2, -1)$, VIIb $(1, -\tfrac12, \tfrac12, -1)$ and VIII $(1, -1, -1, -1)$. The twelve results are distinct, and `simple()` is true exactly for the first six.
2. Boundaries. A zero in any of the four places (and $-0.0$) gives `None`, and so does NaN. So does $AD = BC$, for example $(1, 2, \tfrac12, 1)$ and $(-1, 3, 3, -9)$.
3. The rescaling. Over all 1296 quadruples with entries in $\{\pm\tfrac14, \pm1, \pm4\}$, which are exact in `f64`, the test computes $d_0$, $b_0$, $c_0$ and $e_0$ from the rescaled system above (by $\varepsilon_1 B/|D|$, $C/A$, $\varepsilon_1\varepsilon_2$ and $d_0 - b_0c_0$, not by the product formulas) and reads the case from a second copy of the table typed into the test. It agrees with `unfolding_case` on every quadruple, with `None` exactly where $e_0 = 0$. All twelve cases occur.
4. Symmetries. On the same grid, the quadruple $(D, C, B, A)$ gives the same case except that II and III, and V and VIII, exchange. Multiplying $(A, C)$ by $s_1 > 0$ and $(B, D)$ by $s_2 > 0$ keeps the case.
5. The engine. For each of the twelve quadruples of test 1, the closed-form model of test 4 of Section 8j with $(a, b, c, d) = (A, B, C, D)$ gives $g = (2A, 2B, 2C, 2D)$ and the case of test 1, for both evaluators. At the published point of test 1 of Section 8j (with $\theta = -1.7009$, $\delta = -2.3517$, $\theta\delta = 4.0 > 1$) the case is VIa. At the ODE-limit point of test 3 the case is V. At $(a, b, c, d) = (0.7, -0.4, 0.55, -0.9)$ it is VIIb, and at $(0.7, -0.4, 0.55, 0.9)$ it is III. The purely linear model of the zero-denominator unit test of `hopfhopf.rs` (two oscillators at $2.3$ and $1$ with no coupling, plus the decoupled `w`) has $B$ and $C$ exactly $0$ and gives `None`. The scaled function of test 5 of Section 8j, with the five scale pairs listed there, keeps the case at the points of tests 1 and 4.
6. `pc-cli dde-hh` on the data of test 1 of Section 8j prints `"case": "VIa"` and fourteen keys in alphabetical order. On the linear model of test 5 it prints `"case": null`, with `theta`, `delta` null and `sign` 0 as before.

## 8k. The HH label on a Hopf curve of delay equilibria (phase E9a, step 9)

Step 9 adds a second option to the Hopf curve of Section 8f. With it, the run computes the characteristic roots of the equilibrium (Section 8c) at every point with $\kappa > 0$ and labels a sign change of a root test as `HH`, a Hopf-Hopf point, where a second pair of roots reaches the imaginary axis while the first pair is on it. It does not compute the normal-form coefficients along the curve (Section 8j computes them at one point), does not sort the point into one of the unfolding cases, does not follow the second Hopf curve, and does not look for a real root (the `ZH` label of Section 8f covers it). It changes no C function, no MATLAB function and no IR entry. The ways in are `dde::continue_hopf_with` and the key `hh` of `pc-cli dde-hopf`. Without the option nothing changes: `dde::continue_hopf` and `dde::continue_hopf_with_lyapunov` are the functions of Sections 8f and 8g-2, and a problem file without the key prints what it printed before.

**What is not verified.**
- The test and its constant are my own choices. I consulted no source for a root-based Hopf-Hopf test on a Hopf curve. The only checks are the closed form and the oracle table of the tests below.
- A root that is not found is not seen. A crossing is missed when the second pair lies outside the window $\operatorname{Re}\lambda \ge r_{\min}$ or beyond the degree $n$ of Section 8c, or when its candidate is in the list `unrefined` of Section 8c. The warnings below count the second case only; they cannot see the first.
- The test is the largest real part among the roots with $\operatorname{Im} > 0$ that are not the critical root. If a second pair stays in $\operatorname{Re} > 0$ while a third crosses, only the largest is followed, and the third crossing is missed. If the pair that is tracked stops being the largest, the test is still continuous (a maximum of continuous functions), but it has a kink there.
- A pair that meets the real axis and splits into two real roots leaves the set of roots with $\operatorname{Im} > 0$, so the test jumps. The locator drops a jump (`locate.rs` keeps a codimension-2 zero only if its value is at most `POLE_TOL` of the smaller end value, and it gives no warning). A crossing hidden in the same step is missed.
- A NaN on either side of a step skips the test (Section 8f). A pair that goes from below $r_{\min}$ to above 0 in one step, with no value in between, is missed.
- The label is not tested at a resonance ($\omega_1 : \omega_2 = 1:2$, $1:3$) or for a double critical root. A second pair within `CRIT_TOL` $(1 + \omega)$ of $i\omega$ is taken for the critical root and is missed.
- The `HH` label says that two pairs are on the axis. It does not say whether the point is a Hopf-Hopf point in the sense of Bosschaert, Janssens and Kuznetsov: that needs the nonresonance conditions of Section 8j, which the run does not check beyond the roots it finds.
- `COLLAPSE_MARGIN` has no source. The two sizes of the floor come from my analysis of a nilpotent $2\times2$ block and decide only which losses are counted. They are checked only by a unit test that scans such blocks at $s = 1, 10, 10^3$ with one rounding error of up to four units in one entry. I did not test a larger block or a model whose Jordan block is spread over several entries. On the BT model of Section 8f, a scratch run that is not kept in the repo lost the pair for $\sqrt{\kappa} \le 6.44\cdot10^{-9}$. If the corrector accepts a point at a residual near `tol_f` rather than near rounding, the pair may be lost over a wider range, roughly $\sqrt{\mathrm{tol\_f}}$ in $\sqrt\kappa$, which is above $\Omega$ for $\mathrm{tol\_f} = 10^{-12}$. I did not measure that case.
- When $s$ is large, $\Omega$ can cover the whole curve, and then `hh_lost` never fires along it, since a lost pair is NaN and not counted there. Test 12 can therefore check only `hh_unrefined` on the stiff model. The start check has no floor and still guards the start. Computing $s$ from the block that holds the pair is a design choice for the owner.
- The cost. Every evaluation of the test computes the roots of Section 8c, at every point and at every trial of the locator, so a run with the option takes several times as long as one without. The step of the curve is not changed to follow the test.

**The roots at a point.** For a point of the curve with $\kappa > 0$ and $\omega = \sqrt\kappa$ let $\Lambda$ be the root set of `characteristic_roots` for the linearization of the point (`dde::linearize` at $(u, p)$, with $p$ the declared parameters of the point), with $n$ and $r_{\min}$ from the option. A root of $\Lambda$ is the critical root when $|\lambda - i\omega| \le \mathrm{CRIT\_TOL}\,(1 + \omega)$ with $\mathrm{CRIT\_TOL} = 10^{-6}$. The constant has no source. It is far above the accuracy of an accepted root ($10^{-12}$ in the scaled residual) and far below the spacing of roots in the examples. `dde::hopf_pairs(&RootSet, omega) -> HopfPairs { critical: usize, second: Option<CharRoot> }` returns the number of roots of $\Lambda$ with $\operatorname{Im}\lambda > 0$ within that distance of $i\omega$ (roots with $\operatorname{Im} \le 0$ are not looked at, and a root with $\operatorname{Im} = 0$ is not counted) and the root with $\operatorname{Im} > 0$ and the largest real part among the others. If two such roots have the same real part, the one with the larger imaginary part is `second`.

**The test.** $h = \operatorname{Re}\lambda_2$ for `second` $= \lambda_2$ when `critical` is exactly 1, and NaN otherwise. It is also NaN for $\kappa \le 0$, for $\sqrt{\kappa} \le \omega_{\min} = 10^{-8}$ (the constant of the start check; the locator probes $\kappa \approx 10^{-17}$ at a BT point, where nothing can be linearized), and when the linearization or `characteristic_roots` returns an error. The floor $\Omega$ below decides only whether such a NaN counts as lost. A sign change of $h$ between two points is the label `HH`. The root of the second pair has $\operatorname{Re}\lambda_2 < 0$ before the crossing and $> 0$ after it, or the reverse.

**The floor near BT.** Let $s$ be the largest absolute entry of $A_0$ and of every $A_j$ of the linearization, and at least 1. Near a Bogdanov-Takens point the pair at $\pm i\omega$ comes from a nilpotent block of size $s$, and two things lose it:
- One rounding of a matrix entry moves $\omega^2$ by about $\varepsilon s^2$. A pair with $\omega$ near $\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,s$ is then two real roots, and `hopf_pairs` finds no critical root.
- The refinement of Section 8c starts from a vector found by inverse iteration at a point $\mathrm{SHIFT} = 10^{-8}$ from the root. For the block $\begin{pmatrix}0 & s\\ -\omega^2/s & 0\end{pmatrix}$ the ratio of the smallest to the largest singular value of that matrix is about $\mathrm{SHIFT}\,\omega/s^2$, so its LU factorization reports a singular matrix once the ratio is near $\varepsilon$, that is for $\omega$ below about $\varepsilon s^2/\mathrm{SHIFT}$. I saw this at $s = 10$, $\omega = 3.74\cdot10^{-6}$; at $s = 1$ and the same $\omega$ the root is refined. The root is then listed in `unrefined`.

The floor is $\Omega = \mathrm{COLLAPSE\_MARGIN}\cdot\max(\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,s,\ \varepsilon s^2/\mathrm{SHIFT})$ with $\varepsilon = 2^{-52}$ and $\mathrm{COLLAPSE\_MARGIN} = 10$. It is $2.2\cdot10^{-7}$ at $s = 1$ and $2.2\cdot10^{-5}$ at $s = 10$. The floor never switches the test off. For $\sqrt{\kappa} \le \Omega$ the roots are computed as everywhere else. If `critical` is exactly 1 the value $h$ is used. If it is not, or if `characteristic_roots` fails, the test is NaN and the evaluation is not counted in `hh_lost`, because losing the pair there says nothing about a second crossing. The reason for this order is that $s$ is the largest entry of all the matrices, not of the block that holds the pair: a model with a stiff block elsewhere has a high $\Omega$ at every point, and a floor that skipped the test would hide every `HH` point below it without a warning (test 12). An `HH` point within $\Omega$ of a BT point is detected only if the roots still show the pair at $\pm i\omega$. The first version used the fixed $\omega_{\min}$ for this, a factor of 1.6 above the loss measured on the BT model of Section 8f. The second mechanism was found by the unit test of the floor.

**The option.** `HopfExtras { lyapunov: bool, hh: Option<RootOptions> }` and `continue_hopf_with(model, setup, opts, cancel, extras)`. The two existing functions are wrappers: `continue_hopf` has `lyapunov: false` and `hh: None`, and `continue_hopf_with_lyapunov` has `hh: None`. `RootOptions { n, re_min }` is that of Section 8c, and the option has no default for either field.
- `re_min` must be finite and negative, else `BadProblem` naming `re_min`. The window has to reach below 0 for the test to take negative values, and the crossing is a change of sign of a value in the window.
- `n` and the order limit of Section 8c are checked by `characteristic_roots`. One call at the polished start point, before the run, returns its error unchanged (so $n = 0$ and $d(n + 1) > 4000$ are the `BadProblem` of Section 8c). If that root set does not have exactly one root within `CRIT_TOL` $(1 + \omega)$ of $i\omega$, the start is `BadProblem` with a message that names `hh` and says to raise `n`, since a Hopf point has one.

The problem of Section 8f gets the test as its last entry. The list of tests is $(\kappa, \det\Delta(0), [\ell_1], [h])$ and `codim2_labels` is `["BT", "ZH"]`, `["BT", "ZH", "GH"]`, `["BT", "ZH", "HH"]` or `["BT", "ZH", "GH", "HH"]`. A run without `hh` has the lists of Sections 8f and 8g-2 and evaluates no root.

**The result.** A `DdeHopfPoint` gets `hh: Option<f64>`, `Some` for a finite $h$ and `None` otherwise, and always `None` without the option. A `DdeHopfSpecial` gets `omega2: Option<f64>`. It is `Some(Im λ_2)` only for an `HH` special, computed after the run at the parameters and the state of the special, as `l2` is, and it is `None` where $h$ is NaN there. Because $\operatorname{Re}\lambda_2 = 0$ at the located point up to the locator tolerance, $i\,\mathrm{omega2}$ is the second critical root, and `kappa` of the special is $\omega_1^2$.

**The warnings.** `DdeHopfBranch.warnings` gets up to two strings with the option, each with a count, and none without it:
- `hh_unrefined`: the number of evaluations whose root set has a non-empty `unrefined`. The message says that the second pair may have been missed and that $n$ should be raised.
- `hh_lost`: the number of evaluations at $\sqrt{\kappa} > \omega_{\min}$ where the linearization failed, and of those with $\sqrt{\kappa} > \Omega$ where `characteristic_roots` failed or `critical` is not exactly 1. The message says that the test was NaN there. At $\sqrt{\kappa} \le \Omega$ a failure of the roots or a `critical` other than 1 is NaN and not counted. `hh_unrefined` counts every evaluation above $\omega_{\min}$ whose root set has an `unrefined` entry, except one at $\sqrt{\kappa} \le \Omega$ where `critical` is not 1.

A point with `critical` equal to 1 and no non-critical root with $\operatorname{Im} > 0$ is legitimate (all of them below the window) and is not counted. Whether the counts are quiet on a run with a good $n$ is part of test 6.

**Command line.** `pc-cli dde-hopf` takes one more optional key, `hh`, an object with the two required keys `n` (an integer, at least 1) and `re_min` (a number). Any other key inside it, a missing key, a type that is not a number, a non-integer `n`, or a value of `hh` that is not an object (`true`, `null`, an array) is exit 2 with `"hh"` in the message, and so is an error from the checks above. With the key, every point of the output has `"hh"` after `"l1"` (or after `"omega"` without `lyapunov`): a number, or `null` where $h$ is not defined. An `HH` special has `"omega2"`, a number or `null`, after `"index"`. Without the key the output has neither and is the output of Sections 8f and 8g-2.

**Acceptance tests.** Rust tests with the models and numbers below. The numbers were computed with numpy by Newton's method on the determinant, at 16 digits, with residuals below $3\cdot10^{-16}$ in the determinant, and the root lists were scanned by Newton starts on a grid over $-3 \le \operatorname{Re} \le 1$, $0 < \operatorname{Im} \le 15$ to check that no larger root is missing. Every engine test runs with the JIT and with `Evaluator::Fallback`, and with $n = 30$ and $r_{\min} = -3$ unless it says otherwise.

The coupled model is `par a=1.4, b=1.2, c=1, d=0.1`, `x' = c*y - a*delay(x,1)`, `y' = -d*x - b*delay(y,1.5)`. The origin is its equilibrium for every $(a, b)$. $\Delta(\lambda) = \begin{pmatrix}\lambda + a e^{-\lambda} & -c\\ d & \lambda + b e^{-1.5\lambda}\end{pmatrix}$ and $\det\Delta = (\lambda + a e^{-\lambda})(\lambda + b e^{-1.5\lambda}) + cd$. The Hopf curve $\det\Delta(i\omega_1) = 0$ in the free parameters `["a", "b"]` has these points, and at each one the root of the second pair with the largest real part is $\lambda_2$:

| $b$ | $a$ | $\omega_1$ | $\operatorname{Re}\lambda_2$ | $\operatorname{Im}\lambda_2$ |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0.5 | 1.4785748560820808 | 1.5556781375325988 | −0.39165142207365444 | 0.7958437106116013 |
| 0.8 | 1.4607797466651513 | 1.5397916943606513 | −0.18239464598238153 | 0.9908174631714135 |
| 1.0 | 1.4534871707126467 | 1.5269665249416076 | −0.08089123463134608 | 1.0716178583676754 |
| 1.1 | 1.452883435032108 | 1.5207071844004396 | −0.03599432482169971 | 1.105192730266564 |
| 1.19 | 1.4547106602831545 | 1.5157479954733453 | 0.0023306868371833615 | 1.1324322809175285 |
| 1.25 | 1.4572787145041843 | 1.513025660156989 | 0.027062503671456562 | 1.1491131389877451 |
| 1.4 | 1.4679734804429971 | 1.5089603975736543 | 0.08654184735983078 | 1.1856600816673084 |

The Hopf-Hopf point is at $b^* = 1.1844194631859732$, $a^* = 1.454526952022688$, $\omega_1 = 1.5160279413134377$ and $\omega_2 = 1.1308210684126256$ (both determinants vanish, residual $1.2\cdot10^{-16}$).

The decoupled model is the same text with $c = d = 0$. The Hopf curve is $a = \pi/2$ with $\omega_1 = \pi/2$ for every $b$, and the second pair solves $\lambda + b e^{-1.5\lambda} = 0$. Its root with $\operatorname{Im} > 0$ and the largest real part is $(-0.3449703636863726,\ 0.764662957019079)$ at $b = 0.5$, $(-0.02185582394371507,\ 1.0330958822334395)$ at $b = 1$ and $(0.06480995836586662,\ 1.086902617617715)$ at $b = 1.2$. The Hopf-Hopf point is at $b = \pi/3$ with $\omega_2 = \pi/3$, since $e^{-1.5 i\omega_2} = e^{-i\pi/2} = -i$ gives $i\omega_2 + b(-i) = 0$ there.

1. `hopf_pairs` on hand-made root sets, with conjugates included. A set with a root at $i\omega$ and others: `critical` is 1 and `second` is the root with $\operatorname{Im} > 0$ and the largest real part, and a real root with a larger real part and a root with $\operatorname{Im} < 0$ are not taken. A root at $i\omega + 5\cdot10^{-7}(1 + \omega)$ counts as critical, and one at $i\omega + 2\cdot10^{-6}(1 + \omega)$ does not. Two roots within the distance give `critical` $= 2$. An empty set and a set with only real roots give `critical` $= 0$ and `second` `None`. A set with the critical root only gives `critical` $= 1$ and `second` `None`. Two roots with equal real part give the one with the larger imaginary part.
2. The oracle table. For every row of the table of the coupled model, build the `LinearDde` of the model at $(a, b)$ (the matrices of `dde::linearize` at the origin), compute the roots, and check that `critical` is 1, that the critical root is within $10^{-9}$ of $i\omega_1$, and that `second` has real and imaginary part within $10^{-9}$ of the row. The same for the decoupled model at $a = \pi/2$ and the three values of $b$ above, within $10^{-9}$.
3. The `HH` point of the coupled model. Start $a = 1.46$, $b = 0.8$, `x0 = [0.001, 0.001]`, $\omega = 1.54$, free `["a", "b"]`, `bounds = [0.5, 1.4]` (on $b$), `hmax = 0.05`, `tol_x = tol_f = 10^{-12}`, `max_newton = 20`, `direction` $= 1$, `hh` as above. The polished start has $a$ within $10^{-9}$ of the $b = 0.8$ row. `stop` is `bounds`. Exactly one `HH` special and no other, with $b$ within $10^{-6}$ of $b^*$, $a$ within $10^{-6}$ of $a^*$, `kappa` within $10^{-6}$ of $\omega_1^2$, `omega2` within $10^{-6}$ of $\omega_2$, and $|u| \le 10^{-8}$. Every point has `hh` `Some`. The `hh` of the points with $b$ below the special is negative and that of the points from its `index` on is positive. The $\operatorname{Re}\lambda_2$ of the roots at the special is within $10^{-8}$ of 0.
4. The other direction. The start of test 3 with `direction` $= -1$: no special, `stop` is `bounds` with the last $b = 0.5$ within $10^{-8}$ and $a$ within $10^{-8}$ of the first row, and `hh` is `Some` and negative on every point.
5. The decoupled closed form. The decoupled model with start $a = 1.57$, $b = 0.8$, `x0 = [0, 0]`, $\omega = 1.55$, `bounds = [0.5, 1.2]`, `direction` $= 1$ and the other settings of test 3. On every point $a = \pi/2$ and $\omega_1 = \pi/2$ within $10^{-8}$. Exactly one `HH` special at $b = \pi/3$ within $10^{-6}$ with `omega2` within $10^{-6}$ of $\pi/3$ and $a = \pi/2$ within $10^{-8}$.
6. The warnings. The runs of tests 3, 4 and 5 have no warning string of `hh_unrefined` or `hh_lost` with $n = 30$. A run of test 3 with `n = 30` and `re_min = -3` that is checked for the counts reports 0 for both. (This test is written first and the SPEC is changed if the counts are not 0, with the reason.)
7. Negative controls. The `BT` and `ZH` runs of test 5 of Section 8g-2 (its models, starts and options), each with `hh` $= \{n: 30, r_{\min}: -3\}$ and each with both evaluators. The label list is `["BT"]` and `["ZH"]`, and no `HH`. `hh` is `None` wherever $\kappa \le 0$. The points are identical bit for bit to those of the same run without `hh`, and so are the specials and the warnings.
8. Off equals absent. The coupled run of test 3 with `continue_hopf` has no special and `hh` is `None` at every point. `continue_hopf_with` with `hh: None` gives the same number of points and the same `alpha`, `x` and `kappa` bit for bit as `continue_hopf`, and the same warnings. `continue_hopf_with` with `hh` gives the same points as that run, and the same `alpha`, `x` and `kappa` bit for bit.
9. With `lyapunov`. The run of test 3 with `lyapunov: true` and `hh`: every point has `l1` and `hh`, there is exactly one `HH` special with the values of test 3, and any other special is `GH`. The `HH` is found at the same $b$ as in test 3 within $10^{-8}$ (the index of `hh` among the tests moves by one).
10. Option errors. Each of these is `BadProblem` for the coupled model with the start of test 3: `re_min` equal to $0$, $0.5$, NaN and $-\infty$ ($\pm\infty$ are not finite), $n = 0$, and $n = 2000$ (order $2 \cdot 2001 > 4000$). The message of the `re_min` cases names `re_min`. A start without exactly one root at $i\omega$ is `BadProblem` naming `hh` (the check does not use $\Omega$, so a start below $\Omega$ whose pair is lost is refused too), tested with the smallest $n$ for which the root set at the start is deterministic (recorded in the test), and if no such $n$ exists the check is not tested and this item says so.
11. `pc-cli dde-hopf` on the coupled model with the data of test 3 and `"hh": {"n": 30, "re_min": -3.0}` (JIT) prints one special, `HH`, with $b$ within $10^{-6}$ of $b^*$ and `"omega2"` within $10^{-6}$ of $\omega_2$, and every point has a numeric `"hh"`. The same file without the key prints no `"hh"`, no special and no `"omega2"`, and has the same points as with the key. `"hh": true`, `"hh": null`, `"hh": [30, -3]`, `{"n": 30}`, `{"re_min": -3}`, `{"n": 30.5, "re_min": -3}`, `{"n": 30, "re_min": "x"}`, `{"n": 30, "re_min": -3, "m": 1}`, `{"n": 30, "re_min": 1}` and `{"n": 0, "re_min": -3}` each exit 2 and the message has `"hh"`. `--help` lists the key.
12. A stiff block elsewhere. The decoupled model of test 5 with a third state `w' = -10000*w` (`x0 = [0, 0, 0]`, the start, bounds and options of test 5, both evaluators). The largest matrix entry is $10^4$, so $\Omega \approx 22$ at every point, far above $\omega_1 = \pi/2$. Exactly one special, `HH`, with $b$ within $10^{-6}$ of $\pi/3$ and `omega2` within $10^{-6}$ of $\pi/3$, and no warning with `hh` in it.

## 8l. Periodic solutions of a delay equation: the boundary value problem (phase E9b, step 1)

Step 1 of E9b computes one periodic orbit of $x'(t) = f(x(t), x(t-\tau_1), \dots, x(t-\tau_S); p)$, a model lowered by Section 8d, as the solution of a periodic boundary value problem by collocation. It corrects an explicit start by Newton's method at a fixed parameter, and it starts from a Hopf point (Section 8e) with the parameter free. It does not continue a branch of cycles or label LPC, PD and NS points. Floquet multipliers are Section 8m, and the continuation of a branch of cycles is Section 8n. The labels are a later step of E9b and get their own section. Section 8s puts a MATLAB front end in front of the delay engine in slices, and a command that its slices do not name has none.

**What is not verified.**
- The formulation is that of the DDE-BIFTOOL manual (Sieber et al., arXiv 1406.7144, Section 3.1.1, equations (15) and (16), BSD-2-Clause): time rescaled to $s = t/T$, a piecewise polynomial on a mesh, Gauss collocation, the delayed argument evaluated by the polynomial at a shifted point, periodicity and an integral phase condition. I read the manual. I did not read the papers it cites for the method, Engelborghs, Luzyanina, in 't Hout and Roose, *SIAM J. Sci. Comput.* 22(5), 2000, and Engelborghs, Luzyanina and Roose, *ACM Trans. Math. Softw.* 28(1), 2002. I wrote the rows and every Jacobian column below from the definition and checked them against central differences (test 1). I ported no code.
- The Jacobian is dense. A delayed argument couples intervals that are far apart in the mesh, so the matrix is not block banded and I use the dense bordered solve of `Problem` (Section 5). That limits the size: $\dim \le$ `MAX_DIM` $= 3000$. A larger problem is `BadProblem`. The limit is my own choice for memory and time (a $3000\times3000$ matrix is 72 MB). A sparse or condensed solve is not written.
- At a mesh node a shifted point has a one-sided derivative. The shifted point of a collocation point $s$ is $s' = \operatorname{frac}(s - \tau_k/T)$, and the polynomial of the interval that contains $s'$ is $C^0$ at its ends. I take the interval $j' = \min(\lfloor N s'\rfloor, N-1)$, so a point on a node uses the interval to its right. Then $\partial f/\partial T$ and $\partial f/\partial\alpha$ (which contain $u'(s')$) are the derivative from the right, and Newton's method is a semismooth iteration there. This happens for sure when $N\tau_k/T$ is a half-integer and $m$ is odd, because the central Gauss node is at $\zeta = \tfrac12$. For the oracle below it happens for $N = 10$, $m = 3$ and for no other mesh of the table. When $N\tau/T$ is an integer no shifted point is on a node, because the Gauss nodes are interior. I measure that case (the table below). I do not prove that the iteration converges there.
- The tolerance tables below are measured from the code. They are not derived from the order of the method.
- The oracle values for the multipliers (file `references/dde-cycle-oracle.json`) are numerical, about nine digits, from a solver of my own. They are not used in this step.
- The phase condition is normalized by $\|\text{phase row}\|_2$ in this problem (my choice, so that the scale of the row does not depend on $N$).
- The Hopf start does not check that the point it is given is an equilibrium of the model, only that $\Delta(i\omega)$ has a null vector (below). The equilibrium is the responsibility of the caller, as for `dde-hopf`.
- The result is a periodic orbit of the discretized problem, not a proof that the delay equation has one near it. Whether the orbit found is stable is not computed here.

**Unknowns.** The model has $n$ states and $S$ delay slots (`DdeModel.delays`). Slot $k$ delays state $\sigma_k$ by the time $\tau_k$, which is `params[j]` for `DelayTime::Param(j)` and a literal otherwise. The mesh has $N$ intervals (`ntst`) of $m$ Gauss points (`ncol`, 2 to 7, as in Section 7). The orbit is the polynomial $u$ of degree $m$ on each interval, given by its values at the $Nm + 1$ base points $s_q = q/(Nm)$, $q = 0, \dots, Nm$. The unknown vector is

$$x = (u_0, u_1, \dots, u_{Nm}, T, \alpha),\qquad \dim = n(Nm+1) + 2,$$

with $u_q \in \mathbb R^n$ at index $qn$, $T$ at index $\dim - 2$ and the free parameter $\alpha$ (`params[free]`) at index $\dim - 1$. Base point $l$ of interval $j$ is $q = jm + l$.

**Rows.** There are $\dim - 1$ rows.
1. Collocation, $Nmn$ rows ordered by (interval $j$, Gauss point $i$, component $c$). With $h = 1/N$, $z_i$ the Gauss nodes, $s_{ji} = (j + z_i)h$, $D_{il} = L_l'(z_i)$ and $V_{il} = L_l(z_i)$ for the Lagrange basis on the base points of the interval:
$$r_{jic} = N\sum_{l=0}^{m} D_{il}\,u_{j,l,c} - T\,f_c\big(y_0, y_1, \dots, y_S;\ p\big),\qquad y_0 = \sum_l V_{il}\,u_{j,l},\quad y_k = u(s'_{ji,k})_{\sigma_k}\ \text{(the other components of that evaluation are not used)}.$$
$y_0$ is the state at the Gauss point and $y_k$ is the delayed state of slot $k$, the polynomial of the interval $j'$ evaluated at $\zeta' = N s' - j'$. In the evaluation of the model, `params` holds the declared parameters with $\alpha$ in place, then $y_1, \dots, y_S$, then $t = 0$ (Section 8d). `build_dde` refuses a model that reads $t$.
2. Periodicity, $n$ rows: $u_{Nm} - u_0$.
3. Phase, one row: $\hat\pi\cdot u - \hat\pi\cdot u_{\rm ref}$, where $\pi$ is the integral phase row $\int_0^1 u_{\rm ref}'(s)\cdot u(s)\,ds$ written on the base points (the row of `CycleProblem::set_reference`, used here as the free function `phase_row`) and $\hat\pi = \pi/\|\pi\|_2$. For a periodic $u_{\rm ref}$ the integral of $u'_{\rm ref}$ is zero and $\pi\cdot u_{\rm ref}$ is zero up to rounding (Gauss quadrature is exact to degree $2m-1$).

**The shifted point.** $s'_k = \operatorname{frac}(s - \tau_k/T)$ with $\operatorname{frac}(y) = y - \lfloor y\rfloor$. A delay longer than the period is covered by the fractional part. The interval is $j'_k = \min(\lfloor N s'_k\rfloor, N-1)$ (the minimum covers $s'_k$ that rounds to 1) and $\zeta'_k = N s'_k - j'_k$.

**The Jacobian.** Let $A = \partial f/\partial y_0$ (`JacU`, the lagged values held fixed), $J_k = \partial f/\partial y_k$ (the column $n_{\rm user} + k$ of `JacP`) and $P = \partial f/\partial\alpha$ (the column `free` of `JacP`, which holds only the explicit dependence on $\alpha$). Write $u'_k = N\sum_l L_l'(\zeta'_k)\,u_{j'_k,l}$ for the derivative $du/ds$ at the shifted point. For the row $(j,i,c)$:
- the columns of $u_{j,l}$ get $N D_{il}\,\delta_{cc'} - T A_{cc'} V_{il}$ in component $c'$;
- the columns of $u_{j'_k,l}$ (state $\sigma_k$) get $-T J_{k,c}\,L_l(\zeta'_k)$, added to the entry above when $j'_k = j$;
- the column of $T$ gets $-f_c - \tfrac1T\sum_k \tau_k J_{k,c}\,u'_{k,\sigma_k}$, because $\partial s'_k/\partial T = \tau_k/T^2$;
- the column of $\alpha$ gets $-T P_c + \sum_{k:\,\text{the time of slot }k\text{ is }\mathtt{Param(free)}} J_{k,c}\,u'_{k,\sigma_k}$, because $\partial s'_k/\partial\tau_k = -1/T$. The explicit dependence of $f$ on $\alpha$ is in $P$, and the dependence through $y_k$ in the second term.
The periodicity and phase rows are linear in $u$ and have zero columns for $T$ and $\alpha$.

**The corrector.** `correct` of Section 5 solves $F(x) = 0$ with one more row, the border. For a fixed $\alpha$ the border is $e_{\dim}$ with the right side 0, so $\alpha$ keeps its value. The phase reference is the profile of the start, and the phase row is built from it once. The corrector uses `Options` `max_newton`, `tol_x` and `tol_f`. It reads no other key (in particular no `mesh_adapt`, which is for the continuation step). A failure to converge is `NewtonFailed { iters: max_newton, residual: NaN }`, a cancellation is `Cancelled`, a singular bordered matrix is `Singular`, and a non-finite model value is `NotFinite`.

**The API.** In `pc_engine::dde`, re-exported at the crate root with a `dde_` prefix:
- `DdeCycleSetup { params: Vec<f64>, free: usize, ntst: usize, ncol: usize }`. `params` are the declared parameters (length `n_user_params`, with the start value of $\alpha$ at `free`).
- `DdeCycleStart { profile: Vec<f64>, period: f64 }`, the profile in the layout of $u$ above ($n(Nm+1)$ values).
- `DdeHopfCycle { x: Vec<f64>, omega: f64, eps: f64 }`, the equilibrium, the critical frequency and the amplitude. `eps` has no default.
- `DdeCycle { profile: Vec<f64>, period: f64, alpha: f64, iters: usize, residual: f64 }` where `residual` is the largest absolute value of the $\dim-1$ rows at the result.
- `correct_cycle(model, setup, start, opts, cancel)` for a fixed $\alpha$ and `hopf_cycle(model, setup, hopf, opts, cancel)` for the Hopf start.
- `cycle_residual(model, setup, reference, x)` and `cycle_jacobian(model, setup, reference, x)`, the $\dim - 1$ rows and the $(\dim-1)\times\dim$ column-major Jacobian at the unknown vector `x`, with `reference` the profile of the phase condition. Tests 1 and 2 use them. They run the same checks as the two functions above, except that they do not read `start`.
Every check below is `BadProblem` and runs before any allocation that depends on the sizes. `check_dims` of the ODE cycles is not used, since the parameter count of a `DdeModel` is its declared one.
- `params`: length `n_user_params` (as `check_params` of Section 8d), every value finite, and every delay time given by a parameter finite and positive; `free < n_user_params`.
- `ntst >= 1`, `ncol` in 2 to 7, $n(Nm+1)+2 \le$ `MAX_DIM` (a product that overflows is the same error).
- `profile.len() == n(Nm+1)` and every value finite; `period` finite and positive.
- For `hopf_cycle`: `x.len() == n`, `x` finite, `omega` finite and positive, `eps` finite and positive (the message names `eps`).

**The Hopf start.** Let $\Delta(\lambda) = \lambda I - A - \sum_k J_k e^{-\lambda\tau_k}$ at the equilibrium `x` (`dde::linearize`) and let $q$ be the null vector of $\Delta(i\omega)$ with $\|q\|_2 = 1$ that the Hopf routines of Section 8g compute (`lyap::pair`). A `pair` that fails ($\Delta(i\omega)$ not singular to its tolerance) is `BadProblem`. The first guess is the linear mode,
$$u_q = x + \varepsilon\,\varphi(s_q),\quad \varphi(s) = \Re q\,\cos 2\pi s - \Im q\,\sin 2\pi s,\qquad T_0 = 2\pi/\omega,\qquad \alpha = \alpha_H\ (\texttt{params[free]}).$$
The corrector gets $\alpha$ free: the border is $v = (\varphi(s_0), \dots, \varphi(s_{Nm}), 0, 0)/\|\varphi\|$ with $\|\varphi\|$ the Euclidean norm over all base points, and the right side is $\varepsilon\|\varphi\|$. This is the hyperplane $\varphi\cdot(u - x) = \varepsilon\|\varphi\|^2$, which excludes the equilibrium. $\alpha$ is free because the equilibrium of many models persists in $\alpha$ and a fixed-$\alpha$ Newton iteration from $x + \varepsilon\varphi$ then has no periodic solution to go to. The phase reference is the guess. There are no step-size halvings in this step: a Newton failure is an error, and the caller lowers `eps`.

**Command line.** `pc-cli dde-cycle FILE` reads the problem by hand as `dde-hopf` does (Section 8f), takes keys `model`, `params` (by name), `free` (the name of one parameter), `ntst`, `ncol`, optional `options` (only `max_newton`, `tol_x` and `tol_f`; any other key is an error, because the corrector reads nothing else), and exactly one of `orbit` (an object with `profile`, a flat array in the layout above, and `period`) or `hopf` (an object with `x0`, `omega` and `eps`, all required). Unknown keys are errors. Exit 2 is a bad problem, as for the other `dde-*` commands, and 1 is a failed run (no convergence, a singular matrix). The output is one JSON object with `alpha`, `period`, `iters`, `residual`, `ntst`, `ncol` and `profile`.

**The oracle.** `references/dde-cycle-oracle.json`. The model is `par mu=0.3, tau=1.5707963267948966` with `x' = -delay(x,tau) + mu*x*(1 - x^2 - delay(x,tau)^2)`. For $\tau = \pi/2 + 2\pi K$ with $K = 0, 1, 2, \dots$ and every $\mu$, $x(t) = \sin t$ is a solution with $T = 2\pi$: $x(t-\tau) = -\cos t$ and the right side is $\cos t - \mu\,\sin t\,(1 - \sin^2 t - \cos^2 t) = \cos t$. The origin is an equilibrium with $\Delta(\lambda) = \lambda - \mu + e^{-\lambda\tau}$. A Hopf point has $\cos\omega\tau = \mu$ and $\omega = \sin\omega\tau$, so $\omega = \sqrt{1-\mu^2}$ and $\tau_H = \arccos\mu/\omega$. For $\mu = 0.3$ these are $\omega = 0.9539392014169457$ and $\tau_H = 1.3272372818926783$ ($|\Delta(i\omega)| = 6\cdot10^{-17}$). At $\tau = \pi/2$ the Hopf value of $\mu$ is $0$ with $\omega = 1$, where the system is a linear center, so the Hopf test frees $\tau$ with $\mu = 0.3$ fixed. The JSON has the Floquet multipliers of the exact orbit that later steps use.

**Acceptance tests.** Rust tests, in `crates/pc-engine/tests/dde_cycle.rs`, with the models above and these two:
- `OSC`, the oracle model with `K` chosen in the test (`tau` $= \pi/2 + 2\pi K$).
- `TWO`, `par a=0.9, tau1=1.1`, `x' = -a*x + 0.5*delay(y,tau1) - 0.3*x*y`, `y' = -y + 0.8*delay(x,0.7) + 0.2*delay(y,tau1)*delay(x,0.7)`. Two states, a parameter delay and a literal delay.

1. Jacobian against central differences (step $10^{-6}(1+|x_i|)$). `OSC` with $K = 0$, $N = 6$, $m = 3$ and with $K = 1$, $N = 5$, $m = 4$, once with `free = tau` and once with `free = mu`, and `TWO` with $N = 5$, $m = 3$ and `free = tau1` and again with `free = a`. The profile is $u_c(s) = 0.2c + 0.5\sin(2\pi s + 0.3c) + 0.1\cos(4\pi s)$ at the base points and $T = 5.3$ for `TWO` and $T = 6.1$ for `OSC` (neither is a solution). Before the comparison the test checks that every shifted point is at least $10^{-3}$ (in $s$) from a mesh node, and fails if one is not. Every entry satisfies $|J - J_{\rm fd}| \le 10^{-6}(1 + |J|)$.
2. The residual of the exact orbit. $\tau = \pi/2$ ($K = 0$) and $5\pi/2$ ($K = 1$), $\mu = 0.3$, the profile $\sin 2\pi s_q$ and $T = 2\pi$, for $N = 10, 20, 40$ and $m = 3, 4$. The largest absolute value of the rows is at most the number in the residual table, and doubling $N$ reduces it by at least a factor $2^{m-1}$ (checked for the pairs where both values are above $10^{-13}$).
3. Newton from a perturbed start. The profile $1.2\sin 2\pi s_q + 0.05\sin 4\pi s_q$, $T_0 = 6$, $\alpha$ fixed at $\tau$, `OSC` with $K = 0$ and $K = 1$ and the meshes of test 2. `correct_cycle` converges, and $|T - 2\pi|$ and $\max_q|u_q - \sin 2\pi s_q|$ are at most the numbers of the Newton table. The phase condition leaves a unique shift for this start (the shift $\delta$ solves $\sin 2\pi\delta = 0$), so the result is compared with $\sin 2\pi s$ with no shift. `alpha` equals the input and `residual` is at most $10^{-9}$.
4. The Hopf start. `OSC` with $K = 0$ and the value of `tau` set to $\tau_H$ ($\mu = 0.3$), `free = tau`, $N = 20$, $m = 4$, `x = [0]`, $\omega = 0.9539392014169457$ and $\varepsilon = 0.04, 0.02, 0.01$. `hopf_cycle` converges. Let $\Delta\alpha(\varepsilon) = \alpha - \tau_H$. Then $\Delta\alpha \ne 0$, $\Delta\alpha(\varepsilon/2)/\Delta\alpha(\varepsilon)$ is within $0.005$ of $1/4$ for both pairs, and $|T - 2\pi/\omega|$ is at most $2\cdot10^{-4}$ for $\varepsilon = 0.01$ and falls by a factor within $0.1$ of 4 from $\varepsilon = 0.02$ to $0.01$. (The measured values are in the Hopf table. A wrong tangent or a wrong $\alpha$ column breaks the first two at once.)
5. Errors. Each of these is `BadProblem` for `OSC`: `ntst` 0, `ncol` 1 and 8, `free` equal to the number of parameters, `params` of the wrong length, a NaN parameter, a delay time $0$ and $-1$ given by `tau`, a profile of the wrong length, a NaN in the profile, `period` 0, $-1$ and NaN, a problem with $\dim > 3000$ (for example `ntst` 1000, `ncol` 4), a product `ntst * ncol` that overflows, and for `hopf_cycle` an `x` of the wrong length, `omega` $\le 0$ or NaN, `eps` 0, $-0.1$, NaN and $\infty$ (the message names `eps`), and an `omega` for which $\Delta(i\omega)$ is not singular ($\omega = 2$ at $\mu = 0.3$, $\tau = \tau_H$). A set `cancel` flag gives `Cancelled`.
6. The two evaluators. Tests 1 (the values), 3 and 4 with `Evaluator::Jit` and `Evaluator::Fallback`. The Jacobians agree to $10^{-10}(1 + |J|)$, and the converged $T$, $\alpha$ and the profile agree to $10^{-10}$.
7. `pc-cli dde-cycle` with `OSC` ($K = 0$), `orbit` from test 3 at $N = 20$, $m = 4$ prints the same `period` and `profile` as `correct_cycle` (bit for bit, JIT), and `hopf` from test 4 prints the same `alpha` and `period` as `hopf_cycle`. The file with both `orbit` and `hopf`, with neither, with an unknown key, with a bad `eps`, and with a profile of the wrong length is exit 2. A run that does not converge (`max_newton` 1 in `options`) is exit 1.

The residual table, the Newton table and the Hopf table are measured with the code (test first, then recorded here). The bounds in the tests are 2.1 to 2.9 times the measured values, except the two $N = 40$, $m = 4$ bounds on $\lvert T - 2\pi\rvert$, which are $10^{-12}$ (the measured values are at the rounding level). They are measured from the code and are not derived from the order of the method. The measured values are the first run of `print_tables` (`cargo test -p pc-engine --test dde_cycle print_tables -- --ignored --nocapture`).

Residual of the exact orbit (test 2). The two values of $K$ agree to $10^{-13}$, since $\tau$ differs by $2\pi$ and the shifted points are the same modulo 1.

## 8m. Floquet multipliers of a delay cycle (phase E9b, step 2a)

Step 2a of E9b computes the leading Floquet multipliers of a periodic orbit found by Section 8l. It does not continue a branch of cycles and does not label LPC, PD and NS points. The continuation is step 2b (Section 8n) and the labels are step 3, which gets its own section. Section 8s names the slices of the MATLAB front end, and none covers this step yet.

**What is not verified.**
- The formulation below is my own derivation. I did not read how DDE-BIFTOOL or the papers cited in Section 8l compute multipliers, and I did not look for a paper on a pseudospectral monodromy operator (Section 8c cites Breda, Maset and Vermiglio 2005 for roots; I did not read it). Nothing here is cited from a source. I checked the result against a numerical oracle (below), not against a published formula.
- The oracle (`references/dde-cycle-oracle.json`, key `multipliers`) is numerical, about nine digits, from a solver of my own that uses a different method (RK4 with Hermite cubic history, on the exact orbit). It is not a closed form. The test with a block triangular model (test 6) has a closed form for one multiplier.
- The tolerance tables are measured from the code. They are not derived from the order of the method.
- The multipliers of small modulus are not meaningful: the monodromy operator of a delay equation is compact, so its spectrum accumulates at 0, and the discretized matrix has $n(M+1)$ eigenvalues of which only the leading ones approximate the operator. The caller says how many to keep with `count`, and there is no default (Section 8l's rule for opt-in values). A multiplier whose modulus is below about $10^{-10}$ is at the rounding level of the matrix.
- The stage limit below is my own choice for time and memory.

**Variational equation.** Let $u(t)$ be the orbit of period $T$ at the parameter $\alpha$. The variational equation is
$$v'(t) = A(t)\,v(t) + \sum_{k=1}^{S} J_k(t)\,e_{\sigma_k}^{\!\top} v(t - \tau_k),$$
with $A(t) = \partial f/\partial y_0$ and $J_k(t) = \partial f/\partial y_k$ at $(u(t), u(t-\tau_1)_{\sigma_1}, \dots)$, the same matrices as in the Jacobian of Section 8l. A solution that starts from the history $v_0 \in C([-\tau_{\max}, 0]; \mathbb R^n)$ at $t = 0$ has history $v_T$ at $t = T$. The map $v_0 \mapsto v_T$ is the monodromy operator, and its nonzero eigenvalues are the Floquet multipliers. Differentiating $u'(t) = f(u(t), u(t-\tau_1), \dots)$ shows that $v = u'$ solves the variational equation and is $T$-periodic, so 1 is always a multiplier (the trivial one). The multiplier closest to 1 therefore measures the discretization error, and the result reports it.

**History segment.** The history on $[-\tau_{\max}, 0]$, $\tau_{\max} = \max_k \tau_k$, is represented by its values at the $M+1$ Chebyshev extreme points $\theta_0 = 0 > \theta_1 > \dots > \theta_M = -\tau_{\max}$ (`dde::nodes::Nodes`, Section 8c), with $M$ = `nhist`. The state is $W(t) \in \mathbb R^{d}$, $d = n(M+1)$, with $W_{j,c} = v_c(t + \theta_j)$ at index $jn + c$. Differentiating the interpolant gives $W' = G(t) W$ with
- rows $(j, c)$, $j \ge 1$: $\sum_l D_{jl} W_{l,c}$, where $D_{jl} = \ell_l'(\theta_j)$ (the history moves along the segment);
- rows $(0, c)$: $\sum_{c'} A_{cc'}(t) W_{0,c'} + \sum_k J_{k,c}(t) \sum_l \ell_l(-\tau_k) W_{l,\sigma_k}$ (the equation itself, with the delayed value taken from the interpolant). The values $\ell_l(-\tau_k)$ are `Nodes::lagrange_at`, which returns a unit vector when $\tau_k$ is a node, so a delay equal to $\tau_{\max}$ is exact.

**Time integration.** $\Phi' = G(t)\Phi$, $\Phi(0) = I_d$, is integrated over $[0,T]$ interval by interval of the mesh of Section 8l, with the $m$-stage Gauss collocation (implicit Runge-Kutta) method on the interval. The stage times are the Gauss points $s_{ji}$ of the orbit mesh, where $A$ and $J_k$ are already evaluated, so the model is evaluated at the same points as the corrector. With $c_i$ the Gauss nodes in $(0,1)$, $b_i$ the weights, $a_{il} = \int_0^{c_i} \ell_l(z)\,dz$ (computed as $c_i \sum_q b_q \ell_l(c_i c_q)$, exact because $\ell_l$ has degree $m-1$) and $h = T/N$, one interval from $\Phi_a$ to $\Phi_b$ solves the stage equations
$$Y_i - h\sum_{l=1}^{m} a_{il}\,G_l\,Y_l = \Phi_a\ (i = 1, \dots, m),\qquad \Phi_b = \Phi_a + h\sum_i b_i\,G_i Y_i,$$
where $G_i = G(t_{ji})$ at the Gauss point $t_{ji} = s_{ji}T$ and each $Y_i$ is a $d\times d$ matrix. This is one dense system of order $md$ with $d$ right sides. $\Phi(T)$ is the product over the $N$ intervals. The multipliers are the eigenvalues of $\Phi(T)$ (`pc_linalg::eig::eigenvalues`).

**Result.** The multipliers are sorted by modulus, largest first, with ties in the order of `eigenvalues` (real part descending, then imaginary part descending, so a conjugate pair keeps its member with positive imaginary part first). The first `count` are returned. A cut can fall inside a conjugate pair, and then the result has one member of it. The caller who needs the pair asks for one more.

**The API.** In `pc_engine::dde`, re-exported at the crate root with a `dde_` prefix:
- `DdeFloquetOptions { nhist: usize, count: usize }`, no `Default`.
- `DdeFloquet { multipliers: Vec<(f64, f64)>, trivial_error: f64 }`: `(re, im)` pairs, `count` of them, and $\min |\mu - 1|$ over all $n(M+1)$ multipliers (not only the kept ones).
- `floquet_multipliers(model, setup, cycle, options, cancel)`, with the types of `correct_cycle` of Section 8l (`model: &mut DdeModel`, `setup: &DdeCycleSetup`, `cycle: &DdeCycleStart`, `cancel: &AtomicBool`) and `options: &DdeFloquetOptions`. The value of the parameter is `setup.params[setup.free]`. The profile need not be an exact solution, but the result is only meaningful for one.
Every check is `BadProblem` and runs before any allocation that depends on the sizes:
- the checks of Section 8l for `setup` and `cycle` (`profile` length and finiteness, `period` finite and positive);
- the model has at least one delay slot (a model without delay is an ODE cycle, Section 7);
- `nhist >= 1`;
- `1 <= count <= n (nhist + 1)`;
- the stage system $m\,n\,(M+1) \le$ `MAX_STAGE` $= 1000$ (a product that overflows is the same error).
A set `cancel` flag, checked once per mesh interval, gives `Cancelled`. A non-finite model value is `NotFinite`, and an eigenvalue iteration that fails is `Singular`.

**Command line.** `pc-cli dde-cycle` takes one more optional key, `floquet`: an object with `nhist` and `count`, both required, integers of at least 1. Without the key the output is unchanged. With it, the output has `multipliers` (an array of `[re, im]`) and `trivial_error`, for the orbit that the corrector returns, with `params[free]` set to the `alpha` of the result (for a `hopf` start the corrector moves $\alpha$). The options restriction of Section 8l is unchanged. `floquet` of any other form (`true`, `null`, an array, a missing member, an unknown member, a non-integer, 0, a negative number) exits 2 with a message that names `floquet`.

**Acceptance tests.** Rust tests, in `crates/pc-engine/tests/dde_floquet.rs`, with the models of Section 8l and these two:
- `FORCED`, `par mu=0.3, tau=1.5707963267948966`, the `OSC` equation, with a second state `y' = -0.1*y + 0.5*delay(x,tau)`. The first equation does not read $y$, so the variational matrix is block triangular and the multipliers of the orbit are those of `OSC` together with $e^{-0.1T}$ (for $T = 2\pi$, $0.5335\ldots$) and the multipliers of the $y$ history, which are zero in the operator.
- `OSCLIT`, the `OSC` equation with the literal `delay(x,1.5707963267948966)`.

1. Oracle. `OSC`, the orbit from `correct_cycle` as in test 3 of Section 8l (start $1.2\sin 2\pi s_q + 0.05\sin 4\pi s_q$, $T_0 = 6$) with the mesh and `nhist` of the table. For each row of the oracle (K = 0 at $\mu = 0.1, 0.2, 0.3, 0.5$ and K = 1 at $\mu = 0.1, 0.3, 0.5$), drop the multiplier closest to 1 and the members with negative imaginary part, sort the rest by modulus, and compare the first three with the oracle row: each differs by at most the bound of the table.
2. The trivial multiplier. In test 1 the field `trivial_error` is at most the bound of the table.
3. Convergence in `nhist` and in the mesh. For `OSC` at $K = 0$ and $K = 1$, $\mu = 0.3$, the error of the leading nontrivial multiplier against the oracle falls as `nhist` grows through the values of the table and as the mesh is refined through the meshes of the table, until it reaches the level that the error of the orbit sets (the $u$ error of Section 8l at that mesh).
4. `count`. The result for `count` $= c$ is the first $c$ entries of the result for `count` $= n(M+1)$, bit for bit, for $c = 1, 3, 10$.
5. The block triangular model. `FORCED` at $K = 0$, the orbit of `correct_cycle` from the start $(\sin 2\pi s_q, 0)$ with $T_0 = 2\pi$, $N = 20$, $m = 4$, `nhist` $= 16$. Of the multipliers, one is within $10^{-9}$ of $e^{-0.1T}$ for the computed $T$, and the three leading multipliers of the `OSC` block agree with the oracle within the bounds of the table.
6. A literal delay. `OSCLIT` and `OSC` give multipliers that agree within $10^{-10}$.
7. The two evaluators. Tests 1 (the first row of each $K$) and 5 with `Evaluator::Jit` and `Evaluator::Fallback`: the leading multipliers agree within $10^{-9}$.
8. Errors. Each of these is `BadProblem` for `OSC` at $N = 10$, $m = 3$: `nhist` 0, `count` 0 and $n(M+1) + 1$, a profile of the wrong length, a NaN in the profile, `period` 0, $-1$ and NaN, `ntst` 0, `ncol` 1 and 8, a stage system over the limit (`nhist` 400), and a model without a delay slot (a built model whose delay list the test empties, since the builder of Section 8d refuses a model without a delay call). A set `cancel` flag gives `Cancelled`.
9. `pc-cli dde-cycle` with the problem of test 3 of Section 8l (`OSC`, $K = 0$, `orbit`, $N = 20$, $m = 4$) and `"floquet": {"nhist": 16, "count": 4}` prints `multipliers` with 4 entries equal, bit for bit, to those of `floquet_multipliers` on the corrected orbit, and a numeric `trivial_error`. The same file without the key prints neither. Each malformed `floquet` of the list above exits 2 and the message has `floquet`. `--help` lists the key.

**Measured tables.** The tables are measured with the code (tests first, then recorded here, release build). The bounds in the tests are not derived from the order of the method. Except for `TRIVIAL_BOUND` they are a factor of 2 to 3 above the largest measured value.

Test 1 and 2, `OSC` at $N = 40$, $m = 4$, with `nhist` $= 16$ for $K = 0$ and `nhist` $= 24$ for $K = 1$. The columns are the error of the first, second and third leading nontrivial multiplier against the oracle, and `trivial_error`.

| $K$ | $\mu$ | 1st | 2nd | 3rd | `trivial_error` |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | 0.1 | 3.4e-12 | 2.9e-10 | 4.1e-8 | 1.2e-13 |
| 0 | 0.2 | 2.4e-11 | 2.6e-10 | 4.1e-8 | 1.1e-13 |
| 0 | 0.3 | 4.8e-11 | 2.8e-10 | 3.9e-8 | 1.0e-13 |
| 0 | 0.5 | 1.0e-11 | 5.7e-10 | 5.4e-8 | 9.1e-14 |
| 1 | 0.1 | 6.3e-10 | 1.5e-10 | 8.6e-8 | 5.1e-13 |
| 1 | 0.3 | 8.5e-8 | 9.9e-9 | 4.3e-6 | 3.3e-13 |
| 1 | 0.5 | 7.8e-7 | 7.6e-7 | 4.8e-5 | 2.4e-13 |

The bounds in `ORACLE_BOUND` are 1.5e-10, 1.5e-9 and 1.5e-7 for $K = 0$ and 2e-6, 2e-6 and 1.2e-4 for $K = 1$. The bound for the first multiplier at $K = 0$ is close to the resolution of the oracle (about ten decimals), so a tighter bound would test the oracle. `TRIVIAL_BOUND` is $10^{-11}$ for both $K$, about 20 times the largest measured value, because `trivial_error` is at the level of the rounding error of $\Phi(T)$ and a factor of 2 to 3 would make the test depend on the platform's floating-point library.

Test 3, $\mu = 0.3$, error of the leading nontrivial multiplier. Left, by `nhist` at $N = 40$, $m = 4$. Right, by mesh at `nhist` $= 16$ ($K = 0$) and `nhist` $= 24$ ($K = 1$).

| $K$ | `nhist` | error | | $K$ | $(N, m)$ | error |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | 4 | 1.4e-3 | | 0 | (10, 3) | 1.1e-4 |
| 0 | 6 | 6.4e-6 | | 0 | (20, 3) | 3.8e-8 |
| 0 | 8 | 2.6e-7 | | 0 | (20, 4) | 2.3e-12 |
| 0 | 12 | 2.3e-11 | | 0 | (40, 4) | 4.8e-11 |
| 1 | 8 | 1.9e-2 | | 1 | (10, 3) | 5.4e-4 |
| 1 | 12 | 5.3e-4 | | 1 | (20, 3) | 3.7e-7 |
| 1 | 16 | 4.4e-5 | | 1 | (20, 4) | 8.5e-8 |
| 1 | 24 | 8.5e-8 | | 1 | (40, 4) | 8.5e-8 |

In each sequence a step at least halves the error unless the error before the step is at most `ERROR_FLOOR` $= 2\cdot10^{-7}$, and the test requires nothing of the steps after that. For $K = 0$ the step from $(20, 4)$ to $(40, 4)$ goes up from 2.3e-12 to 4.8e-11, which is the orbit's own error and rounding at that level. For $K = 1$ the error stops at 8.5e-8 from $(20, 4)$ on, the level that the history discretization sets at `nhist` $= 24$.

Test 5, `FORCED` at $K = 0$: the multiplier $e^{-0.1T}$ is reproduced to $10^{-15}$ (the bound is $10^{-9}$), and the three leading multipliers of the `OSC` block have the errors of the $K = 0$, $\mu = 0.3$ row above.

## 8n. Continuation of periodic solutions of a delay equation (phase E9b, step 2b)

Step 2b of E9b continues the periodic orbit of Section 8l in one parameter by pseudo-arclength continuation (Section 5) and reports each point as a profile, a period and a parameter value. It does not compute the Floquet multipliers of the points and does not label LPC, PD and NS points. Step 3, which is Section 8o, adds both. Section 8s names the slices of the MATLAB front end, and none covers this step yet.

**What is not verified.**
- The formulation below is my own. It runs the pseudo-arclength continuation of Section 5 (`run_branch`) on the rows of Section 8l, and it moves the phase reference to each accepted profile as the cycle branches of Section 7 do. Nothing is cited from a source. I did not read how DDE-BIFTOOL continues periodic orbits, only the part of its manual that Section 8l cites.
- The mesh is fixed. The shifted points of Section 8l depend on the mesh, and I did not write the move of a profile onto a new mesh for a delay problem, so `mesh_adapt` is ignored. The code does not estimate the discretization error along the branch. An orbit that grows a steep part is resolved only as well as the start mesh allows, and there is no warning. Section 8p adds an opt-in move of the mesh through a separate entry point.
- `detect_lp`, `detect_bp` and `detect_h` are ignored. The point tests of an equilibrium do not apply to the unknowns of a cycle, and no equilibrium-type special point is reported. The labels of cycles are Section 8o. A branch point of the discretized problem (a second multiplier at $+1$) is not detected unless the opt-in `bpc` of Section 8q is on, and then only where an odd number of branches cross. The run may follow either branch there. Switching to the other branch at a `BPC` is Section 8r.
- The branch is a branch of the discretized problem, not a proof that the delay equation has these orbits.
- The claim that the Hopf branch of the oracle model at $\mu = 0.3$ reaches the exact orbit at $\tau = \pi/2$ comes from a scratch calculation of my own, not kept in the repository, with a different discretization. It found $\alpha = 1.570796328$ and $T = 6.28318531$ at amplitude $1.0000000022$. Test 2 checks the same path with the code of this section, to the bounds measured below. It is a numerical oracle, not a closed form. The $\mu$-line of test 1 is exact up to the discretization.
- The dense Jacobian of Section 8l costs $O(\dim^3)$ per Newton step. A run with $\dim$ near the limit of 3000 and hundreds of points is slow, and I have not timed it.
- The tolerance tables below are measured from the code. They are not derived from the order of the method.

**Formulation.** The unknowns, the $\dim - 1$ rows and the Jacobian are those of Section 8l, with $\alpha$ the continuation parameter. `run_branch` adds the arclength row to the corrector, with the step $h$ in the Euclidean norm of the whole unknown vector (the profile, $T$ and $\alpha$ count alike), and uses the bordered solve of `Problem`. These parts are specific to a delay cycle:
- *The phase reference.* The phase row of Section 8l is built from a reference profile and normalized by $\|\pi\|_2$. At every accepted point the reference becomes the profile of that point and the row is built again (`Problem::accept`). Inside one corrector call it does not change. As in `run_branch` for the cycles of Section 7, the tangent of a new point is computed with the reference of the corrector that produced the point, which is the previous point's profile.
- *The start.* `start` is corrected by Newton's method at the fixed $\alpha$ of `setup.params[free]` first, as `correct_cycle` does, with the phase reference at the profile of `start`. The corrected point is the first point of the branch. The first tangent comes from the bordered solve, and `options.direction` sets the sign of its $\alpha$ component.
- *The parameter.* $\alpha$ is the last component. `options.bounds` apply to it, and a run that reaches a bound stops there with `StopReason::Bounds`, the point located to `tol_refine`. If the parameter is a delay time (`DelayTime::Param(free)` for some slot), `bounds.0` must be above 0, as in Section 8e.
- *A bad trial point.* A trial point with $T \le 0$, a non-finite $\alpha$ or a delay time that is not positive and finite is `NotFinite` of Section 8l, which the corrector reports as a failure, so the step is halved. No such point is ever reported.
- *Ignored options.* `mesh_adapt`, `detect_lp`, `detect_bp` and `detect_h` (above). `h0`, `hmin`, `hmax`, `max_steps`, `max_newton`, `tol_x`, `tol_f`, `direction`, `bounds`, `max_locate` and `tol_refine` work as in Section 5. A run with `max_steps` $= k$ that is not stopped earlier has $k + 1$ points.
- *Cancellation.* A `cancel` flag that is set when the function is called gives `Err(Cancelled)` before any other work. A flag set during the run stops it with `StopReason::Cancelled` and the points found so far.

**The API.** In `pc_engine::dde`, re-exported at the crate root with a `dde_` prefix:
- `continue_cycle(model, setup, start, opts, cancel) -> Result<DdeCycleBranch, EngineError>`, with `DdeCycleSetup` and `DdeCycleStart` of Section 8l.
- `DdeCycleBranch { points: Vec<DdeCyclePoint>, stop: StopReason, warnings: Vec<String> }`.
- `DdeCyclePoint { profile: Vec<f64>, period: f64, alpha: f64, h: f64, newton_iters: usize }`, the profile in the layout of Section 8l, `h` the step that reached the point (0 for the first) and `newton_iters` the corrector iterations at the point (0 for the first).
The checks are those of Section 8l (`BadProblem`, before any allocation that depends on the sizes), then the delay-time rule above, then the step control of `run_branch`: `h0`, `hmin`, `hmax` finite, `h0 > 0`, `hmin > 0`, `hmax >= hmin` and `max_newton >= 1`, otherwise `BadProblem("bad step control options")`. The step control is checked before the start is corrected. A start that does not converge gives the errors of `correct_cycle`.

**Command line.** `pc-cli dde-cycle FILE` gets the key `branch`, a boolean that is off when it is absent or `false`. With `branch: true` the command continues the orbit of `orbit` or `hopf`. The `hopf` start is first run through `hopf_cycle` and the result is the start of the continuation, so the first point of the branch is the Hopf cycle at `eps`. `options` then goes through the reader of the `cont` problem files, as for `dde-branch`, except that `detect` and `mesh_adapt` are refused, since they do nothing here. Without `branch`, `options` keeps the restriction of Section 8l. `branch` together with `floquet` was a bad problem (exit 2) in this step. Section 8o allows it. The output is one JSON object with `ntst`, `ncol`, `points` (objects with `alpha`, `period`, `profile`, `h` and `iters`), `stop` (the names of `cont`, as for `dde-branch`) and `warnings`. A start that does not converge is exit 1, as in Section 8l. A run that stops later with `error` still prints its points, writes the message to stderr and exits 0, as `dde-branch` does.

**Acceptance tests.** Rust tests in `crates/pc-engine/tests/dde_cycle_branch.rs` with `OSC` of Section 8l, $N = 20$, $m = 4$ unless a test says otherwise, and a start corrected at the first point.
1. The $\mu$-line, an exact family. $K = 0$ and $K = 1$ ($\tau = \pi/2 + 2\pi K$), `free = mu`, start $\mu = 0.3$ with the profile $\sin 2\pi s_q$ and $T = 2\pi$, `bounds` $= (0.1, 0.9)$, `hmax` $= 0.2$, `max_steps` $= 200$, once with `direction` $+1$ and once with $-1$. The exact orbit $x = \sin t$, $T = 2\pi$ exists for every $\mu$, and $\mu = 0$ is a degenerate point (Section 8l) that the bounds avoid. Each run stops with `Bounds`, the last $\alpha$ is the bound that the direction points to within $10^{-9}$, and at every point $\lvert T - 2\pi\rvert$ and $\max_q\lvert u_q - \sin 2\pi s_q\rvert$ are at most the numbers of table 1.
2. The Hopf branch to the exact orbit. $\mu = 0.3$, `free = tau`, `hopf_cycle` with $\varepsilon = 0.05$ and `tau` set to $\tau_H$ (as test 4 of Section 8l), then `continue_cycle` from that cycle with `params[free]` set to the $\alpha$ of that cycle and `direction` $+1$, `bounds` $= (1, \pi/2)$, `hmax` $= 0.5$, `max_steps` $= 500$. The run stops with `Bounds`. At the last point $\alpha = \pi/2$ within $10^{-9}$, and $\lvert T - 2\pi\rvert$, $\lvert\max u - 1\rvert$, $\lvert\min u + 1\rvert$ and the difference of $\int_0^1 u^2\,ds$ from $0.5$ are at most the numbers of table 2 ($\max$ and $\min$ over the base points, the integral by the trapezoid rule on the base points of one period). The amplitude, $\max u - \min u$, increases from point to point along the branch.
3. The same path backwards. `OSC` with $K = 0$ and $\mu = 0.3$, `free = tau`, the exact start at $\tau = \pi/2$, `direction` $-1$, and `bounds.0` the value of $\alpha$ of the $\varepsilon = 0.05$ cycle of test 2 (computed by `hopf_cycle` in the test), `bounds.1` $= 2$. The run stops with `Bounds` at that value. The period and the amplitude, $(\max u - \min u)/2$, of the last point differ from those of the $\varepsilon = 0.05$ cycle by at most the numbers of table 3, and differ from $6.584130506595359$ and $0.0500015$ (values from my scratch calculation of that cycle) by at most the numbers there.
4. Every point is a solution. For every point of tests 1 to 3, `cycle_residual` with the profile of the point as the reference is at most $10^{-9}$, and `correct_cycle` from the point at its own $\alpha$ returns the same period to $10^{-9}$ in at most 3 iterations.
5. Direction and step control. For the $\mu$-line of test 1 ($K = 0$), `direction` $+1$ gives $\alpha_1 > \alpha_0$ and $-1$ gives $\alpha_1 < \alpha_0$. `max_steps` $= 3$ gives 4 points and `MaxSteps`. A start outside `bounds` gives one point and `Bounds`.
6. Errors. Each of the following is `BadProblem` for `OSC`: the errors of test 5 of Section 8l (`ntst` 0, `ncol` 1 and 8, the wrong `params`, the wrong profile, the bad period, $\dim > 3000$), a delay-time `free` with `bounds.0` $= 0$, $-1$ and NaN (the message names `bounds`), and bad step control (`h0` $= 0$, `hmin` $>$ `hmax`, `hmax` $= \infty$, NaN, `max_newton` $= 0$). A set `cancel` flag at the call gives `Cancelled`. A start for which the corrector does not converge (`max_newton` $= 1$ with a perturbed profile) is `NewtonFailed`.
7. The two evaluators. The first 6 steps of test 1 ($K = 0$, `direction` $+1$) and of test 2 with `Evaluator::Jit` and `Evaluator::Fallback` give the same number of points, and `alpha`, `period` and `profile` agree to $10^{-10}$ (relative to $1 + \lvert\cdot\rvert$).
8. `pc-cli dde-cycle` with `OSC` ($K = 0$), `orbit` and `branch: true` with the options of test 1 prints the same points as `continue_cycle` (the `alpha`, `period` and `profile` of every point bit for bit, JIT) and `stop` $=$ `"Bounds"`. The same with `hopf` and the options of test 2. `branch: false` and no `branch` print the output of Section 8l. `branch: true` with `options.mesh_adapt`, with `options.detect`, and with a delay-time `free` and `bounds.0` $= 0$ is exit 2. (Section 8o lifts the refusal of `branch` with `floquet`.)

**Measured tables.** Debug build, JIT, `print_tables` in the test file. The bounds are about 2 to 3 times the measured values, except the ones marked below.

Table 1 ($N = 20$, $m = 4$, `hmax` $= 0.2$; the maximum over every point of the run). The runs with $\mu$ going up have 13 points and the runs going down have 9, for both $K$.

| $K$ | direction | $\max\lvert T - 2\pi\rvert$ | $\max_q\lvert u_q - \sin 2\pi s_q\rvert$ |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | +1 | $1.9\cdot10^{-11}$ | $5.30\cdot10^{-8}$ |
| 0 | -1 | $2.2\cdot10^{-11}$ | $5.29\cdot10^{-8}$ |
| 1 | +1 | $1.1\cdot10^{-11}$ | $5.30\cdot10^{-8}$ |
| 1 | -1 | $1.7\cdot10^{-11}$ | $5.29\cdot10^{-8}$ |

Bounds of test 1: $5\cdot10^{-11}$ for $T$ and $1.4\cdot10^{-7}$ for the profile. The errors are the discretization error of the fixed mesh, which does not depend on $\mu$ to the digits shown.

Table 2 (25 points, last point at $\alpha = 1.5707963267946774$, started from the $\varepsilon = 0.05$ cycle at $\alpha = 1.3276067784641747$).

| $\lvert T - 2\pi\rvert$ | $\lvert\max u - 1\rvert$ | $\lvert\min u + 1\rvert$ | $\lvert\int_0^1 u^2 - 0.5\rvert$ |
|---|---|---|---|
| $1.9\cdot10^{-11}$ | $3.2\cdot10^{-13}$ | $3.2\cdot10^{-13}$ | $2.2\cdot10^{-9}$ |

Bounds of test 2: $5\cdot10^{-11}$, $10^{-12}$, $10^{-12}$ and $6\cdot10^{-9}$. The two extreme values are at the level of rounding and are the most likely to change between platforms, so their bound is about 3 times the measured value and I expect to widen it if a CI platform needs it.

Table 3 (25 points, last point at the $\alpha$ of the $\varepsilon = 0.05$ cycle, period $6.584130506595406$, half amplitude $0.0500015404004258$).

| $\lvert T - T_{\text{hopf}}\rvert$ | $\lvert a - a_{\text{hopf}}\rvert$ | $\lvert T - 6.584130506595359\rvert$ | $\lvert a - 0.0500015\rvert$ |
|---|---|---|---|
| $4.7\cdot10^{-14}$ | $5.5\cdot10^{-13}$ | $4.7\cdot10^{-14}$ | $4.0\cdot10^{-8}$ |

Bounds of test 3: $10^{-12}$ and $3\cdot10^{-12}$ for the first two, which are at rounding level (about 20 and 5 times the measured values), $10^{-12}$ for the period against the scratch value, and $10^{-7}$ for the amplitude, since the scratch amplitude has 6 digits. The scratch period agrees with `hopf_cycle` to $4.7\cdot10^{-14}$, so the scratch value does not give a second, independent check of the period at the $10^{-12}$ level.

## 8o. Labels of cycles of a delay equation: LPC, PD and NS (phase E9b, step 3)

Step 3 of E9b puts the Floquet multipliers of Section 8m on the branch of Section 8n and reports the points where the branch has a limit point of cycles (LPC), a period-doubling (PD) or a Neimark-Sacker point (NS). Section 8s names the slices of the MATLAB front end, and none covers this step yet.

**What is not verified.**
